# Residence Life Operations Knowledge Base

## Duty & On-Call
### Collaborating with Public Safety
### Collaborating with Public Safety

This section outlines expectations and roles when Residence Life staff and Public Safety work together during an incident.

#### When Public Safety Calls You
- Respond immediately to the specified location.
- Bring a way to take notes.

#### When You Call Public Safety
- Manage the incident until Public Safety officers arrive.
- When officers arrive:
  - Explain the incident clearly, including the exact location.
  - Briefly connect with the officers to provide an update.
  - Once briefed, officers take control of the incident.
  - RAs remain actively engaged and continue to assist as needed.

**Sources:**
- `Collaborating with Public Safety.pdf`

---

ROLE OF THE RA WHEN COLLABORATING WITH PUBLIC SAFETY

When working with Public Safety during any incident response, RAs must:

- **Share Information**
  - Provide officers with all the information you have so they can accurately write their reports and appropriately address the incident.

- **Address Incorrect Information**
  - If officers share or repeat information that is incorrect, kindly and respectfully correct it in the moment.

- **Call the CD/GSA on Duty**
  - Follow the “Who To Call” document to determine when calling the CD/GSA on Duty is required.
  - You may also call the CD/GSA on Duty if you have questions, need clarity on procedure, or if something about the situation feels off.

- **Write a Report**
  - Submit an incident report in Advocate every time, regardless of what Public Safety says.
  - You must document in Advocate whether or not Public Safety is writing their own report.

**Sources:**
- `Collaborating with Public Safety.pdf`

### Community Presence & Rounds
**Community Presence Guidelines**

Before commencing each set of rounds, every Resident Assistant (RA) on duty is required to spend at least ten minutes in a common area to establish a community presence. Suitable locations for this presence include lobbies, high traffic areas, or other designated community spaces. During this time, RAs should engage with residents and be prepared to document these interactions. A specific section in the duty log is designated for recording details of these engagements, so RAs should ensure they have writing materials ready to capture this information accurately.

**Sources:**
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`

---

### Community Walks / Rounds Procedures
Community Walks (Rounds)

Rounds are scheduled to occur at 8pm, 10pm, and 12am (midnight) daily, with an additional round at 2am on weekends (Friday and Saturday nights). During these rounds, the following procedures must be followed:

- Check every entrance to ensure it is secure.
- Verify the presence of all fire safety items, such as fire extinguishers and EXIT signs.
- Stay vigilant and observant, as familiarity with the environment can lead to overlooking unusual occurrences.
- Conduct a thorough walk-through of the entire area or building.
- Take note of anything that appears out of place, suspicious, or concerning.
- Address any policy violations encountered.
- Maintain punctuality and adhere to the scheduled times.
- Uphold integrity throughout the process. 

These guidelines are essential to ensure safety and compliance within the residence community.

**Sources:**
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`

### Duty Overview
Duty is a health and safety process conducted at a consistent time each night, providing residents with reliable access to a Resident Assistant (RA) and various campus resources. This process involves a series of rounds designed to ensure the health and safety of the building. During these rounds, any concerns are documented to facilitate an appropriate response. This structured approach ensures that residents have dependable support and that any issues are promptly addressed. RJ1

**Sources:**
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`

---

### Duty Phone Procedures
**DUTY PHONE PROCEDURES**

Each community is equipped with a mobile phone to facilitate duty and communication. Below are the guidelines for holding and using the duty phone:

**Holding the Phone:**
- Ensure you pick up the phone by 4:30 PM. Punctuality is crucial.
  - Upon picking up the phone, check in by texting the Community Director (CD) or Graduate Student Assistant (GSA) on duty.
  - If you are late, the CD/GSA will follow up with you and inform your supervisor.
- Keep the phone with you throughout your shift. Return it the next day when your shift ends at 8:00 AM, but no later than 10:00 AM.

**Using the Phone:**
- Answer every call with: “RA on Duty, this is [your name].” This ensures the caller knows who they are speaking to.
- If you miss a call, return the call as soon as possible.
- Do not install or use any additional apps on the phone unless you have received approval from your CD/GSA.
- At the end of each shift, plug the phone in to charge.

These procedures ensure effective communication and responsibility while on duty.

**Sources:**
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`

### Duty Preparation
**Duty Preparation Guide**

**Attire:** Wear your SWAGA St. Thomas shirt, preferably your Res Life t-shirt, to ensure you are easily identifiable.

**Phone:** Each community is equipped with a duty phone stored in the hall. Ensure you have access to it during your shift.

**Meal Planning:** Duty extends through dinner time, so plan your meals in advance. Use your dining dollars or opt for a to-go meal to ensure you are prepared.

**Mindset:** Approach your duty with readiness and maintain a positive presence in the hall. Be prepared to handle any situation that may arise.

**Technology:** Ensure you have access to Roompact for managing the duty log and roster. This is essential for keeping track of your responsibilities and any incidents that occur.

**Sources:**
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`

### Duty Schedule & Coverage
Residence Life staff are on duty every day, including weekends, holidays, and J-Term. Duty hours are from 4:30 PM to 8:00 AM on both weeknights and weekend nights. During this time, desk hours are maintained from 4:30 PM to 8:00 PM. In certain communities, duty rounds may involve two Resident Assistants (RAs). This schedule is consistent throughout the year, including the summer months.

### General Duty Overview
When considering the concept of duty, it is important to reflect on the responsibilities and obligations associated with it. Duty often involves a commitment to perform tasks or uphold certain standards within a given role or position. It requires a sense of accountability and dedication to fulfilling one's responsibilities effectively and ethically. Understanding duty involves recognizing the expectations and requirements that come with a specific role, whether in a professional, academic, or personal context. It is essential to approach duty with a mindset focused on reliability, integrity, and the willingness to meet obligations consistently.

### Overview / Definition of Duty
**What is Duty?**

While on duty, Resident Assistants (RAs) serve as a vital resource for students who may reach out for various reasons, including needing someone to talk to, checking out desk items such as vacuums, cleaning supplies, and pots and pans, or accessing campus resources. RAs are also available to discuss concerns, including conflicts and incidents.

Duty is an essential time for community building due to the RAs' required presence in the building. During their shifts, on-duty RAs will engage in several activities to foster a supportive environment. These activities include speaking with students to develop personal relationships, greeting students as they enter buildings or spaces, and stopping by rooms with open doors to greet residents and check in. Additionally, RAs will walk through the buildings to assess community activities and respond accordingly.

**Sources:**
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`

### RA Duty Expectations
Resident Advisors (RAs) must diligently document incidents in their duty logs, which should be submitted after completing the final set of community walks. Being on duty is an excellent opportunity to engage with residents. Use community walks to familiarize yourself with the residents in your building or area. Maintain an approachable demeanor by keeping your door open when possible. If the door needs to be closed, display a sign indicating "On Duty, Please Knock" or specify your location within the building.

RAs must remain within the building while on duty. You may have friends visit, but inform them that you might need to respond to incidents or attend to residents, which could require them to leave immediately. Always be prepared to respond to calls throughout the night. Ensure the duty cell phone is set to a volume that will wake you if needed.

**Sources:**
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`

### RA Duty Phone Protocol
**Resident Advisor Duty Cell Phones**

Resident Advisors (RAs) are required to use their building or area’s duty cell phone exclusively for duty-related communications. These phones facilitate communication with Public Safety, the CD/GSA on duty, and other RAs. Personal use, entertainment, and unauthorized app downloads are prohibited. Data usage is restricted to emergencies when Wi-Fi is unavailable. RAs must handle these phones responsibly and report any damages or malfunctions to their CD/GSA, who will inform the Associate Director.

**Duty Protocol:**

1. **Phone Pickup and Check-In:**
   - RAs must collect the duty cell phone from the designated location by 4:30 p.m.
   - A check-in text to the CD/GSA duty phone with the RA's name is required by 4:30 p.m. Check-ins can occur no earlier than 10 minutes before the duty shift starts (i.e., 4:20 p.m. or 7:50 p.m.).
   - If an RA checks in early, the CD/GSA will request a re-check at the appropriate time.

2. **During Duty:**
   - The duty phone must be kept on the RA’s person at all times.
   - Calls should be answered promptly, beginning with: “Community Name RA on duty, this is NAME.” Missed calls require immediate voicemail review and callback.
   - The phone’s ringer must remain on high; silent or vibrate modes are not permitted.
   - Charge the phone as needed during possession.

3. **Phone Return:**
   - Return the phone to the designated location by 10:00 a.m. the following morning and ensure it is plugged in for charging.

4. **In Case of Phone Malfunction:**
   - Notify Public Safety, CD/GSA on duty, and Desk staff with alternative contact information.

5. **Duty Coverage:**
   - If an RA has a class extending beyond the duty start time, another RA must cover until the class ends. Both the initial and subsequent RA must check in via text.

**Check-In Follow-Up:**

- If an RA fails to check in within 5 minutes, the CD/GSA will text the duty phone.
- No response within 10 minutes prompts the CD/GSA to call the duty phone, then the RA’s personal or room phone, and if necessary, physically check the hall.
- Delays beyond the 5-minute grace period will be reported to the RA's supervisor.

**Resident Interaction:**

- Duty phone numbers and operating hours are posted in each community.
- Residents may call for lockouts, noise issues, or other concerns. For emergencies, residents should be directed to call the PS Emergency Line, and emergency protocols should be initiated.
- Prank calls should be logged, and persistent issues reported to Public Safety.

**Public Safety Protocol:**

- **4:30 p.m. – 8:00 a.m.:** Call the RA duty phone first; if unanswered, contact the CD/GSA on duty.
- **8:00 a.m. – 4:30 p.m.:** Directly contact the CD/GSA on duty.

**Contact Information:**

- **Public Safety:**
  - Emergency: 651-962-5555 (2-5555 from campus)
  - Non-Emergency: 651-962-5100 (2-5100 from campus)
- **CD/GSA Duty Phone:** 612-801-8484 (For Residence Life team use only)
- **RA Duty Cell Phone Numbers:**
  - Brady: 651-675-8603
  - Dowling: 651-675-8487
  - Flynn: 651-675-8583
  - Frey: 763-406-1343

  - Morrison: 651-675-8587
  - Murray: 651-675-8544
  - Tommie North: 763-406-1403

RAs must ensure they are physically present in their hall with the duty phone by the start of their shift.

**Sources:**
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`

### RA Duty Rounds & Community Walks
Resident Advisor Duty Building/Community Walks

Resident Advisors (RAs) are required to conduct Building/Community Walks several times throughout the evening to ensure the safety and security of all residents. These walks should be staggered and typically occur around 8:00 pm, 10:00 pm, 12:00 am, and 2:00 am, with the 2:00 am walk only taking place on weekends. 

At the beginning of each walk, RAs should spend the first 15 minutes in a common area, such as a front desk, lobby, or student meeting place, to be visibly present and engage with residents. The walks are not meant to be quick; they should be conducted with intention and integrity, allowing RAs to build relationships with students while monitoring the community's safety and security.

During these walks, RAs should check for the following:

1. **Noise and Policy Violations**: Address any noisy areas or concerning activities by knocking on doors or approaching involved individuals. Document noise complaints and other policy violations in the duty log using Advocate. Call Public Safety for assistance if needed.

2. **Exterior Entrances**: Ensure all entrances are locked and secure. If any doors are unsecured, contact Public Safety, who may also involve Facilities for assistance.

3. **Unescorted Guests**: If guests are violating policies, follow response procedures. If they are only unescorted, politely ask them to leave. Call Public Safety for assistance if necessary.

4. **Common Areas**: Check that bathrooms, lounges, study rooms, elevators, stairs, and lobbies are free of messes, vandalism, and damage, and that all furniture is present. Take photos, document via Advocate, email photos to your CD/GSA, and submit an RFS if needed.

5. **Kitchens**: Ensure ovens and stovetops are off if no resident is present. Turn them off if necessary and call Public Safety for any fire concerns.

6. **Laundry Machines**: Verify that laundry machines are functioning properly. Submit a work order via the CSC app if needed.

7. **Fire Extinguishers**: Confirm all fire extinguishers are present. Call Public Safety and document via Advocate if there are issues.

8. **Damage and Vandalism**: Ensure all spaces, including resident doors, are free of damage and vandalism. Take photos, call Public Safety, document via Advocate, and email photos to your CD/GSA.

Final community walks should be completed by 12:00 am from Sunday to Thursday, and by 2:00 am on Fridays and Saturdays. These walks are also an excellent opportunity to visit with residents, especially those not living on your floor, to foster community relationships.

Documentation of community walks and any incidents should be recorded in the duty log form in Roompact. If any significant events occur while on duty, such as a fire alarm, ensure they are documented appropriately.

**Sources:**
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`

### RA Duty Schedule & Rotation
**Resident Advisor Duty Rotation and Schedule**

The Resident Advisor (RA) duty schedule is designed to ensure consistent coverage across all communities and may be adjusted based on specific community needs. 

**Duty Schedule:**
- **Regular Periods:** RAs are on duty from 4:30 pm to 8:00 am.
- **Break Periods:** Duty hours shift to 8:00 pm to 8:00 am.
- The start of break periods is determined by Residence Life, and your Community Director (CD) or Graduate Staff Assistant (GSA) will inform you when the duty start time changes to 8:00 pm. Unless otherwise notified, assume a 4:30 pm start.
- Duty is scheduled in rotation with your immediate team, coordinated by your CD/GSA.
- Every building or area must have at least one RA on duty each evening.

**Beginning of the Year:**
- First-Year (FY) communities require two RAs on duty on Fridays and Saturdays.
- During the initial weeks of the school year, increased duty coverage may be necessary across all communities.
- CDs/GSAs will determine the appropriate level of RA presence needed.

**Scheduling:**
- The CD/GSA is responsible for coordinating and updating the duty schedule in Roompact.
- For detailed procedures, consult your CD/GSA.

**Duty Schedule Changes:**
- Any changes to duty assignments must be updated in Roompact and approved by the CD/GSA.

**Responsibilities When Beginning Duty:**
- **Pick Up Duty Phone:** Refer to the Resident Advisor Duty Phones section for more details.
- **Check-In:** Text your CD/GSA on-duty from your community’s duty phone. This should be done no more than 10 minutes before the start of your shift at either 4:30 pm or 8:00 pm.
- **Name Card Placement:** Where applicable, place your name card in designated areas within the building to inform students of the contact person for assistance.

**Sources:**
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`

### Responding to Medical Incidents During Duty
After completing your 10 p.m. rounds and returning to your room to study, you receive a call from Public Safety about a student who has hit their head on their bedframe. Public Safety informs you that they are already present with the student. To handle this situation, follow these steps:

1. Immediately proceed to the student's room to assess the situation firsthand.
2. Upon arrival, introduce yourself to the student and any Public Safety officers present.
3. Evaluate the student's condition, checking for any visible injuries and asking about symptoms such as dizziness, nausea, or confusion.
4. If the student requires medical attention, coordinate with Public Safety to contact emergency medical services.
5. Provide comfort and support to the student, ensuring they remain calm while waiting for further assistance.
6. Document the incident thoroughly, noting the time, location, individuals involved, and any actions taken.
7. Follow up with the student after the incident to ensure their well-being and offer additional resources if needed.

**Sources:**
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`

### Typical Public Safety & Residence Life Calls
Typical Public Safety and Residence Life Calls

Public Safety and Residence Life are typically involved in the following types of situations in and around the residence halls:

- Anything occurring in a residence hall, for the most part, falls under typical Public Safety and Residence Life response.
- Liquor law violations – always require a report.
- Drug law violations – always require a report.
- Weapon law violations – always require a report.
- Noise complaints.
- Damage to property.
- Welfare checks.

Notification to Residence Life:

- Any time Public Safety responds to a residence hall, Residence Life will likely be notified.
- Exceptions: Residence Life is generally not notified when Public Safety is responding solely for:
  - An access request.
  - A fire alarm.
  - A mechanical malfunction.

## Emergencies
### Active Shooter Response
### Active Shooter Response: Run. Hide. Fight. / ADD

Our active shooter response training is based on the nationally endorsed **Run. Hide. Fight.** model, which is also supported by the FBI. We also use the acronym **ADD** to reinforce the same concepts:

- **Avoid = Run**  
- **Deny = Hide**  
- **Defend = Fight**

**Run / Avoid**  
- Focus first on getting away from the threat if it is safe to do so.

**Hide / Deny**  
- If you cannot safely run, hide in a way that **denies** the shooter access to you.  
- Research has shown that in active shooter emergencies, the majority of those injured or killed are people who were hiding.  
- Common failures include:
  - Forgetting to **barricade** themselves.
  - Hiding in a way that does **not** allow an effective transition to **fight/defend** if necessary.

**Fight / Defend**  
- If you cannot run or effectively deny access, be prepared to **defend** yourself as a last resort.

A critical part of **Run. Hide. Fight.** is **preparation and planning** through a **personal safety plan**. Staff and residents should think in advance about how they would avoid, deny, and defend in different locations so they can act quickly and effectively in an emergency.

**Sources:**
- `2025 Res Life Presentation.pdf`

### Contacting Public Safety / Emergency & Non-Emergency Lines
Contacting Public Safety

All on-campus calls to Public Safety must be directed through the Public Safety dispatch center.

Non-Emergency Line  
- Phone (off campus): 651-962-5100  
- From an on-campus phone: dial 2-5100  

Use the Public Safety Non-Emergency Line for the following situations:
- You need access to your dorm, office, or other campus areas.
- Your vehicle will not start and you need a jump start.
- You have locked your keys in your car.
- You would like a vehicle or walking escort.
- You have a question for Public Safety.

Emergency Line  
- Phone (off campus): 651-962-5555  
- From an on-campus phone: dial 2-5555  

Use the Public Safety Emergency Line for the following situations:
- You witnessed a crime.
- You are a victim of a crime.
- You are reporting suspicious activity.
- Someone needs emergency help.
- If you think it is an emergency, call the emergency line.

Additional Option  
- You may also contact Public Safety using the Safe Zone App.

**Sources:**
- `2025 Res Life Presentation.pdf`

### Counseling & Psychological Services (CAPS) & Mental Health Support
Counseling & Psychological Services: Offerings and Support

Counseling & Psychological Services provides a range of clinical and consultative services to support students’ mental health and well-being. Available services include:

- **Group Counseling and Workshops**  
  Structured group counseling sessions and psychoeducational workshops are offered to address common concerns, build skills, and provide peer support.

- **Individual Counseling**  
  One-on-one counseling is available for students seeking personalized support for mental health, emotional, or personal concerns.

- **Substance Use Resources**  
  Resources and support are provided for students with questions or concerns related to substance use, including education, assessment, and referral as appropriate.

- **Consultation and Outreach**  
  Staff are available to consult with students, staff, and faculty about mental health concerns and to provide outreach programming to the campus community.

- **Crisis Drop-In Hours**  
  Crisis drop-in services are available Monday through Friday from 2:00 p.m. to 4:00 p.m. for students experiencing urgent psychological concerns.

- **24/7 Crisis Phone Counseling**  
  Crisis phone counseling is available at all times, 24 hours a day, 7 days a week, for immediate support during mental health emergencies or acute distress.

- **Staff Liaisons to Key Campus Areas**  
  Counseling & Psychological Services maintains designated staff liaisons to the following areas to support communication, collaboration, and tailored services:
  - Athletics  
  - Veterans  
  - LGBTQIA  
  - Office of International Students & Scholars (OISS)  
  - Student Diversity and Inclusion Services (SDIS)  
  - School of Nursing  
  - Residence Life

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

---

### Counseling and Psychological Services (CAPS)
Counseling and Psychological Services (CAPS)

All enrolled undergraduate and graduate students are eligible to receive CAPS services. Counseling services are included in the Health Fee, so there is no additional charge for counseling sessions.

CAPS provides short-term, goal-oriented therapy with no formal session limits. Students receive individualized treatment recommendations, which may include off-campus referrals when necessary.

CAPS treats a wide range of emotional and psychological concerns, including but not limited to:
- Depression  
- Anxiety  
- Sexual assault  
- Eating disorders  
- Identity development  
- Family issues  
- Other emotional or mental health concerns  

In addition to counseling, CAPS offers testing for ADHD and other learning disabilities. There is a charge for this testing, which is billed to insurance.

Location: CAPS is located at the corner of Finn and Grand, approximately 200 steps from the arches.

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

### Emergency Calling Protocols
**STUDENT LEADER EMERGENCY CALLING PROTOCOL**

This protocol outlines the appropriate contacts for various emergency situations. In all cases, if you are uncertain whether to call, it is better to over-report than under-report. If a situation arises that is not listed, or if you need assistance, contact the CD/GSA on duty. Additionally, if a student is sent to the care room following Public Safety's assessment, inform the CD/GSA on duty.

**Contact Information:**
- **CD/GSA on Duty:** 612-801-8484
- **Public Safety:** 
  - Non-emergency: 651-962-5100
  - Emergency: 651-962-5555

**Incident Response Guidelines:**

- **Accidents not requiring hospital transport:** Call Public Safety.
- **Acts of Intolerance/Bias/Harassment/Hate Crime:** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety.
- **Alcohol or Drug Violation:** Call Public Safety.
- **Death of a Student:** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety.
- **Fireworks or other Fire Hazards:** Call Public Safety.
- **Flood in Hall:** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety.
- **Noise Violation:** No calls necessary.
- **Physical Assault:** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety.
- **Pulled Fire Alarm:** Call Public Safety.
- **Real Fire Emergency:** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety.
- **Room/Building Search (community safety risk):** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety (only Public Safety can conduct searches).
- **Room/Building Search (alcohol/drugs or guests):** Call Public Safety (only Public Safety can conduct searches).
- **Serious Mental Health Concerns:** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety (CD/GSA will likely call).
- **Serious Vandalism (extensive damage, broken windows):** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety.
- **Sexual Misconduct:** Call CD/GSA (they will contact Public Safety if requested).
- **Student Arrest:** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety.
- **Student Emergency Transport to Hospital:** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety.
- **Student Requiring Attention in CARE Room:** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety (although they’re likely already there).
- **Emergency Personnel in Community (e.g., Police, EMTs, Fire):** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety (although they’re likely already there).
- **Suicide Attempt/Gesture:** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety.
- **Theft:** Call Public Safety.
- **Unconscious Student:** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety.
- **Unresponsive Intoxicated/Impaired Student:** Call Public Safety.
- **Unusual Facilities Incidents:** Call both CD/GSA and Public Safety.
- **Visitation Violation:** No calls necessary.
- **Weapons:** Call Public Safety.

**Sources:**
- `C. Student Leader Emergency Calling Protocol-1.pdf`
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`
- `C. Student Leader Emergency Calling Protocol.pdf`

### Fire Alarm Response
Fire Alarm Response Procedures

In the event of a fire alarm in your building, follow these steps:

1. **Evacuate the Building Immediately**  
   - If you hear a fire alarm, you must exit the building.  
   - Direct and instruct all residents to evacuate, even if you believe the alarm may be a false alarm (for example, if you smell overcooked popcorn).

2. **If You See a Fire**  
   - If you see an actual fire, contact Public Safety immediately at **651-962-5555**.

**Sources:**
- `2025 Res Life Presentation.pdf`

### Mental Health & Suicide Risk
### Behavioral Clues of Possible Suicidal Risk

Be alert to the following behavioral clues, especially when they occur together or represent a change from a person’s usual behavior:

- Any previous suicide attempt.  
- Acquiring a gun or stockpiling pills.  
- Co-occurring depression, moodiness, or expressions of hopelessness.  
- Putting personal affairs in order (e.g., organizing finances, making final arrangements).  
- Giving away prized possessions.  
- A sudden interest in religion or a sudden loss of interest in religion.  
- Drug or alcohol abuse, including relapse after a period of recovery.  
- Unexplained anger, aggression, or irritability.

**Sources:**
- `UPDATED-QPR-GATEKEEPER August 2025 RA.pdf`

---

### Mental Health & Suicide Risk Response
**Referral Options for Supporting a Person at Risk**

When responding to someone who may be at risk (including risk of suicide), use the strongest possible referral option available in the situation. The options, in order of preference, are:

1. **Best Referral (Highest Priority)**  
   - Personally take the person directly to someone who can help (for example, a counselor, medical professional, or other appropriate support resource).

2. **Second-Best Referral**  
   - Obtain a clear commitment from the person that they will accept help.  
   - After securing this commitment, make the arrangements to connect them with that help (such as scheduling an appointment or contacting the resource on their behalf).

3. **Third-Best Referral**  
   - Provide the person with appropriate referral information (such as phone numbers, locations, or contact details for support services).  
   - At the same time, try to obtain a good faith commitment from them that they will not attempt or die by suicide.

**Sources:**
- `UPDATED-QPR-GATEKEEPER August 2025 RA.pdf`

### Mental Health & Wellness Concerns
### Responding to Supervisor Outreach About a Resident’s Mental Well-Being Concern

**Scenario:**  
Your supervisor emails you to inform you that they have been trying to get in touch with one of your residents. The outreach is in response to a concern raised by the resident’s professor, who contacted the Dean of Student’s Office about the resident’s mental well-being. Your supervisor has already attempted to knock on the resident’s door and call them, but the resident has not responded. Your supervisor asks you to connect with the resident to help get them connected to resources and to encourage them to respond to outreach attempts.

This section outlines how you might begin, what type of touch point to use, what that touch point might look like, and what follow-up steps to consider.

---

### 1. Where to Start

1. Review the information provided by your supervisor:
   - Note that the concern originated from a professor.
   - Understand that the concern has already reached the Dean of Student’s Office.
   - Acknowledge that your supervisor has already tried door knocks and phone calls without success.

2. Consider the resident’s known patterns and context:
   - Any prior interactions you have had with the resident.
   - Their typical responsiveness to email, text, or in-person contact.
   - Any relevant information about their schedule or habits that might affect how and when you reach out.

3. Plan an initial outreach that:
   - Aims to establish contact in a supportive, non-threatening way.
   - Clearly communicates care and availability.
   - Encourages the resident to respond and engage with available resources.

---

### 2. Choosing Between a Formal or Informal Touch Point

When deciding whether a formal or informal touch point is more applicable, consider:

- The seriousness of the concern (mental well-being concern escalated to the Dean of Student’s Office).
- The fact that your supervisor’s attempts (door knocks and calls) have not received a response.
- Your existing relationship with the resident (if any).

**Informal touch point:**  
- May be more appropriate as an initial step if you have some rapport with the resident or if you believe a lower-pressure approach will increase the likelihood of a response.  
- Can feel more personal and less intimidating, which may encourage the resident to open up.

**Formal touch point:**  
- May be appropriate if informal attempts are unsuccessful, if you have no prior relationship with the resident, or if your supervisor or institutional practice indicates a need for more structured documentation and follow-up.  
- Can help clearly outline concern, expectations, and available resources in a documented way.

You may choose to start informally and then move to a more formal approach if the resident does not respond.

---

### 3. What the Touch Point Might Look Like

Depending on whether you choose an informal or formal approach, the touch point could include:

**Informal touch point examples:**
- A casual, supportive email or message:
  - Expressing that you’ve noticed outreach attempts from staff.
  - Letting the resident know you are available to talk or meet.
  - Emphasizing care, support, and confidentiality within your role’s limits.
- A friendly in-person attempt:
  - Knocking on their door at a time they are likely to be home.
  - Introducing the conversation in a non-pressuring way if they answer.
  - Offering to talk in a comfortable, private space.

**Formal touch point examples:**
- A more structured email or written message:
  - Identifying yourself and your role.
  - Noting that campus staff (including your supervisor and the Dean of Student’s Office) have been trying to reach them due to concern for their well-being.
  - Clearly inviting them to schedule a time to meet.
  - Listing specific campus resources (e.g., counseling services, Dean of Student’s Office, other support services) and how to access them.
- A scheduled, documented meeting:
  - If the resident responds, arranging a set time and place to talk.
  - Using the meeting to listen, share resources, and encourage connection with appropriate offices.

In all cases, the tone should be:
- Nonjudgmental and empathetic.
- Focused on support and options, not demands.
- Clear that the goal is to connect them with help, not to punish or discipline.

---

### 4. Follow-Up Steps to Consider

After the initial touch point, consider the following follow-up actions:

1. **If the resident responds and engages:**
   - Meet with them (if appropriate) to:
     - Listen to their concerns.
     - Provide information about relevant resources (e.g., counseling, Dean of Student’s Office, academic support).
     - Encourage them to follow up with the offices that have reached out (e.g., Dean of Student’s Office).
   - Check in after the meeting:
     - A brief follow-up message or conversation to see how they are doing.
     - Ask if they were able to connect with the suggested resources.
   - Communicate with your supervisor as required by your role:
     - Share that contact was made.
     - Note any next steps you and the resident agreed upon, within privacy and reporting guidelines.

2. **If the resident does not respond:**
   - Try an alternate method of contact:
     - If you started informally (e.g., a casual message), consider a more formal email or written note.
     - Attempt another in-person check at a different time of day.
   - Document your attempts as required:
     - Note dates, times, and methods of outreach.
   - Update your supervisor:
     - Inform them of all attempts and lack of response.
     - Ask if there are additional steps they would like you to take or if the situation should be escalated further.

3. **Ongoing considerations:**
   - Continue to be observant:
     - If you see the resident around the building, consider a brief, supportive check-in.
   - Maintain appropriate boundaries:
     - Offer support and connection to resources without taking on the role of counselor or therapist.
   - Follow any additional instructions from your supervisor or institutional protocols related to mental well-being concerns and non-responsiveness.

---

This process is intended to guide you in responding when your supervisor asks you to help connect a resident to resources and encourage them to respond to outreach attempts, particularly when there is a concern for their mental well-being that has already reached the Dean of Student’s Office.

**Sources:**
- `Creating Connections & Touch Points 25-26.pptx`

### Personal Safety / Active Threat Response
### Personal Safety Plan

A personal safety plan is an opportunity to prepare in advance using scenario-based response planning. The goal is to build “muscle memory” so that, in an actual emergency, you can draw on practiced responses and your prior experiences as tools to help you survive. You will never be able to imagine exactly how a real incident will look, but planning ahead and practicing scenarios improves your ability to respond effectively.

A key framework for personal safety planning is **Run. Hide. Fight.** This is not a checklist to be followed in order; all three are options, and you choose based on what is safest and most realistic in the moment. A personal safety plan should include:

- Planning ahead.
- Scenario-based exercises.
- Breaking down and practicing each element of Run. Hide. Fight.

#### Run
- Run far and run long.
- Do not wait to start moving to safety.
- When you encounter law enforcement, keep your hands up.

#### Hide
- Lock and/or barricade doors.
- Treat property as non-essential; it is not an issue if you need to damage it to stay safe (for example, break a window).
- Turn lights out.
- Keep your phone on silent.

#### Fight
- Commit fully: this is an all-in response.
- Get to the assailant’s level (engage directly if you must).
- Do not hesitate.

**Sources:**
- `2025 Res Life Presentation.pdf`

### RA Response Procedures
**RA Response Procedures**

**Evacuation Protocols:**
- In the event of an emergency, such as a fire, leave the building immediately and adhere to instructions from Public Safety, the Fire Department, or other officials.

**Bias Incidents:**
- For detailed information on bias incidents and related policies, refer to the Bias Reporting website: [Bias Reporting](https://www.stthomas.edu/deanofstudents/bias-reporting/).
- If you witness a potential bias incident, assume it is a bias incident and contact Public Safety immediately.
- After notifying Public Safety, inform the CD/GSA on duty, who will then contact the Associate Director of Residence Life.
- Preserve any physical evidence of the bias incident until Public Safety arrives; do not disturb or erase it.
- Regardless of the perceived severity or offensiveness of the act, if it relates to identity, report it to Public Safety without delay.
- Document the situation thoroughly in an incident report.

**Severe Weather Procedures:**

*Tornados and Severe Thunderstorms:*
- Public Safety will alert the CD/GSA on duty during a severe weather watch or warning.
- The CD/GSA on duty must update the Res Life Staff GroupMe and inform all RAs on duty via the duty phones.
- Ensure all team members are kept informed of any updates from Public Safety.
- Upon hearing a civil defense siren, clear all floors and move to the lower level or designated safe areas in each residence hall, regardless of the current weather appearance.
- If possible, monitor WCCO 830 AM radio, local television stations, or a reliable weather app for updates on severe weather conditions.
- Evacuate all floors to safeguard students, as high winds can shatter glass and pose a danger.
- Remain in safe areas until the siren ceases. Be aware that the siren may sound before the tornado is visibly close; always follow evacuation procedures promptly.

**Sources:**
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`

---

**RA Response Procedures**

**Death of a Student**  
- Immediately contact Public Safety followed by the CD/GSA on duty.  
- Do not notify the student’s parents/guardians or release any information about the situation or the deceased to students, friends, family, media, or any St. Thomas affiliated media.  
- Prioritize self-care and utilize all available campus resources during and after such incidents.  

**Sexual Misconduct**  
- Refer to the Title IX policy and consult the Title IX website for resources.  
- Inform the reporting party that you are required to report the incident to a CD/GSA, but assure them that the information will remain within the mandatory reporting structure.  
- Immediately contact the CD/GSA on duty, who will provide resources and work with the student to address their immediate needs.  
- Submit the Title IX Reporting Form with assistance from the CD/GSA on duty.  
- Do not document the situation in Advocate or Roompact.  
- You may be asked by your CD/GSA to provide additional support.  

**Mental Health Concerns (Including Suicidal Ideation)**  
- If a student indicates mental health concerns, contact the CD/GSA on duty.  
- If immediate medical attention is required, notify Public Safety first, then contact the CD/GSA on duty.  
- Document the situation in Advocate.  

**Missing Student**  
- Contact Public Safety immediately.  
- Do not release information or notify the missing student’s parents or guardians.  
- Document the situation in Advocate.  

**Bomb Threats**  
- If notified directly of a bomb threat (via phone call, email, etc.), contact Public Safety immediately.  

**Fire Preparation and Prevention**  
- Discuss fire safety and prevention at the initial community meeting.  
- Student leaders should confront irresponsible behavior and monitor floor decorations throughout the year.  
- Refer to the Residence Life Handbook for current information on prohibited items.  

**Fire Evacuation**  
- Follow the established fire evacuation procedures as outlined in the Residence Life Handbook.  

This comprehensive guide ensures that all RAs are equipped to handle various emergency situations effectively while maintaining confidentiality and compliance with university policies.

**Sources:**
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`

### Suicide Prevention & Mental Health Emergencies
**Recognizing Suicide Warning Signs**

When assessing potential suicide risk, be aware of four main categories of warning signs. Each category can provide important information and should be taken seriously.

1. **Direct Verbal Clues**  
   These are clear, explicit statements about wanting to die or intending self-harm. Examples include a person directly saying they want to kill themselves or expressing a desire to end their life.

2. **Indirect Verbal Clues**  
   These are less explicit statements that still suggest hopelessness, despair, or thoughts about death. A person may talk about having no reason to live, feeling like a burden, or wishing they could disappear, without directly stating suicidal intent.

3. **Behavioral Clues**  
   These involve actions or changes in behavior that may indicate increased risk. Examples include withdrawing from friends or activities, giving away possessions, sudden changes in mood or behavior, or engaging in risky or self-destructive activities.

4. **Situational Clues**  
   These are circumstances or recent events that may increase vulnerability to suicide. Examples include significant losses (such as relationships, jobs, or housing), major life changes, legal or financial problems, or other stressful or traumatic events.

All four types of clues—direct verbal, indirect verbal, behavioral, and situational—should be considered together when evaluating concern for a resident’s safety.

**Sources:**
- `UPDATED-QPR-GATEKEEPER August 2025 RA.pdf`

---

### Suicide Risk & Prevention
### Situational Clues for Suicide Risk

The following situational factors may indicate elevated suicide risk. Any one of these alone can be significant; multiple factors occurring together may further increase concern:

- Previous suicide attempt  
- Being fired from a job or being expelled from school  
- A recent unwanted move (e.g., forced relocation or move the person did not choose)  
- Loss of any major relationship  
- Death of a spouse, child, or best friend — especially if the death was by suicide  
- Diagnosis of a serious or terminal illness  
- Sudden, unexpected loss of freedom or fear of punishment  
- Anticipated loss of financial security  
- Loss of a cherished therapist, counselor, or teacher  
- Fear of becoming a burden to others

**Sources:**
- `UPDATED-QPR-GATEKEEPER August 2025 RA.pdf`

### Welfare Checks / Welfare Check Response Procedures
Welfare Check Response Procedures

Public Safety typically receives welfare check requests first. Whenever Public Safety officers respond to a residence hall for a welfare check, they will notify Residence Life.

Upon arrival at the residence hall room, officers will knock on the door. If there is no answer, officers will key into the room.

If officers enter and no one is present, they will conduct a plain-view search to look for any signs that the student has been harmed or may have self-harmed. If no one is located in the room and no immediate concerns are identified, officers will provide Residence Life with the Public Safety report number and clear from the call with the Residence Life staff member. Public Safety officers will continue attempts to locate and track down the student until the student is found. Officers will follow up with a phone call to the Hall Director (HD) to provide an update.

If the student is in the room when officers enter, officers will identify the problem and work to find an appropriate solution. Depending on the situation, this may include:
- Allowing the student to remain in their room if officers feel comfortable with that option, or  
- Contacting CAPS, or  
- Contacting SPFD.

For welfare checks, Public Safety will not wait for Residence Life staff to arrive before entering the room. However, officers will remain afterward to brief Residence Life staff about the situation.

**Sources:**
- `2025 Res Life Presentation.pdf`

## Facilities
### Flooding & Water Leaks
While on rounds, you notice that the door to a bathroom is open and water is flowing into the hallway. You enter the bathroom and see approximately one inch of water covering the entire floor. There is no running water from any fixtures in the bathroom, but you see a ceiling tile on the floor.

The following steps outline the full interaction from beginning to end, identify policy issues, note risk/harm factors, and clarify who may need to be contacted.

1. **Initial Observation and Safety Check**
   - Observe that the bathroom door is open and water is flowing into the hallway.
   - Enter the bathroom to assess the situation.
   - Note that about an inch of water covers the entire bathroom floor.
   - Confirm that there is no running water from sinks, toilets, showers, or other fixtures.
   - Observe that a ceiling tile has fallen and is on the floor.
   - Evaluate immediate safety concerns, including:
     - Slipping hazards in the hallway and bathroom due to standing water.
     - Possible electrical hazards if water is near outlets, lights, or other electrical fixtures.
     - Potential structural concerns related to the fallen ceiling tile.

2. **Immediate Response Actions**
   - Prevent students from walking through the flooded area by:
     - Closing off or restricting access to the bathroom.
     - Warning students in the vicinity about the water and the slipping hazard.
   - If safe to do so, place temporary signage or barriers to indicate that the bathroom is out of service and the area is wet.
   - Avoid touching any electrical fixtures, switches, or outlets that may be affected by the water.

3. **Notification and Communication**
   - Determine whether you need to call anyone based on the conditions observed:
     - Contact the appropriate on-call professional staff member (e.g., Residence Life professional on call) to report:
       - The open bathroom door.
       - Approximately one inch of standing water on the floor.
       - Water flowing into the hallway.
       - The absence of visible running water from bathroom fixtures.
       - The ceiling tile that has fallen to the floor.
     - Contact Facilities or Maintenance to:
       - Address the source of the water (likely from above, given the fallen ceiling tile).
       - Stop any ongoing leak that may not be immediately visible.
       - Begin cleanup and repair of the flooded area and ceiling.
   - Follow any building-specific or department-specific protocols for reporting flooding, water damage, or ceiling damage.
   - Document the incident according to internal reporting procedures, including:
     - Time and location of the incident.
     - Observations (water depth, spread into hallway, ceiling tile on floor, no running fixtures).
     - Actions taken (area secured, staff notified, calls made).

4. **Policy Issues to Address**
   - Consider whether any policy issues may be involved, including:
     - Possible damage to university property (flooding, ceiling damage, and any related structural or cosmetic damage).
     - Any resident behavior that may have contributed, if later discovered (e.g., tampering with fixtures or ceiling tiles), even though no such behavior is evident at the time of discovery.
   - Ensure that all relevant policies regarding:
     - Facility damage.
     - Safety hazards.
     - Reporting of maintenance emergencies.
     are followed and documented.
   - If subsequent information indicates student misconduct (e.g., intentional damage to the ceiling or plumbing), follow established conduct reporting procedures.

5. **Risk and Harm Factors for Students**
   - Identify factors that may contribute to risk or harm to students:
     - Slipping and falling due to water on the bathroom floor and in the hallway.
     - Potential exposure to contaminated water, depending on the source of the leak.
     - Risk of mold or mildew if the area is not dried and repaired promptly.
     - Possible electrical hazards if water has reached or may reach electrical systems, fixtures, or wiring above the ceiling.
     - Limited access to bathroom facilities if this is a primary or heavily used bathroom.
   - Monitor the area until Facilities or Maintenance arrives, as appropriate, to reduce risk to students.

6. **Follow-Up**
   - Confirm that Facilities or Maintenance has responded and that cleanup and repairs are underway.
   - Ensure that the bathroom remains closed to student use until it is deemed safe and operational.
   - Provide any requested information to professional staff or Facilities for incident follow-up.
   - If necessary, communicate with residents (per established procedures) about:
     - Temporary bathroom closures.
     - Safety reminders regarding wet floors and restricted areas.

This scenario is an example of a common incident that must be addressed during rounds: water intrusion, flooding, and ceiling damage. Staff must walk through the full interaction from discovery to resolution, identify and mitigate safety risks to students, address any related policy issues, and contact the appropriate personnel to ensure a timely and safe response.

**Sources:**
- `Enforcing Policies and Following Procedures.pdf`

### Maintenance Requests Procedure
**Maintenance Requests Procedure**

Residents are encouraged to submit their own maintenance requests. Resident Assistants (RAs) are permitted to submit requests for their own rooms or community spaces. Note that this process is not applicable for issues related to laundry machines or Information Technology Services (ITS).

**Process:**

1. The resident or RA submits a maintenance request through the Housing System. Detailed instructions can be found at: [Maintenance Requests Guide](https://www.stthomas.edu/_media-library/_documents/residence-life/how-to-submit-a-maintenance-request.pdf).
   
2. Alexis, the Operations and Finance Manager, receives the request and coordinates with Facilities Management to address the issue in the appropriate area.

3. Facilities Management visits the designated space to resolve the issue. It is not necessary for students to be present during this visit.

4. Once the issue is resolved, Alexis receives a notification, closes the request, and an automated email is sent to the student or RA confirming the resolution.

### Recycling & Waste Management
### Organics Recycling in Residence Halls

Every residence room is equipped with an organics recycling container. This container is for items that are accepted for organics recycling, including:

- All food scraps  
- Napkins  
- Products with the BPI certified logo  

For a complete list of accepted items, refer to the organics recycling information at:  
https://one.stthomas.edu/sites/facilities-management/recycling/SitePageModern/183249/organics-recycling

#### How to Use Your Organics Container

1. **Bag requirement**  
   - Collect organics in a **green BPI certified bag**.  
   - These bags are provided in the waste rooms on each floor.

2. **When the container is full**  
   - Tie the green BPI certified bag closed.  
   - Bring the tied bag to your floor’s waste room.  
   - Drop the bag into the large organics recycling bin located there.  

3. **Replace the bag**  
   - After depositing your full bag, take another green BPI certified bag from the waste room.  
   - Place the new bag in your room’s organics recycling container for continued use.

**Sources:**
- `Resource_ Sustainability_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### Assessment & Surveys
### Tommie Check-In Survey

The Tommie Check-In Survey is sent to all first-semester Tommies, including first-year students, DFC alumni, and transfer students. It is designed to help the university understand the first-semester student experience and identify areas for improvement, as well as students who may need additional support.

Students can also use the survey to give shout-outs. The survey is administered once per term, typically around weeks 6–8.

**Sources:**
- `2025StudentLeaderTraining - CSA.pdf`

---

### Background & History
A BRIEF HISTORY: DEVELOPMENT OF THE PRINCIPLES

The Principles emerged from highly collaborative efforts over many years. The following timeline outlines the key steps in their creation, implementation, and development:

- 2005  
  - A conversation with the Congregation for Catholic Education during the ACCU Rome Seminar sparks the idea for the Principles initiative.

- 2006  
  - Sponsorship for the project is secured from the leadership of ACCU, ASACCU, and JASPA.  
  - November 2006: Writers meet for a weekend retreat at Wye River, MD, to develop the first draft.

- 2007  
  - The draft document is shared for feedback at national meetings of ACCU, ASACCU, and JASPA.  
  - December 2007: The final document is sent to presidents and senior student affairs officers of all Catholic colleges and universities in North America.

- 2009  
  - The Principles assessment project is authorized by ACCU, ASACCU, and JASPA.  
  - November 2009: A Principles assessment retreat is held at Wye River.

- 2010  
  - Work focuses on vetting the diagnostic queries and preparing the second edition.  
  - December 2010: The second edition is sent to presidents and senior student affairs officers of all Catholic colleges and universities in North America.

- 2012  
  - Publication of Building the Capacity for Mission through Use of the Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs at Catholic Colleges and Universities, Catholic Education.

- 2016  
  - Publication of Student Life in Catholic Higher Education: Advancing Good Practice.

**Sources:**
- `ASACCU Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs.pdf`

### Belonging & Inclusion / Residential Programming Overview
The Belonging & Inclusion Residential Connection Team organizes a variety of events to foster community and engagement among residents. These events include Community Wide Events, which are designed to bring together all residents for shared experiences and activities. Additionally, Individual Resident Assistant (RA) Events are tailored by each RA to address the specific interests and needs of their assigned residents. During the Critical First Six Weeks, a crucial period for new residents to acclimate, Paired RA Events are held, where two RAs collaborate to host activities that encourage interaction and connection among their residents. These initiatives collectively aim to create a supportive and inclusive residential environment.

**Sources:**
- `RL-Residential Connection Events1 (1).pdf`

### Campus & Community Resources
Campus Ministry: Partnering with Residence Life

Campus Ministry at the University of St. Thomas exists to help students encounter God, live with meaning and purpose, and embrace their faith more intentionally. As a Catholic university, St. Thomas is committed to supporting the spiritual life and flourishing of all students. Campus Ministry serves as a companion on each student’s journey as they explore deep questions of meaning and purpose, and it provides connections to a vibrant faith community through a passionate staff team and student ministry leaders.

Residence Life staff play an important role in connecting residents with Campus Ministry. Your support helps students discover opportunities for spiritual growth, community, and service. Encourage your residents to explore Campus Ministry resources and initiatives so they can:

- Tackle big questions of purpose and meaning  
- Take part in weekday and Sunday liturgies  
- Participate in regular Campus Ministry programming  
- Share their gifts and passions by becoming part of the Campus Ministry team or volunteering in different initiatives  
- Connect with spiritual leaders and staff who can support their faith journey  

Campus Ministry offers students connections and opportunities for encounters so they can live with meaning and purpose and embrace their faith more intentionally. The mission is to invite and encourage all students to encounter God’s presence in the world and to provide a connection to a vibrant faith community.

Ways to Connect and Refer Residents:
- Website: Direct residents to explore Campus Ministry online at https://www.stthomas.edu/ministry/ to discover current initiatives, programs, and resources.  
- In-Person: Encourage residents to visit the Iversen Center for Faith to learn more and engage in person. Information about the Iversen Center for Faith is available at https://www.stthomas.edu/about/our-campuses/st-paul/iversen-center-for-faith/index.html.  
- Social Media: Suggest that residents follow Campus Ministry on Instagram at @ust_campusministry (https://www.instagram.com/ust_campusministry) for updates, events, and reflections.  
- Tommie Link: Point residents to Campus Ministry’s page on Tommie Link at https://tommielink.stthomas.edu/organization/campusministry to find events and opportunities to get involved.  

We are grateful for the connection you can support between Campus Ministry and your residents. Your encouragement helps students access the resources, accompaniment, and community they need to grow spiritually and to live out their faith with intention.

**Sources:**
- `Resource_ Campus Ministry_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

Student Sustainability Leadership Program (SSLP)

Overview  
The Student Sustainability Leadership Program (SSLP) is a team of student leaders who increase student sustainability knowledge and action through peer education. Student Sustainability Leaders bring hands-on education to the campus community through programs such as educational activities and events, Trash Talking, Tommies Closet, and more.

Campus Ministry & Faith-Based Engagement Overview

Campus Ministry at St. Thomas offers students many ways to connect with the campus faith community, deepen their relationship with God, and build intentional community with peers. The information below outlines key annual events, ongoing opportunities, and how Residence Life staff can help connect students.

---

### Annual Block Party (First Thursday of Classes)

- The annual Block Party is traditionally celebrated on the **first Thursday of classes**.
- All students are welcome to attend.
- The Block Party is a primary opportunity to:
  - Meet the **Campus Ministry team**.
  - Connect with **faith organizations on campus**.
  - Experience St. Thomas’ **vibrant faith community**.
- For students who are **not familiar with Campus Ministry** or other faith-related opportunities on campus, the Block Party serves as a **front-door** to learn more.
- Residence Life staff are asked to **invite and encourage residents** to attend and take part in this major event.

---

### First Year Retreat – “Invite God In” (Last Weekend of September)

- As each academic year begins, RAs and other staff are encouraged to invite **First Year Students** to “**Invite God In**” by participating in the **First Year Retreat**.
- The retreat is held on the **last weekend of September**.
- Purpose and benefits:
  - Provides first-year students an opportunity to **get away** from campus.
  - Helps them **connect with other students** who are interested in faith connections.
  - Supports students in getting their **St. Thomas faith journey off to a great start**.
- Retreat experiences include (see video: `https://www.youtube.com/shorts/-dCTz00gSvw`):
  - Experiencing the **beauty of nature**.
  - Walking the **camp trails**.
  - **Canoeing**.
  - **Reflecting by the lake**.
  - Playing **volleyball**.
  - Enjoying the **company of peers by the bonfire**.
- Throughout the retreat, students make **lasting connections with other first year students**.

---

### Peer Ministry

- **Peer Ministers** (more information: `https://www.stthomas.edu/ministry/programming/peer-ministry/index.html`) are students who have themselves experienced the gift of encountering Christ.
- They are committed to:
  - Helping other students **deepen their relationship with God**.
  - Supporting students in **living out the Gospel call** to love and serve our neighbors.
  - **Creating meaningful encounters** that foster faith and community.
- Peer Ministers are a key resource to:
  - Get connected with the **faith community on campus**.
  - Help students form **intentional community** around faith and service.

#### Meeting Peer Ministers: Pancakes with Jesus

- The best place to meet Peer Ministers and learn about their programs and upcoming events is **Pancakes with Jesus**.
- **Pancakes with Jesus** is held on the **second Wednesday of each month** in **Scooters**.
- Residence Life staff should:
  - Attend and **introduce themselves** to the Peer Ministry team.
  - Let Peer Ministers know that they are **RAs**.
  - Be aware that Peer Ministers are **excited to work alongside and support RAs** as fellow student leaders.

---

### Ongoing Campus Ministry Opportunities

- Campus Ministry offers students **great possibilities to deepen their relationship with God** through **countless opportunities on a weekly or monthly basis**.
- These opportunities include, but are not limited to, participation in the **sacramental life** of the Church and other faith-based programs.

---

### Sacramental Life & the Chapel of St. Thomas Aquinas

- The **Chapel of St. Thomas Aquinas** stands at the **center of campus** as a **beacon of faith**, reminding the community of **God’s presence among us**.
- All are invited to participate in:
  - **Daily** sacramental and prayer opportunities.
  - **Sunday** worship and related faith gatherings.
- Campus Ministry encourages everyone to:
  - **CONNECT** with the faith community.
  - Remain open to **ENCOUNTER** with God through the sacramental life and campus faith events.

**Sources:**
- `Resource_ Campus Ministry_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### Campus Resources & Services
Campus Ministry: Multifaith Support, Programs, and Gatherings

Campus Ministry is committed to supporting students of all faith traditions and “to promote the pastoral care of all members of the university community” (EE 6,1). The office offers a wide range of programs, prayer opportunities, and community-building events, often in collaboration with faith-based student clubs and affiliated ministries that align with the institution’s mission and value cooperation in the pastoral care of students.

I. Sacred Spaces and Prayer  
The Chapel and other spaces of prayer provide a beautiful environment for prayer and silence amidst busy college life. These sacred spaces, including the Chapel and designated prayer areas, are intended to help make the Lord’s Day a grounding experience for students’ time at St. Thomas. More information about Catholic liturgies is available online:  
- Catholic liturgies: https://www.stthomas.edu/ministry/catholic-liturgy/index.html  
- Sacred spaces: https://www.stthomas.edu/ministry/sacred-spaces/index.html  

The Iversen Center for Faith includes a meditation room and wudu stations that are available for use at any time. In addition, there are three other meditation spaces on campus that are also available for use at any time. Details about the Iversen Center for Faith can be found here:  
- Iversen Center for Faith: https://www.stthomas.edu/about/our-campuses/st-paul/iversen-center-for-faith/index.html  

II. Muslim Student Programming  
Campus Ministry supports the Muslim community by offering opportunities to enrich their faith throughout the week.  
- Halaqa (scripture study) is led by the Imam each Wednesday.  
- Salat-ul-Jum’a (Friday prayer) is offered every Friday, followed by a community meal.  

Both the weekly halaqa and Salat-ul-Jum’a, including the community meal, take place in the meditation room in the Iversen Center for Faith.  

During Ramadan, Campus Ministry helps coordinate several opportunities on campus, including iftar dinners and an Eid celebration.

III. Jewish Student Programming: St. Thomas Hillel  
St. Thomas Hillel welcomes Jewish students and others who would like to learn about Jewish traditions.  
- Weekly gatherings are held on Thursdays in Campus Ministry.  
- Students celebrate holidays, play games, eat Jewish snacks, and build community.  

All are welcome to participate.

IV. Catholic Student Community: Tommie Catholic  
Tommie Catholic is a lively community of students who authentically embrace their Catholic faith and make Jesus the center of their lives.  
- Events are planned by students for students.  
- The group meets in the Iversen Center for Faith every Tuesday from 8–9 p.m.  

Students are invited to come, be embraced by the community, enjoy a variety of topics, and get connected to everything Catholic on campus.

V. Christian Worship Gatherings: Tommie BeLoved  
Tommie BeLoved is a weekly Christian gathering focused on praise, worship, and community.  
- Each Sunday evening, Christian students gather in the OEC Auditorium.  
- Together they unite their hearts, share the love of Jesus, and build a community where everyone feels seen and valued.

VI. Hispanic Faith Community: caFE y Amigos / Celebrando la Fe Juntos  
Campus Ministry offers Hispanic students, and all interested in the Hispanic experience of faith, opportunities to build community by celebrating and enriching faith experiences and traditions.  

Celebrando la Fe juntos – caFE y Amigos are monthly gatherings with food and fellowship.  
- These events are a collaboration between Campus Ministry and Catholic Studies Guadalupe Scholars.  
- The aim is to offer students the opportunity to gather and celebrate their faith together.  
- Although this initiative is tailored to Hispanic students, everyone is welcome.

More information is available here:  
- Hispanic students programming: https://www.stthomas.edu/ministry/programming/hispanic-students/index.html  

VII. Afro Christian Fellowship (ACF)  
Afro Christian Fellowship offers monthly ACF nights that provide students a space to celebrate their faith through cultural traditions.  
- Students can join for fellowship and growth as they learn about African faith tradition and expression.  
- Although tailored for students of African descent, everyone is welcome.

VIII. Peace Meals  
Peace Meals is a biweekly event that gathers people from different faith traditions, religious identities, and cultural backgrounds to share a meal and engage in honest discussions.  
- The goal is to build greater awareness and understanding of each other’s worldviews.  
- Peace Meals offers a path forward to build community and understanding across lines of religious and cultural division by gathering at the common table for food, friendship, and hospitality.  
- Peace Meals is a six-part series that requires advance registration; students should sign up at the beginning of the semester.

IX. Retreats  
Campus Ministry offers a number of overnight and weekend retreats throughout the academic year.  
- Offerings range from on-campus half-day events to destination weekend-long retreats.  
- These retreats provide opportunities to step away from the hecticness of daily life and devote time to encountering God and one another.  

Students can learn more about retreat offerings throughout the year here:  
- Retreats: https://www.stthomas.edu/ministry/programming/retreats/index.html  

X. Faith-Based Student Clubs and Affiliated Ministries  
Campus Ministry’s work is complemented by faith-based clubs and affiliated ministries that support the institution’s mission and collaborate in the pastoral care of students.  
- Students can explore events and activities hosted by each of the six faith-based student clubs that are affiliated with local Christian ministries.  
- Information is available on Tommie Link: https://tommielink.stthomas.edu/organizations?categories=8504  

XI. Multifaith Initiatives  
Campus Ministry’s multifaith initiatives include the programs and gatherings listed above—such as Muslim student programming, St. Thomas Hillel, Peace Meals, caFE y Amigos, Afro Christian Fellowship, Tommie Catholic, Tommie BeLoved, retreats, and the use of shared sacred spaces—all designed to support students of all faith traditions and foster understanding, hospitality, and community across religious and cultural lines.

**Sources:**
- `Resource_ Campus Ministry_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`
- `Resource_ Campus Ministry_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### Campus Services & Partnerships
MHC 106 U.S. Bank – Campus Banking Partnership

The University of St. Thomas partners with U.S. Bank (MHC 106) to provide a Campus Banking program rooted in financial wellness for the St. Thomas community. Key components of this partnership include:

**Young Adult Checking + Affinity Debit Cards**  
- A checking account that is easy and affordable for young adults (under 25).  
- A Visa® debit card branded with University of St. Thomas and related imagery.  
- Tommie Affinity Debit Card available as part of this program.

**Financial Wellness & Goals Coaching**  
- In-residency Goals Coaching program designed to boost financial wellness.  
- Educational videos and interactive tools to support financial wellness and goal setting.  
- Monthly workshops on campus covering both financial and non-financial topics.  
- 1:1 goals coaching sessions available to anyone in the St. Thomas community.

**Scholarship Opportunities**  
- Scholarship opportunities for students through the National Scholarship Sweepstakes.  
- St. Thomas–specific annual scholarship opportunities offered through the program.

**On-Campus Banking Support**  
- ATMs conveniently located on campus.  
- Collaboration space on campus to support banking and financial wellness activities.  
- Local Highland Park branch located approximately one mile off campus.

**Digital Banking Experience**  
- Best-in-class digital tools for budgeting and goal tracking.  
- Account aggregation features to keep financial life organized in one place.

### Campus Sustainability Events
Campus Sustainability Events

Campus Sustainability Month (October)  
Campus Sustainability Month is a celebration of sustainability in higher education held each October. Throughout the month, colleges and universities raise visibility around sustainability solutions and engage students and employees in initiatives to create a better world for present and future generations.

At St. Thomas, Campus Sustainability Month information and events are posted on:
- OneStThomas: https://one.stthomas.edu/sites/sustainability/SitePageModern/96509/campus-sustainability-month  
- Tommie Link: https://stthomas.campuslabs.com/engage/organization/osi  

Students and employees should check these sites regularly during October for updated event listings.

Sustainability Week (April)  
Sustainability Week is celebrated each year during the week of Earth Day (April 22). During this week, departments and organizations across campus host events that invite campus community members to explore sustainability initiatives and celebrate Earth Day together.

Information about Sustainability Week, including the schedule of events, is posted during spring semester. Community members should be on the lookout for the Sustainability Week schedule of events at:  
- OneStThomas: https://one.stthomas.edu/sites/sustainability/SitePageModern/165796/sustainability-week

**Sources:**
- `Resource_ Sustainability_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

Community Communications

Community Communications are the various methods Residence Life uses to share information with residents so they can find resources, engage in their community, and stay informed about what is happening on campus. These communications are provided to help students be resourceful and well-informed about campus opportunities and expectations, and to support them in meeting the Learning Outcomes of the Tommie Advantages.

Examples of Community Communications include, but are not limited to:
- Emails  
- Bulletin boards  
- GroupMe messages  

We use Community Communications to:
- Connect residents with campus resources  
- Promote community engagement opportunities  
- Share important updates and information about what is happening on campus  
- Support residents in achieving the Learning Outcomes of the Tommie Advantages

### Community Communications & Outreach
### Importance of Timely Community Communications

The CD team and Erica began preparing this year’s Community Communications in late March. All communications are planned and released according to a timeline that is informed by institutional context and history. For example, we generally know that more roommate conflicts occur in October and November than in September, so related content is scheduled accordingly.

Different types of content have different timing needs:
- Some communications must be released early enough to give residents ample time to read and process the information before related events or issues are likely to occur.
- Other communications are only posted for a short period, either because they are time-bound or because they are created in collaboration with campus partners who have their own schedules and deadlines.

All of this planning depends on staff posting materials when they are scheduled. TLDR: Post all communication on time.

**Sources:**
- `Community Communications.pdf`

### Community Values & Philosophy
COMMUNITY

Our community is defined by the following core qualities:

- **Connected** – Residents and staff build and maintain meaningful relationships, fostering a sense of belonging.
- **Intentional** – Actions, programs, and interactions are purposeful and thoughtfully designed to support residents.
- **Inclusive** – All individuals are welcomed and valued, with differences respected and embraced.
- **Empowering** – Residents are encouraged and supported to take initiative, make decisions, and grow in independence.
- **Safe** – Physical, emotional, and social safety are prioritized in all aspects of community life.
- **Welcoming** – New and returning residents are greeted with openness, warmth, and hospitality.
- **Comfortable** – Spaces and interactions promote ease, relaxation, and a sense of being at home.
- **Caring** – Community members look out for one another’s well-being and offer support when needed.

**Sources:**
- `Creating Connections & Touch Points 25-26.pptx`

### Counseling & Psychological Services (CAPS)
Groups at CAPS

Counseling and Psychological Services (CAPS) offers groups that provide a supportive, confidential space to connect with others and grow. These groups are facilitated by trained professionals to keep the group safe, focused, and guided.

Key benefits of participating in a CAPS group include:

- **Shared Understanding**  
  Connect with peers who “get it” and are facing similar challenges.

- **Support & Encouragement**  
  Give and receive feedback in a safe, non-judgmental space.

- **Skill Building**  
  Practice coping strategies, communication, and problem-solving in real time.

- **New Perspectives**  
  Hear different viewpoints that can shift your thinking and spark new solutions.

- **Sense of Belonging**  
  Reduce feelings of isolation by being part of a supportive community.

- **Real-World Practice**  
  Try out new ways of relating in a group before applying them in everyday life.

- **Confidential & Guided**  
  Participate in a confidential setting that is facilitated by trained professionals to maintain safety and focus.

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

### International Students & Visas
**Steps for International Students to Study in the United States**

International students must complete the following steps, in order, to come to the United States to study and remain in status while at school:

1. **Apply to a university that has been approved by the government.**  
   - You must first apply to a university that has been approved by the government to enroll international (nonimmigrant) students.

2. **Receive Form I-20 “Certificate of Eligibility for Nonimmigrant Students.”**  
   - After you are admitted to an approved university, you will receive Form I-20, titled “Certificate of Eligibility for Nonimmigrant Students.”

3. **Pay the I-901 SEVIS Fee ($350).**  
   - Once you have your Form I-20, you must pay the I-901 SEVIS Fee in the amount of **$350**.

4. **Schedule an appointment for a student visa.**  
   - After paying the I-901 SEVIS Fee, schedule an appointment for a student visa at a U.S. embassy or consulate.

5. **Arrive in the United States.**  
   - When your student visa is approved, you may travel and arrive in the United States to begin your studies.

6. **Maintain a full course of study while at school.**  
   - After arrival, you must maintain a full course of study while at school in order to remain in compliance with your student status.

**Sources:**
- `Res Life Training 2025.pdf`
- `Res Life Training 2025.pdf`
