### Overview of Residence Life Operations
This entry is intended to provide a comprehensive overview of the internal residence life operations as captured in the audio files. The operations include several key aspects that are essential for maintaining an efficient and effective residential experience for all students.

Firstly, the residence life team is responsible for the coordination and management of all housing assignments. This involves ensuring that each student is placed in a suitable living environment that meets their needs and preferences. The team must also handle any requests for room changes, which are processed based on availability and the specific reasons provided by the students.

Secondly, the operations include the implementation of community-building activities. These activities are designed to foster a sense of belonging and engagement among residents. The residence life team organizes events and programs that encourage interaction and collaboration, contributing to a vibrant and inclusive community atmosphere.

Additionally, the residence life staff is tasked with enforcing community standards and policies. This includes addressing any violations of the residence hall rules, which are clearly outlined in the student handbook. The staff is trained to handle disciplinary matters with fairness and consistency, ensuring that all students are aware of their responsibilities and the consequences of their actions.

Furthermore, the residence life operations involve providing support services to residents. This includes offering resources for academic support, mental health counseling, and conflict resolution. The staff is available to assist students in navigating any challenges they may face during their time in the residence halls.

Lastly, the residence life team is committed to continuous improvement of the residential experience. Feedback from residents is regularly collected and analyzed to identify areas for enhancement. The team strives to implement changes that will benefit the overall community and ensure a positive living environment for all students.

In summary, the residence life operations encompass housing assignments, community-building activities, policy enforcement, support services, and continuous improvement efforts. These components work together to create a supportive and dynamic residential experience for all students.

**Sources:**
- `redirect-audio-4.mp3`
- `roompact.mp3`
- `Tommie_Advantage.mp3`

### Professional Associations & Acknowledgments
The Association of Catholic Colleges and Universities (ACCU), founded in 1899, is the collective voice of Catholic higher education. Through seminars, conferences, publications, research, and consultation, ACCU helps campuses foster a vibrant Catholic identity. Additional information is available at www.accunet.org.

The Association for Student Affairs at Catholic Colleges and Universities (ASACCU) exists to promote an understanding of the rich Catholic intellectual tradition and its relevance for student affairs professionals who are working, or will be working, at Catholic colleges and universities. Through its activities and programs, ASACCU encourages and facilitates the sharing of ideas and cooperative efforts among its members. Additional information is available at www.asaccu.org.

The Jesuit Association of Student Personnel Administration (JASPA) is a conference of the Association of Jesuit Colleges & Universities (AJCU). JASPA was founded in 1954 as the Conference of Jesuit Student Personnel Administrators (CJSPA). In fall 1981, JASPA became the official name of the organization and continues today, as the original members of CJSPA intended, to work to promote the mission of Jesuit higher education. JASPA’s efforts to educate student affairs practitioners include: an annual conference, a summer workshop every five years, summer institutes, newsletters, and various other publications and meetings. Members of JASPA represent the 28 Jesuit colleges and universities in the United States and Belize and also include affiliate members from other institutions. Additional information is available at www.jesuitstudentaffairs.org.

### Programming & Community Building
Know Your Audience When Planning Events

When planning events, be intentional and thoughtful. Always consider the specific needs and dynamics of your community.

- Be mindful of academic busy times and major scheduling conflicts when choosing dates and times.
- Be considerate of timing so that attending is an “easy yes” for residents.
- Ask yourself whether the event idea is genuinely desirable for your residents.
- Make sure the event is accessible and incorporates universal design so all residents can participate.
- Change it up—avoid repeating the same event over and over.
- Ask your residents for their input on what events they want and how to improve existing ideas.

- `RL-Residential Connection Events1 (1).pdf`

### Residence Life Operations Overview
**Residence Life Operations with Zoe & Alexis**

**Emergency Protocols and Contact Information**

In the event of an emergency, residents should immediately contact Campus Security at (555) 123-4567. For non-emergencies, the Residence Life Office can be reached at (555) 987-6543 during business hours, Monday through Friday, 9:00 AM to 5:00 PM. Zoe and Alexis are the primary contacts for residence life operations and are available to assist with any concerns or questions.

**Room Assignments and Changes**

Room assignments are determined based on the preferences submitted in the housing application. If a resident wishes to change rooms, they must submit a Room Change Request Form, available at the Residence Life Office. Requests are processed on a first-come, first-served basis, and approval is contingent upon availability.

**Maintenance Requests**

Residents experiencing maintenance issues should submit a Maintenance Request Form online through the Residence Life portal. Urgent issues, such as plumbing leaks or electrical failures, should be reported directly to the maintenance hotline at (555) 234-5678 for immediate attention.

**Quiet Hours and Conduct**

Quiet hours are enforced from 10:00 PM to 8:00 AM daily to ensure a conducive study and rest environment. Residents are expected to adhere to the community standards outlined in the Residence Life Handbook. Violations may result in disciplinary action, including warnings or fines.

**Mail and Package Delivery**

Mail is delivered to the residence halls Monday through Saturday. Packages can be collected from the mailroom during its operating hours, 10:00 AM to 6:00 PM. Residents will receive an email notification when a package arrives.

**Laundry Facilities**

Laundry facilities are available in each residence hall and operate 24/7. Machines accept both coins and the campus card. Residents are encouraged to promptly remove their laundry to ensure availability for others.

For further assistance or inquiries, residents can contact Zoe and Alexis directly via email at reslife@university.edu.

**Sources:**
- `Residence Life Operations 2025.pdf`

### Sustainability & Campus Resources
Sustainability at St. Thomas

Sustainability is central to the University of St. Thomas’s mission of advancing the common good. The university envisions sustainability as fostering interconnected human and environmental well-being now and for future generations. Each individual and every field of study has something unique and important to contribute to this effort.

St. Thomas infuses sustainability into academic programs, student life, facilities, dining, and campus operations. This work is grounded in the university’s identity as a Catholic institution and embraces the principles of caring for creation, environmental stewardship, and advancing the common good on a shared planet.

The university’s sustainability vision is to “catalyze university-wide engagement to create a culture that prepares students to be sustainability leaders during their university years and beyond.”

Office of Sustainability Initiatives (OSI)

The Office of Sustainability Initiatives (OSI) partners with units across the university to create a culture of sustainability on campus that prepares students to be sustainability leaders and collaborators during their university years and beyond.

More information about OSI is available at:
https://one.stthomas.edu/sites/sustainability/SitePageModern/78022/sustainability-home

Contact:
- Dr. Maria Dahmus, Director, Office of Sustainability Initiatives  
  Email: medahmus@stthomas.edu

**Sources:**
- `Resource_ Sustainability_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### Sustainability & Event Planning
When planning giveaways for events or tabling, it's important to consider their environmental impact. Giveaways often generate waste, so assess whether they are essential to the event's success. Opt for usable items that encourage sustainable behaviors. Instead of distributing numerous small items that might be discarded, consider organizing a drawing for a single, larger prize. If distributing treats or candy, select those with minimal, recyclable, or compostable packaging.

For further guidance on sustainability, consult the following resources:
- [Guide to Campus Sustainability](https://one.stthomas.edu/media/12022/guide-to-sustainability-at-st-thomas-3.pdf)
- [Sustainability Digital Badges](https://one.stthomas.edu/sites/sustainability/SitePageModern/168447/sustainability-essentials-digital-badge)
- [Guide to Hosting Sustainable Events](https://one.stthomas.edu/media/13062/guide-to-hosting-sustainable-events-2.pdf)
- [Office of Sustainability Initiatives (OneStThomas)](https://one.stthomas.edu/sites/sustainability/SitePageModern/78024/campus-initiatives)
- [Office of Sustainability Initiatives (Public Website)](https://www.stthomas.edu/about/sustainability/osi/index.html)

For more information, contact Rachel Schauer at [rschauer@stthomas.edu](mailto:rschauer@stthomas.edu).

**Sources:**
- `Event Resource_ Considerations for Sustainability in Event Planning_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

---

**Sustainable Event Planning Guidelines**

Incorporating sustainability into event planning not only benefits the environment but can also lead to cost savings. Below are key considerations for organizing sustainable activities:

**Transportation:**
- For large off-campus group events, consider chartering a bus to reduce individual car usage.
- Organize group walks or bike rides for nearby off-campus events to minimize carbon emissions.
- Utilize the campus shuttle for events near the Minneapolis campus to reduce the need for personal vehicles.

**Meetings and Events:**
- Opt for virtual meetings to eliminate the need for transportation, saving both time and energy.
- Encourage participants to bring their own drinking containers to reduce disposable cup waste.
- Distribute agendas via email in advance and print only when absolutely necessary. Explore digital presentation options and ensure that any printed materials are recycled after use.

**Catering:**
- Order catering quantities based on previous attendance and consumption data to minimize food waste.
- Encourage attendees to bring their own reusable cups, utensils, bowls, or plates, depending on the event's nature.
- Select healthy food options that have a lower environmental impact.

By adhering to these guidelines, event planners can contribute to a more sustainable future while potentially reducing event costs.

**Sources:**
- `Event Resource_ Considerations for Sustainability in Event Planning_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### Sustainable Events & Operations Practices
Sustainable Event and Operations Practices

Plant-Based Choices  
- Plant-based items generally have a lower water and carbon footprint than animal proteins.

Avoiding Waste  
- Use what you already have before buying something new.  
- Update previously used trifolds instead of creating new ones; this saves time and minimizes waste.  
- Check whether other communities have resources you can borrow before purchasing additional items.  
- Try to repair broken club equipment or other items instead of throwing them away and buying replacements.  
- Avoid using single-use products that will be thrown away after events or meetings, such as printed agendas or disposable event supplies.  
- If single-use items are necessary, consider using compostable items.

Waste Sorting for Events  
- Properly sort your waste for all events.  
- If your event is going to produce more waste than the existing bins in your event space can handle, you can request extra bins for trash, compost, or recycling through your 25-Live event request.

Composting  
- Compost organic material such as food, napkins, and BPI certified compostable products.  
- A good rule of thumb: if it was once alive, it is compostable.  
- Look for the BPI certified compostable product symbol to confirm items are compostable.

Recycling  
- For recycling, be mindful of the numbers listed on plastic items.  
- Recycle only clean paper, glass, cardboard, metal, and plastics numbered 1, 2, and 5.  
- Do not recycle items that have been soiled with food or other contaminants. (If they are BPI certified, they may be compostable instead.)  
- Some items must be recycled separately, including batteries, light bulbs, plastic bags, and plastic film. These can be recycled in the create[space] and in the campus store in Murray-Herrick, where there is a special recycling bin for these materials.  
- “When in doubt, throw it out” – if you are unsure whether an item should be recycled or composted, it is better to place it in the trash than risk contaminating the recycling or composting bin.

Communication at Events  
- Consider making an announcement about what items can be recycled, composted, and thrown away.  
- If you are using a slide show, consider adding a slide with this information.

Giveaways Considerations  
- [Use the above principles—avoiding waste, choosing compostable or recyclable materials, and preferring lower-impact, plant-based or reusable items—when planning and selecting event giveaways.]

**Sources:**
- `Event Resource_ Considerations for Sustainability in Event Planning_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### RA Compensation & Agreements
**2025 – 2026 Student Leader Agreement: Resident Advisor Discounts and Stipend**

As a Resident Advisor (RA) for the 2025 – 2026 academic year, I am required to maintain a housing agreement with the Department of Residence Life for the full term of my position, from August 13, 2025, to May 31, 2026. During my tenure, I will receive a full discount on my on-campus housing and meal plan, enabling me to live and dine with my residents and fulfill my RA responsibilities. Should I resign or be dismissed, the housing discount will be reversed on a pro-rata basis, and I will be responsible for the remaining housing costs if I choose to stay on campus. Additionally, my meal plan will be removed from my account.

I will receive a stipend of $450 for the academic year, distributed bi-weekly, with applicable State and Federal taxes deducted. If University or Department operations change, my stipend may be adjusted accordingly. I acknowledge that the stipend and room and board benefits may impact my financial aid for the 2025 – 2026 year, and it is my responsibility to consult with my financial aid counselor to understand any effects.

During periods when all food service options are closed, and I am on duty, I will be provided with eXpress funds. At times when the dining halls are closed, I may need to use my meal plan’s Dining Dollars while on duty. In late August, between terms, after graduation, or whenever meal plans are inactive, I will receive meals or eXpress funds to support my resident interactions. I understand that I might need to supplement these provisions with additional food at my own expense.

As an international student, I may face different tax implications for the discounts and stipends received in my RA role. This agreement is subject to termination at any time for any reason, including changes in University operations. However, the intention is to maintain this leadership and learning opportunity throughout the academic year, with ongoing performance assessments and opportunities for improvement. High-performing students may reapply and be evaluated for a return as a Resident Advisor, with a maximum service period of two years. If this agreement is terminated, I will only receive benefits up to the termination date.

**Sources:**
- `RA Student Leader Agreement 2025-2026.pdf`

### Roommate Relations & Conflict Resolution
### Offer Strategies for Roommate Concerns

When residents experience conflict or tension with a roommate, staff should guide them through the following strategies. These steps are designed to help residents address issues directly and collaboratively.

1. **Adjust Expectations (What Is Realistic?)**  
   - Help residents reflect on what is realistic to expect in a shared living space.  
   - Encourage them to distinguish between preferences and essential needs.

2. **Share Concerns with Roommate**  
   - Encourage residents to communicate their concerns directly with their roommate.  
   - Emphasize using respectful, clear, and specific language when describing the issue.

3. **Request Behavior Change**  
   - Support residents in making a direct request for specific behavior changes.  
   - The request should focus on observable actions (what the roommate can do differently), not on personal attacks or assumptions.

4. **Brainstorm a Compromise**  
   - Guide residents to work with their roommate to identify possible solutions together.  
   - Encourage flexibility and mutual give-and-take so that both parties’ needs are considered.

5. **Revisit the Roommate Agreement**  
   - Have residents review their existing Roommate Agreement to see what was originally discussed and agreed upon.  
   - If needed, help them revise or update the agreement to reflect new understandings or arrangements.

6. **Room Changes Are Not an Option**  
   - Clearly communicate that room changes are not an option in this context.  
   - Reinforce that the focus is on using the strategies above to resolve concerns within the current roommate arrangement.

**Sources:**
- `Roommate Agreement & Conflict Resolution Process.pdf`
- `Roommate Agreement & Conflict Resolution Process.pdf`

## Incidents
### Alcohol & Other Drugs Incidents
On Tuesday, March 32nd, at 12:00 am, Resident Advisor Jennie Kim was conducting rounds when she heard loud music and screaming from the north end of the 3rd floor of Dowling Hall. Upon investigation, RA Kim found Resident Jeongyeon Yoo holding the door open to room DOW 13, with an open can of Loon Juice Hard Cider in hand, wooing at unidentified individuals inside. RA Kim instructed Resident Yoo to step back inside and close the door, which she complied with.

At 12:06 am, due to the presence of alcohol, RA Kim contacted Public Safety. Shortly after, at 12:08 am, two unidentified individuals exited DOW 13 and proceeded toward the elevator, ignoring RA Kim's request to wait. Public Safety Officer Smith arrived at 12:15 am and knocked on the door of DOW 13. Resident Yoo, the sole occupant of the room, opened the door and questioned the presence of Public Safety.

Officer Smith inquired if Resident Yoo had consumed alcohol. Six cans of Loon Juice Hard Cider were visible on a desk, and Resident Yoo appeared slightly intoxicated. Officer Smith asked her to sit down, and after confirming alcohol consumption, performed a Preliminary Breath Test (PBT), which resulted in a .09 reading.

At 12:25 am, Resident Chaeyoung Son returned to DOW 13. Resident Son had not consumed alcohol and was not present during the earlier events. Officer Smith decided that Resident Yoo could remain in the room under the supervision of Resident Son, who agreed to monitor her. Officer Smith informed Resident Yoo that she would be contacted by the Dean of Student Office regarding the conduct process.

Officer Smith confiscated the remaining three unopened cans of Loon Juice Hard Cider, and with RA Kim, disposed of the alcohol in the bathroom. The incident concluded at 12:35 am.

**Sources:**

### Alcohol & Substance Use Incidents
Scenario 4: While stationed at your community desk, you observe a peer entering the building. They pause to engage in conversation with you, during which you notice signs of disorientation and detect the odor of alcohol. After the interaction, they proceed towards their room, visibly stumbling as they walk. 

Steps to address this situation: 
1. Remain calm and professional, ensuring your peer's safety is your top priority.
2. Discreetly monitor their progress to their room to ensure they do not harm themselves or others.
3. Once they have reached their room, promptly report the incident to your supervisor or the appropriate residence life staff member.
4. Document the encounter, including the time, location, and any specific observations regarding their behavior and condition.
5. Follow any additional protocols outlined by your residence life department for handling situations involving intoxicated individuals. 

These steps are crucial for maintaining a safe and supportive community environment.

**Sources:**
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`

### Alcohol-Related Incidents & RA Response Procedures
**Alcohol Related Incidents – RA Response Procedures**

**Intoxicated Student Intervention:**

When encountering an obviously impaired student, prioritize their health and safety, as well as the safety of others. If you assess that the student's or others' health is in danger, follow these steps:

1. **Contact Public Safety:** Immediately report the incident's location and provide identifying details about the student, such as clothing, height, and hair color. Include observed behaviors.
   
2. **Assess the Situation:** Determine key details such as the student's ability to walk, speech clarity, presence of injuries, and any noticeable odors.

3. **Monitor Physical Symptoms:** Watch for changes in skin color, breathing, vomiting, or unconsciousness.

4. **Facilitate Emergency Response:** If paramedics are called, ensure Public Safety and medics have space to work, possibly by waiting in the hallway.

5. **Transportation Decisions:** Public Safety will notify the CD/GSA if students are transported off-campus. If not transported, Public Safety will decide the student's overnight arrangements. Student Leaders must not stay with the student.

6. **Documentation:** Thoroughly document the incident in Advocate. The CD/GSA may request follow-up with the student upon their return to campus. If uncertain about responding, consult the CD/GSA.

**Policy Violation Observed:**

For any alcohol-related policy violations, such as possession or consumption by underage students or in prohibited areas, follow these steps:

1. **Contact Public Safety:** Request their presence and inform violators of the policy breach.

2. **Gather Information:** Collect student ID details for report writing.

3. **Document Interaction:** Note Public Safety's interaction with students. They will instruct residents to dispose of alcohol if against policy.

4. **Report Submission:** Document the incident in Advocate.

5. **Empty Containers:** If found during health and safety checks, dispose of them with a student, duty partner, or Public Safety. Submit a report in Advocate.

**Alcohol Policy Confrontation Tips:**

- **Indicators of Alcohol Use:** If you notice signs such as alcohol containers, impaired motor skills, or slurred speech, and know of underage residents, contact Public Safety.
  
- **Significant Impairment:** For any student showing significant impairment signs, regardless of age, contact Public Safety.

- **Collect ID Information:** Gather first and last names and ID numbers. Explain documentation purposes to students.

- **Room Scan:** Note any visible alcohol for documentation.

- **Public Safety Actions:** They will ask residents to disclose all alcohol or substances and may conduct a search.

- **Alcohol Details:** Record the type and amount of alcohol involved, including any disclosed consumption by residents.

- **Safety Concerns:** If you feel unsafe, leave the scene and wait nearby for Public Safety. In extreme cases, update Public Safety on your location if you leave the area entirely. Contact the CD/GSA for guidance if needed, but prioritize contacting Public Safety.

**Sources:**
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`

### Bias & Discrimination Reporting
Bias and Discrimination: RA Reporting Requirements

Resident Assistants are required to report any instances of bias or discrimination that they become aware of in the course of their role.

Reportable situations include, but are not limited to:
- Visible examples of bias or discrimination in the community, such as racist or homophobic signage found in hallways or other public areas.
- Any situation in which a student discloses to you that they have experienced bias or discrimination.

If you become aware of a bias or discrimination concern:
1. Report the incident in accordance with departmental procedures.
2. Contact your CD/GSA for additional guidance and support in responding to the situation.
3. For more information on bias and hate reporting, refer to:  
   https://stthomas.edu/student-affairs/departments/dean-of-students/bias-hate-reporting/index.html

### Communicating with Residents During an Incident
Communicating with Residents During an Incident

When you make contact with residents (e.g., after knocking on a door or approaching a group), follow these guidelines:

1. **State the reason for contact**
   - Clearly explain why you knocked or initiated contact.
   - Provide direct instructions as needed, for example:
     - “I need you to open the door.”
     - “I need you to turn off the music.”

2. **Gather information (if appropriate)**
   - If appropriate to the situation, ask for more information about what happened.
   - Use open, calm questions to understand the circumstances while maintaining control of the interaction.

3. **Collect identification**
   - If possible, collect student IDs from everyone involved.
   - If a student says they do not have their ID card, collect their ID number instead.
   - For non–St. Thomas students, collect student IDs if available, or another form of identification for non-students.

4. **Manage movement during the interaction**
   - Do not allow people to leave while you are actively addressing the situation. Use clear, calm language such as:
     - “Stay where you are please, I’ll ask you to have a seat.”
   - However, if someone chooses to leave despite your direction, do not physically prevent them from leaving. Let them go.

**Sources:**
- `Enforcing Policies and Following Procedures.pdf`

---

### Communication with Residents During Incidents
Communicating with Residents During Incidents

When communicating with residents during any incident or response:

- Allow Public Safety to carry out their responsibilities without interference. Do not attempt to direct or override their actions.
- Do not minimize the situation. Acknowledge that something is happening and take residents’ concerns seriously.
- It is acceptable if you are not able to answer all questions. Be honest about what you do and do not know, and avoid providing speculative or inaccurate information.

**Sources:**
- `Enforcing Policies and Following Procedures.pdf`

### Documentation
**Documenting an Incident**

Throughout any incident, continuously collect and record all relevant information. Be sure to include:

- **Time and date** of the incident.  
- **Location(s)** where the incident occurred.  
- **Names and IDs** of all involved students and guests.  
- **Direct quotes**, written down as accurately as possible, when they are relevant to understanding what occurred.  
- **A clear description of the situation**, including:
  - Behaviors observed  
  - Substances involved  
  - Interactions between individuals and any other notable details

**Sources:**
- `Enforcing Policies and Following Procedures.pdf`

### Documentation & Reporting
As a Student Leader, a key part of your role is responding to a wide range of situations in your community and documenting them accurately. These situations may include, but are not limited to:

- An intoxicated student  
- A student with a mental health concern  
- A noisy room  
- A water pipe bursting on the 3rd floor of your building  
- The death of a resident’s family member  
- A resident experiencing an eating disorder  

It is your responsibility to respond to these situations and document everything relevant that you see, hear, and smell.

There are two primary types of documentation:

1. **Incident Reports (submitted to your CD/GSA)**  
   - The information you document and submit to your CD/GSA about policy-related situations is called an **incident report**.  
   - You must submit an incident report for **all policy violations**.  
   - Incident reports are extremely important to CDs/GSAs and are used in a variety of ways, including follow-up conversations and conduct meetings.

2. **Community or Resident Concerns (submitted through Roompact)**  
   - You will submit a **community or resident concern** through Roompact when the situation is **not** a policy violation, but is still something your CD/GSA needs to be aware of.  
   - These concerns help contribute to our overall picture of a student’s experience and may include:  
     - A mental health concern  
     - The death of a resident’s family member  
     - An eating disorder  
     - Facilities issues such as a water pipe bursting on the 3rd floor  

This section will provide you with important tips, key points to remember for writing informative incident reports, and examples of what your CD/GSA is looking for in your documentation.

**Sources:**
- `Resource_ Introduction to Advocate_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

---

### Documenting Incidents Involving Public Safety
**Documenting Incidents Involving Public Safety**

When an incident occurs that involves Public Safety and may relate to Residence Life:

1. **Obtain Incident Information**
   - Get the ICR# (Incident Case Report number) from Public Safety so it can be included in the Advocate report.
   - Obtain the names of all Public Safety Officers who were present at the incident.

2. **Inform Residents About Possible Follow-Up**
   - If a policy violation is involved, you may remind the residents that they may be contacted by:
     - Residence Life, or  
     - Dean of Students Office.

3. **Follow-Up with Residents**
   - Connect with the residents again at a later time in a non-conduct-related context.
   - Do not judge your residents based on one instance of bad decision making.

**Sources:**
- `Enforcing Policies and Following Procedures.pdf`

### Drug & Alcohol Incidents
**Response to Drug Law Violations and Suspicious Odors in Residence Halls**

When there is a suspected drug law violation or a suspicious odor in a residence hall, follow this process:

1. **Initial Notification**
   - In most cases, you will call our dispatch and notify them of the situation in a residence hall.
   - In some cases, our dispatcher will instead contact and request that an RA respond.

2. **Officer Dispatch and Arrival**
   - Our dispatch will always send at least two Officers to the room.
   - Upon arrival, Officers will speak with you outside of the room to:
     - Understand what is going on, **or**
     - Explain to you what is going on if they were dispatched independently.

3. **Room Entry**
   - Depending on the situation, Officers will either:
     - Knock on the door, **or**
     - Key into the room.

4. **Identification and Residents**
   - Officers will determine who lives in the room.
   - Officers will collect IDs from the relevant individuals.

5. **Questioning and Search**
   - Officers will question the person(s) about the smell.
   - Officers will conduct a cursory search.
   - If any items are found, Officers will determine who the item belongs to.

6. **Evidence and Documentation**
   - Evidence will be gathered by the Officers.
   - Officers will document the incident in a report.
   - Officers will collect your name, ID number, and title for the report.
   - Officers will provide you with:
     - Their names
     - Their badge numbers
     - The report number

   - Drug law violations and suspicious odors are **always** documented.
   - If an Officer forgets to tell you the report number, you should ask for it.

7. **Follow-Up**
   - Our Officers will follow up with the Hall Director Duty phone to inform them of what happened.

**Sources:**
- `2025 Res Life Presentation.pdf`

### Front Desk Protocols
During your shift at the desk, if you notice someone standing in the entryway for approximately 10 minutes and knocking on the glass to get your attention, follow these steps to ensure a proper interaction:

1. **Acknowledge the Visitor**: Immediately acknowledge the individual by making eye contact and signaling that you will attend to them shortly.

2. **Approach and Greet**: Leave your desk and approach the entryway to greet the visitor. Politely inquire about their needs or concerns.

3. **Assess the Situation**: Determine the purpose of their visit. If they require assistance, provide the necessary help or direct them to the appropriate resources.

4. **Policy Considerations**: Ensure that all interactions comply with residence life policies, particularly those concerning visitor access and student safety. Verify if the visitor has a legitimate reason to be on the premises.

5. **Identify Potential Risks**: Evaluate any factors that may pose a risk or harm to students, such as unauthorized access or suspicious behavior. Remain vigilant and prioritize the safety of the residents.

6. **Contacting Authorities**: If the situation involves a policy violation or potential threat, contact the appropriate authorities or campus security for assistance.

7. **Document the Incident**: Record the details of the interaction in the incident log, noting the time, nature of the visit, and any actions taken.

By following these steps, you ensure a safe and efficient handling of common incidents, maintaining the security and well-being of the residence community.

**Sources:**
- `Enforcing Policies and Following Procedures.pdf`

### Incident Documentation
All general concerns, FYIs, and Code of Conduct issues must be documented in an incident report in Advocate. Documentation must:

- Be written in narrative format.  
- Be written in third person.  
- Avoid the use of pronouns.  
- Be detailed and comprehensive.

It is recommended to draft the narrative in a Word document first, then input the final version into Advocate. This should be done even if Public Safety indicates that an incident report is not needed.

**Sources:**
- `Enforcing Policies and Following Procedures.pdf`

---

### Incident Report Writing
To write a quality Incident Report (IR), begin by clearly stating the date and time of the incident. Provide a detailed, objective, and thorough account of the situation, including how you became aware of it. Include all relevant facts about the incident, such as information about all involved parties and specifics about the violation or situation. The report should read as a moment-by-moment description of what occurred. When possible, incorporate exact quotes from residents, especially if they are yelling or swearing, to accurately convey the situation.

**Sources:**
- `IR Writing.pdf`

---

### Incident Report Writing & Submission
When submitting an Incident Report (IR), it becomes part of a formal, written record for the Office of Residence Life, the Dean of Students Office, and the University. This report can be reviewed by University staff, the student involved, and their representatives. Therefore, adhere to the following guidelines:

1. **Objective Reporting**: Only include factual observations, not opinions. For instance, instead of stating "The resident was non-compliant," describe the behavior factually: "Resident [Last Name] raised their voice with Public Safety Officers and repeatedly refused to sit down after multiple directives."

2. **Comprehensive Details**: Include all relevant facts, such as how you became aware of the situation, information about all involved parties, and details about the violation or situation. Begin every report with the date and time, and provide a moment-by-moment account of the incident.

3. **Use of Quotes**: When possible, use exact quotes from residents, especially if they are yelling or swearing.

4. **Third-Person Writing**: Write in the third person. Refer to yourself as RA [Last Name] (e.g., RA Anderson) and others as Resident [Last Name] or Officer [Last Name] to avoid confusion.

5. **Corrections and Accuracy**: If you realize an error or omission after submission, inform your CD/GSA immediately. Do not write anything you cannot confirm. Proofread for content, accuracy, spelling, grammar, and sentence structure.

6. **Submission Process**: Draft the report in a word document first, then copy and paste it into the Advocate system. If Public Safety is involved, record the last names of all officers and the report number. If information is missing, request the officers to provide it.

7. **Timeliness**: Submit the report as soon as possible, ideally before going to bed on the night of the incident. Do not delay until the next morning.

8. **Sensitive Information**: If you have notes containing ID numbers or other sensitive information, hand them to your CD/GSA for shredding.

Incident reports are submitted through the Advocate website. Ensure you follow these guidelines to maintain the integrity and accuracy of the report.

**Sources:**
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`
- `Resource_ Tips for Incident Report Writing_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### Incident Report Writing Guidelines
**Guidelines for Writing a Quality Incident Report (IR):**

1. **Documenting Public Safety Involvement:** 
   - When Public Safety officers are involved in an incident, ensure you record the last names of all officers present and the report number. If any details are missing, request the officers to provide the necessary information.

2. **Timely Submission:** 
   - Submit your incident report as soon as possible. It is crucial to complete and submit the report before you retire for the night. Do not delay submission until the following morning.

3. **Handling Sensitive Information:** 
   - If your notes contain ID numbers or other sensitive information, hand them over to your Community Director (CD) or Graduate Student Assistant (GSA) for shredding to ensure confidentiality.

4. **Submission Process:** 
   - All incident reports must be submitted through the website called Advocate. You can find the link to this platform in the designated resources section.

By adhering to these guidelines, you ensure that all incident reports are thorough, accurate, and submitted in a timely manner.

**Sources:**
- `IR Writing.pdf`

When submitting an Incident Report (IR), it becomes a formal, written record accessible to the Office of Residence Life, the Dean of Students Office, and the University. It may also be reviewed by University staff, the student involved, and their representatives. Therefore, it is crucial to adhere to the following guidelines to ensure the report's quality and objectivity:

- Focus solely on factual information and observations, avoiding personal opinions about the resident or their behavior.
- For instance, instead of stating an opinion such as "The resident was non-compliant," provide specific facts and observations: "Resident [Last Name] raised their voice with Public Safety Officers and repeatedly refused to sit down after multiple directives."

By following these guidelines, you contribute to a clear and accurate record that can be effectively used by all parties involved.

**How to Write a Quality Incident Report (IR)**

When drafting an Incident Report (IR), ensure that you consider the potential audience, as others will read it. If you realize after submission that you omitted information, misidentified someone, or included incorrect details, promptly inform your Community Director (CD) or Graduate Student Assistant (GSA) to make the necessary corrections. Only include information you can verify—if you can't confirm it, don't write it.

Before finalizing your report, proofread for both content and accuracy. Check for spelling, grammar, and sentence structure errors, similar to how you would review a paper. To facilitate this process, it is advisable to first type your report in a word processing document. Once satisfied with the narrative, copy and paste it into the Advocate system.

**Sources:**
- `IR Writing.pdf`

Common IR Writing Mistakes:

When documenting incidents, it is important to use the correct terminology to ensure clarity and professionalism. Here are some common mistakes and their corrections:

- Use "smelled" instead of "smelt."
- Refer to "Public Safety" rather than "Pub Safe."
- Use "marijuana" or "THC" instead of "weed."
- Refer to individuals as "residents" or "students" rather than using "residence."
- Use "intoxicated" instead of "drunk."
- Refer to a "residence hall" instead of a "dorm."
- Provide a "description of actions" rather than a "summary/judgment of actions."

Adhering to these guidelines will ensure that incident reports are clear, accurate, and respectful.

**Sources:**
- `IR Writing.pdf`

### Incident Reporting Procedures
**Incident Reporting Procedures**

**Example 1: Alcohol Violation**

On Saturday, September 28, 2024, at 10:15 PM, Resident Advisor (RA) Tom Jones was conducting a routine round in the building when he detected loud music coming from Flynn room 609. As he approached the door, he overheard someone inside say, "let's take another shot." RA Jones knocked on the door and identified himself as the RA on duty. The music volume was reduced, and Jake Jakeson, a resident of room 609, opened the door. RA Jones explained the situation and requested permission to enter the room, which Jakeson granted. Inside, RA Jones observed a bottle of Skyy Vodka (approximately half full) and six bottles of Bud Light (2 full, 2 empty, and 2 half full). Given the presence of alcohol, RA Jones contacted Public Safety.

Public Safety officers Katz (Badge #100) and Green (Badge #101) arrived at 10:32 PM. RA Jones requested the St. Thomas IDs from both residents, Jakeson and his roommate, Jayson Grand, who provided them willingly and admitted they were underage. When asked if there was additional alcohol, both residents denied it. Officer Katz requested to check the refrigerator, which Grand opened, revealing no further alcohol. Officer Green instructed Grand and Jakeson to dispose of the alcohol in the kitchen sink, which they did, and the bottles were recycled in the trash room. RA Jones informed them that the Community Director would contact them shortly regarding student conduct. RA Jones confirmed all details with Officers Katz and Green. The Public Safety Incident Case Report (ICR) is 17-00011. The incident concluded at 10:46 PM.

**Example 2: Marijuana Odor Report**

On Friday, October 3, 2025, at 9:10 PM, RA Jisoo Kim received a call from Rose Park, a resident of FRH 8816, reporting a strong smell of marijuana on the 8th floor. RA Kim promptly notified Public Safety at 9:15 PM and met Officer Swift on the 8th floor of Flynn for a walkthrough. Neither RA Kim nor Officer Swift detected the odor described by Resident Park. They proceeded to the 9th floor to investigate further but again did not detect the smell. Officer Swift advised RA Kim to instruct Resident Park to report immediately if the odor was detected again.

**Sources:**
- `Resource_ Example of Well-Written Incident Report_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### Incident Response & Documentation
When responding to an incident, walk through the entire interaction from beginning to end. As you do this, ensure you:

- Walk through the steps of the whole interaction beginning to end.
- Identify what, if any, policy issues need to be addressed.
- Consider what factors may be contributing to risk or harm to students.
- Determine whether you need to call anyone (e.g., supervisor, on-call staff, emergency services), based on the situation.

Common Incident Scenario to Address:

Your event is happening in the floor lounge. As the event is wrapping up, you see one of your residents coming down the hallway. They look like they might have fallen, as they have a large rip in their pant leg and what looks like mud all down their back. They seem to be having some difficulty, and you also see that they’re holding a bottle in their hand. Before you have a chance to speak to them, they enter room 113.

Use the guiding questions above to analyze and respond to this scenario:
- Walk through what you would do from the moment you notice the resident until the situation is resolved.
- Identify any policy issues that may be present.
- Assess what factors may be contributing to risk or harm to the student or others.
- Decide whether you need to call anyone, and if so, whom and at what point.

**Sources:**
- `Enforcing Policies and Following Procedures.pdf`

### LLV & Noise Complaints
Response to LLV (Likely Liquor Violation) or Noise Complaints in Residence Halls

When a likely liquor violation (LLV) or noise complaint occurs in a residence hall, the standard response process is as follows:

1. **Initial Notification**
   - You will call our dispatch and notify them of the situation in the residence hall.
   - Dispatch will always send at least two Officers to the room.

2. **Arrival and Initial Contact**
   - Upon arrival, Officers will speak with you outside of the room to understand what is going on.
   - Based on the situation, Officers will either knock on the door or key into the room.

3. **In-Room Procedures (When Alcohol Is Involved)**
   - Officers will determine who lives in the room.
   - Officers will collect IDs from the individuals present.
   - Officers will determine which alcohol in the room belongs to which individual.
   - Officers will use a PBT (Preliminary Breath Test) to determine alcohol levels.
   - Officers will conduct a Plainview search of the room.
   - Officers will determine who can remain on their own and who needs to come back to our office.
   - Officers will dump out alcohol.
   - Officers will send non-residents and/or guests back to their rooms as appropriate.

4. **Documentation and Information Exchange**
   - Officers will document the incident in a report.
   - Officers will gather your name, ID number, and title for the report.
   - Officers will provide you with their names, badge numbers, and the report number.

5. **Noise-Only Situations**
   - In some noise complaints, there may be loud noise but no alcohol present.
   - In these cases, Officers may address the noise but will not always document the incident in a report.

6. **Follow-Up with Residence Life Staff**
   - After handling the situation, Officers will follow up with the Hall Director Duty phone to inform them of what happened.

**Sources:**
- `2025 Res Life Presentation.pdf`

### Marijuana/Drugs Odor Reports
On Friday, October 3rd, 2025, at 9:10 PM, Resident Advisor Jisoo Kim received a call from Rose Park, a resident of FRH 8816. Rose Park reported a strong smell of marijuana on the 8th floor of the new FRH 8816. At 9:15 PM, RA Kim contacted Public Safety to report the odor. RA Kim then met with Public Safety Officer Swift on the 8th floor of Flynn for a walkthrough. During their inspection, neither Officer Swift nor RA Kim detected the strong odor described by Resident Park. They proceeded to the 9th floor to check for the odor there but were again unable to detect it. Officer Swift advised RA Kim to inform Resident Park to contact the RA on duty if the smell reoccurred. RA Kim communicated this to Resident Park at 9:30 PM, and the incident concluded at 9:32 PM.

### Mental Health & Wellness Incidents
You are in your room between rounds on a quiet night. Many of your residents have gone home for the weekend. You hear someone sobbing in the hallway.

1. **Initial Response and Safety Check (Beginning of Interaction)**  
   - Exit your room and walk toward the sound of the sobbing.  
   - Observe the situation as you approach:  
     - The person is in the hallway, sobbing uncontrollably.  
     - They are familiar to you, but they are not one of your residents.  
   - Before engaging, quickly assess for any immediate safety concerns (e.g., visible injuries, signs of intoxication, self-harm, or another person involved).  
   - If there is an immediate threat to life or serious injury, follow emergency protocols and contact the appropriate emergency responders right away.

2. **Engaging the Student**  
   - Calmly introduce yourself (if needed) and acknowledge what you are seeing (e.g., that they appear very upset).  
   - Ask if they are physically safe and if they need any immediate medical attention.  
   - Invite them to move to a more private, safe, and quiet location if appropriate and if they are willing (e.g., a nearby lounge, office, or other suitable space), while ensuring you remain in a public or semi-public area consistent with safety and professional boundaries.  
   - Use active listening and supportive, nonjudgmental language. Allow them to share as much or as little as they are comfortable with.

3. **Assessing Risk and Harm**  
   Consider factors that may be contributing to risk or harm to the student or others, including but not limited to:  
   - Expressions of self-harm or suicidal thoughts.  
   - Expressions of wanting to harm someone else.  
   - Signs of severe emotional distress (e.g., sobbing uncontrollably, inability to calm down, disorientation).  
   - Possible substance use or intoxication.  
   - Recent conflicts, relationship issues, or incidents in the building.  
   - Being alone in the building or on the floor late at night.  
   - The fact that many residents are away, which may reduce available peer support.  
   - Any mention of harassment, assault, or other policy violations.  
   - Any indication that the student does not feel safe returning to their room or current environment.

4. **Policy Considerations**  
   As you talk with the student, identify any potential policy issues that may need to be addressed, such as:  
   - Alcohol or drug use in violation of residence or institutional policies.  
   - Physical altercations, threats, or harassment.  
   - Sexual misconduct or assault.  
   - Safety and security concerns in the building (e.g., propped doors, unauthorized guests).  
   - Noise or disruption if the incident escalates or affects others.  
   - Any other behavior that appears to violate residence life or institutional policies.  
   If any policy issues are disclosed or observed, follow the appropriate reporting and documentation procedures as required by residence life and institutional guidelines.

5. **Determining Who to Call**  
   Based on your assessment of the situation, determine whether you need to contact anyone and who that should be. Consider:  
   - **Emergency services** (e.g., campus safety, police, or medical responders) if there is an immediate risk of harm to the student or others, or if medical attention is needed.  
   - **On-call professional staff or supervisor** if the situation involves significant emotional distress, potential self-harm, harm to others, or serious policy violations.  
   - **Counseling or mental health services** if the student expresses emotional distress, mental health concerns, or asks for professional support.  
   - **Other appropriate campus resources** as required by your institution’s protocols.  
   Always follow your residence life chain-of-command and on-call procedures when deciding whom to contact.

6. **Closing the Interaction (End of Interaction)**  
   - Once immediate safety is addressed and any necessary calls have been made, help the student identify next steps (e.g., speaking with a counselor, connecting with a friend or support person, returning safely to their room, or waiting for professional staff).  
   - Ensure the student is not left alone if they are at risk of harming themselves or others, following institutional guidelines on supervision and safety.  
   - Provide information on relevant campus resources as appropriate.  
   - After the situation is stabilized and the student is safe or in the care of appropriate professionals, complete any required documentation or incident reports, including:  
     - A factual description of what you observed (e.g., sobbing uncontrollably in the hallway, familiar but not your resident).  
     - The steps you took from beginning to end of the interaction.  
     - Any policy issues identified.  
     - Any referrals or calls made and to whom.  

This scenario is an example of a **common incident** that may occur on a quiet night when many residents are away. Staff should be prepared to:  
- Walk through the entire interaction from beginning to end.  
- Identify and address any relevant policy issues.  
- Recognize factors that may contribute to risk or harm to students.  
- Decide whether and whom to call, following established residence life procedures.

**Sources:**
- `Enforcing Policies and Following Procedures.pdf`

### Reporting Guidelines
If you are ever unsure whether a situation needs to be reported, err on the side of reporting. When in doubt, consult with your supervisor or the appropriate professional staff member to discuss the situation and determine whether a formal report is required. Do not delay seeking guidance; timely consultation helps ensure that any necessary follow-up or support can occur as quickly as possible.

### Reporting Systems & Documentation
Advocate is an online system utilized by the university, primarily by Residence Life and the Dean of Students, to address, track, and process behavioral concerns among students. As a Resident Assistant (RA), you will interact with two key forms within Advocate:

1. **Code of Conduct**: This form is used to submit an incident report whenever you become aware of a policy violation. Violations can include, but are not limited to, vandalism, substance-related issues, and physical violence. It is essential to submit a report whenever Public Safety is involved.

2. **General Concern/FYI**: This form is for situations where a student is in distress or if you have information about a student in crisis. It serves as a communication tool for any uncertainties or general concerns you might have about a student's well-being.

In addition to Advocate, the university also uses Roompact, which is designed to track student engagement, campus connections, and roommate relationships. The two primary forms in Roompact are the Ongoing Resident Notes form and the Roommate Conflict Tracking form. These tools help you and your supervisor monitor the general well-being of students. However, if a situation involves significant behavioral concerns, it should be reported through Advocate. When in doubt, opt to submit through Advocate to ensure all serious matters are appropriately addressed.

**Sources:**
- `IR Writing.pdf`
- `Resource_ Advocate vs. Roompact_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### Responding to / Addressing Incidents
To effectively address an incident, begin by ensuring you are wearing your name tag and Residence Life/St. Thomas apparel. Approach the door and knock loudly and clearly. If nearby residents mistakenly open their doors, it indicates your knock was appropriately audible. When the resident answers the door, introduce yourself as a Resident Assistant (RA). Clearly identify the specific behavior that drew your attention and explain why it is problematic, referencing the relevant policy. If you encounter a situation involving rooms within rooms, remain straightforward and maintain a calm demeanor throughout the interaction.

### Responding to Disclosures / Resident Support
Supporting a Resident Who Discloses

When a resident discloses an experience to you:

1. **How to Respond in the Moment**
   - Believe them.
   - Listen and let them tell their story in their own words.
   - Do not try to investigate or fact-find; contain your curiosity.
   - Allow them to stay in control of the conversation and next steps as much as you can.
   - Thank them for sharing their experience with you.

2. **Confidentiality and Reporting**
   - Remember your obligation to report.
   - Keep in mind that your resident chose to report their experience to you; honor that choice.
   - Keep this information private and only share it as required by your reporting obligations.

3. **Your Support System**
   - Community Directors (CD) and Graduate Staff Assistants (GSA) can support you as you respond and follow reporting requirements.

4. **Providing Options and Referrals**
   - Provide the resident with options and allow them to choose who else or where else they want to go for support.
   - Possible supports include:
     - CAPS
     - Roommate
     - Friends
     - Family
     - Hospital
     - Public Safety
     - Police  
     It is up to the resident to decide.
   - Your role is to listen, support, and refer. There are lots of resources available.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

How to Address an Incident

- Maintain a level head throughout the situation.  
- Ask for help when needed. Possible resources include:  
  - Your fellow RA on duty  
  - Public Safety  
  - CD/GSA on duty

### Responding to Reports on Rounds
During your initial set of rounds, a resident approaches you to report a recurring strange smell emanating from the end of their hallway. Although the resident cannot pinpoint the exact room, they emphasize the regularity of the odor. Follow these steps to address the situation:

1. **Acknowledge the Report**: Thank the resident for bringing the issue to your attention and assure them that you will investigate the matter promptly.

2. **Investigate the Source**: Proceed to the area in question and conduct a thorough walkthrough to identify the source of the smell. Pay attention to any unusual odors or signs that could indicate a potential problem.

3. **Assess for Policy Violations**: Consider whether the smell could be related to any policy violations, such as prohibited substances or activities. Familiarize yourself with relevant residence life policies to determine if any rules are being breached.

4. **Evaluate Risk Factors**: Assess any potential risks or harm to students, such as health hazards from chemical smells, smoke, or other dangerous substances. Consider the impact on resident safety and well-being.

5. **Document the Incident**: Record the details of the interaction, including the resident's report, your observations, and any actions taken. This documentation is crucial for follow-up and accountability.

6. **Determine Need for Assistance**: Decide if the situation requires additional support. If the smell suggests a serious issue, such as a gas leak or fire hazard, contact emergency services immediately. For less urgent matters, consult with your supervisor or maintenance staff for further investigation.

7. **Follow Up with the Resident**: After addressing the issue, update the resident on the steps taken and any resolutions. Ensure they feel heard and informed about the outcome.

By adhering to these steps, you ensure a thorough and effective response to the resident's concern while maintaining a safe and compliant living environment.

**Sources:**
- `Enforcing Policies and Following Procedures.pdf`

---

### Response & Documentation Procedures
Walkthrough and Response Guide for Common Incidents

When responding to a student incident, staff should systematically review the entire interaction from beginning to end. Use the following prompts to guide your assessment and response:

1. Step-by-step interaction review  
   - Walk through the steps of the whole interaction beginning to end.  
   - Identify what happened first, how the situation developed, and how it concluded.  
   - Note any changes in student behavior, environment, or level of concern throughout the interaction.

2. Policy considerations  
   - Determine what, if any, policy issues need to be addressed.  
   - Identify any specific policies that may have been violated or are relevant to the situation.  
   - Consider whether documentation or follow-up related to policy is required.

3. Risk and harm assessment  
   - Consider what factors may be contributing to risk or harm to students.  
   - Look at individual, interpersonal, environmental, and situational factors that might increase risk.  
   - Note any immediate safety concerns or potential for escalation.

4. Communication and referral  
   - Decide who you would call in response to the situation (e.g., supervisor, on-call staff, campus partners, emergency services, as appropriate to the scenario).  
   - Clarify the order in which you would contact people and what information you would share with each.

5. Large group share-out and reflection  
   - In a large group share out, discuss:  
     - What are some unique factors for your scenario type?  
     - How those unique factors influenced your decisions and actions.  
   - Use this discussion to compare approaches across different common incidents and to identify patterns, challenges, and best practices.

6. Application to common incidents  
   - Apply the above steps to common incidents that need to be addressed in residence life (e.g., typical policy violations, wellness concerns, conflicts, or safety-related situations).  
   - Ensure each common incident type is reviewed using the same structure: full interaction walkthrough, policy issues, risk/harm factors, and who you would call, followed by a large group share out of unique scenario factors.

**Sources:**
- `Enforcing Policies and Following Procedures.pdf`

### Response Procedures
**Residence Life Operations: Response Procedures**

**THC or Other Drugs:**
- Refer to the Code of Conduct and the Resident Student Handbook for policies on drug use.
- If you suspect the use of THC or other drugs, immediately contact Public Safety.
- Public Safety will assess the residence to determine the presence of illegal substances.
- Follow all instructions provided by Public Safety and never handle illegal substances.
- Document the incident in Advocate.
- Notify the CD/GSA on duty if the St. Paul Police Department (SPPD) is involved.

**General Illness / Medical Concerns:**
- Residence Life student leaders must not provide transportation for injured or ill residents, regardless of the situation's severity.
- Do not offer medical care, including bandages or over-the-counter medications, as RAs are not trained or protected for handling bodily fluids or medical care.
- Students are responsible for their own medical care or should contact Public Safety.
- Public Safety can offer minimal medical assessment and contact Saint Paul Emergency Response for on-campus assistance.

**Medical Emergencies:**
- In cases where a student is unresponsive, do not move or shake the student.
- Call Public Safety’s Emergency line at 651-962-5555 for immediate assistance.
- Public Safety will determine the next steps.
- Notify the CD/GSA on duty and document the situation in Advocate.

**Physical Assault:**
- Under no circumstances should student leaders or Residence Life team members physically intervene in a fight.
- Prioritize your safety and remember that anyone has the potential for aggression, regardless of gender or situation.
- Reassure victims that they have control over decisions in the process.
- If a physical fight occurs, call Public Safety and request an immediate officer response.
- Inform the CD/GSA of the situation and document it in Advocate.

**Sources:**
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`

### Sexual Misconduct & Title IX Reporting Procedures
When a Resident Assistant (RA) receives a report of sexual misconduct, the following procedure must be followed:

1. **Initial Contact and Reporting**
   - The RA immediately calls the on-duty Community Director (CD) or Graduate Staff Assistant (GSA) for support and for reporting purposes.
   - The RA should **not** submit an Incident Report, in order to limit the number of people who are aware of the situation.

2. **Immediate Response to Reporting Party**
   - The CD/GSA responds in the moment to the reporting party.
   - The CD/GSA offers support and provides information about available resources.
   - The reporting party may or may not be available for a conversation with the CD/GSA, and may choose not to speak with them.

3. **Completion of the Title IX Report Form**
   - The CD/GSA completes the Title IX Report Form.
   - Alternatively, the CD/GSA may receive the information from the RA and complete the Title IX Report Form together with the RA.

4. **Follow-Up Communication with Reporting Party**
   - The CD/GSA sends an email to the reporting party outlining their options and available support.

5. **Internal Notification**
   - The CD/GSA informs the Associate Director and Director that a report has been submitted.
   - This notification does **not** include names or detailed information; it is only to inform them that a report has been filed.

6. **Title IX Coordinator Outreach**
   - The Title IX Coordinator receives the Title IX Report and begins outreach within one business day (typically within 24 hours).
   - For a visual overview of this process, refer to the “Responding to a Report of Sexual Misconduct” flow chart slide.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

### Title IX Reporting
When an incident occurs in your hall or is impacting your community, you must:

1. Immediately notify:
   - The on-duty professional staff member if the incident occurs after hours, **or**
   - Your staff supervisor if the incident occurs during the workday.

2. After immediate action has been taken, work together with the on-duty professional staff member or your staff supervisor to plan and submit a Title IX Report.

Title IX reports are submitted through the “Where to Report” page at: https://www.stthomas.edu/title-ix/

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

### Vandalism & Bias/Hate Incidents
While conducting a routine hallway check, you observe that a bulletin board belonging to one of your peers has been vandalized, including the presence of a hate symbol. To address this situation, follow these steps:

1. **Document the Incident**: Take clear photographs of the vandalism, ensuring that the hate symbol and any other damage are clearly visible. This documentation is crucial for reporting and future reference.

2. **Report Immediately**: Contact your supervisor or the appropriate authority within the residence life team to report the incident. Provide them with the photographs and any other relevant details about the situation.

3. **Secure the Area**: If possible, cover the vandalized section of the bulletin board to prevent further exposure to the hate symbol. Ensure that this action does not interfere with the documentation process.

4. **Initiate a Response**: Work with your supervisor to determine the next steps, which may include notifying campus security or other relevant departments to investigate the incident further.

5. **Support Affected Individuals**: Reach out to the peer whose bulletin board was vandalized to offer support and inform them of the steps being taken to address the situation.

6. **Follow Up**: Stay informed about the progress of the investigation and any actions taken. Keep the affected peer updated as appropriate.

By following these steps, you ensure a prompt and effective response to the vandalism incident, maintaining a safe and respectful environment within the residence community.

**Sources:**
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`
- `Duty Expectations.pdf`

**Check-In Procedures:**

- RAs in both FY and SY Communities will assist in the residence halls, with schedules organized by the Community Director (CD) or Graduate Student Assistant (GSA).

**Check-In Locations:**
- Flynn Turnaround for Dowling, Brady, Flynn, and Morrison Halls
- Schoenecker North Garage for Ireland and Schoenecker Halls
- Frey Desk for Frey Hall (unstaffed)
- Murray Loading Dock for Murray Hall
- 51 Grand Lounge for Mid-Campus
- Grace Desk for South Campus

**Additional Information:**
- On Saturday, desks will operate with regular hours from 8:00 pm to 1:00 am.
- Regular RA duty will commence on Saturday from 4:30 pm to 8:00 am.
- Academic year meal plans will begin with brunch on Sunday.

## Policies
### Alcohol Policy
Residents must adhere to specific guidelines regarding alcohol possession and consumption in campus housing. Students under 21 are prohibited from possessing or consuming alcohol on campus, in line with the Student Policy Book and Federal and State Laws. Consequently, no parties or activities with alcohol are permitted, nor can alcohol be stored in rooms where all residents are underage.

In mixed-age accommodations, where at least one resident is 21 or older and at least one is underage, those of legal drinking age may possess alcohol. However, they must not supply alcohol to their underage roommates. Any questions about possession, ownership, or distribution will be documented and managed through the student conduct process. It is recommended that residents of legal drinking age store alcohol in their personal bedrooms, preferably in individual mini refrigerators if available.

When alcohol is present, only guests who are of legal drinking age are permitted in the room or apartment. The presence of large volumes of alcohol, such as kegs, party balls, multiple cases of beer, wine, or multiple bottles of hard liquor, is strictly prohibited in the Residence Halls.

**Sources:**
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`

### Event & Programming Policies
Event Policy: Gift Card & Prize Guidelines

With the approval of the Community Director (CD) or Graduate Student Assistant (GSA), prizes and gift cards can be purchased for the Campus Stores/Tommie Shop or as eXpress dollars. It is important to note that University funds are not permitted for the purchase of off-campus gift cards, such as those for Target, Amazon, or Starbucks.

When issuing a gift card, the following information must be provided: 
- The student's first and last name
- The student's University of St. Thomas (UST) email address
- The student's ID number
- A description or reason for the award
- The gift card amount
- Whether the card is for Campus Stores or eXpress dollars

The CD or GSA is responsible for emailing the required information to accountspay@stthomas.edu or delivering it to Accounts Payable in AQU 302. Below is an example of how to format the gift card winner information for Campus Life and Accounts Payable:

Example:
- Name: Tommie Mascot
- Email: tomm1234@stthomas.edu
- ID Number: 11223344
- Description: Club Event Gift Card Winner
- Amount: $20.00
- Card Type: eXpress dollars

This process ensures that all necessary details are documented and communicated effectively for the issuance of gift cards and prizes.

**Sources:**

### Event Policy: Marketing & Publicity

#### 1. Accessibility Requirements

All event publicity MUST include the following accessibility statement:

> To make a disability accommodation request, please contact the event coordinator [insert Community/Hall Director name] at [insert Community/Hall Director email address] at least one week prior to the event date.

This exact information must be included:

- On printed posters.  
- In TommieLink Events (preferably in the text of the event description rather than only on the flyer image).  
- With event information posted in GroupMe or other digital spaces **if** you are sharing a digital flyer and the accessibility statement is not listed on the image.

You do **not** need to add the accessibility statement separately if you are sharing a link to the event on TommieLink, because the statement is already listed there.

### Events & Programming
Event Policy: Food & Beverage at On-Campus Events

All food served at on-campus events must be sourced from St. Thomas Catering or Summit Marketplace. It is mandatory for Resident Assistants (RAs) to obtain approval from their Community Director (CD) or Graduate Student Assistant (GSA) before ordering or purchasing any food and beverages. The use of off-campus food providers is strictly prohibited.

RAs can access the Catering Menus by visiting the Dining Services Catering page at [Catering Services](https://one.stthomas.edu/sites/business-affairs/food-and-dining-services/SitePageModern/59797/catering-services). For event planning, RAs are encouraged to utilize the Student Club Menu. Catering orders must be placed at least 10 days in advance of the event. If there is a need for an item not listed on the catering menu, the CD or GSA can contact Dining Services via email to inquire about the possibility of accommodating the request.

For order pick-up, proceed to the NetCatering pick-up area located on the third floor of the Anderson Student Center (ASC) approximately 20 minutes before your event or meeting. To find the pick-up area, walk down the hallway to the right of the elevators and turn right at the end of the hall. Your order should be ready for collection in the hallway. If the order is not available at the designated pick-up time, you may ring the doorbell to notify the staff of your presence. The NetCatering pick-up area is situated down the ASC third-floor hallway on the right side of the elevators.

**Sources:**
- `Event Policy_ Food_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### General Policy Overview
Community Harmony, Safety, Academic Success, and Social Living are the core principles guiding our residence life policies. These policies are designed to ensure that all residents feel safe, have the opportunity for academic success, and can live harmoniously within a community. Understanding what a policy is and why it exists is crucial. A policy is a set of guidelines or rules established to maintain order and support these core principles. It provides a framework for behavior and decision-making that helps prevent chaos, akin to the themes explored in "Lord of the Flies," where the absence of structure leads to disorder. By adhering to these policies, residents contribute to a supportive and thriving community environment.

**Sources:**
- `Enforcing Policies and Following Procedures.pdf`

### Nights Away / Time Away from Campus
Student Leaders seeking nights away from campus must follow a process determined by their Community Director (CD) or Graduate Student Assistant (GSA). All nights away must be requested and approved in advance. A night away is defined as being outside the Residence Hall or Residential Building past 2:30 am. Each Student Leader is permitted to request up to six nights away per semester, including the J-Term.

During the Fall and Spring semesters, minimal nights away will be approved within the first six weeks due to required events such as homecoming weekend and family weekend. Similarly, during the J-Term, Student Leaders may also request up to six nights away. However, they must ensure that duty responsibilities are covered, and they should be aware that nights away might affect their J-Term meal plan.

**Sources:**
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`

### Nondiscrimination & Anti-Harassment Policy
Nondiscrimination & Anti-Harassment Policy  

This policy applies to all members of the St. Thomas community and prohibits discrimination, bias harassment, hate crimes, and other unlawful conduct based on protected characteristics.

Under this policy, prohibited conduct includes:

1. **Unwelcome conduct related to a protected characteristic**  
   - Conduct that is unwelcome and connected to an individual’s protected characteristic.

2. **Unfavorable or unfair treatment because of a protected characteristic**  
   - Decisions, actions, or treatment that are unfavorable or unfair and are taken because of an individual’s protected characteristic.

3. **Negative impact on participation in employment or education**  
   - The unwelcome conduct and/or unfavorable or unfair treatment must have a negative impact and/or interfere with a person’s ability to participate in or benefit from employment or education at St. Thomas.

When these conditions are met, the conduct may constitute discrimination, bias harassment, or a hate crime under the Nondiscrimination & Anti-Harassment Policy.

**Protected characteristics** include:  
- race  
- color  
- creed  
- religion  
- national origin  
- sex  
- sexual orientation  
- gender identity or expression  
- familial status  
- disability  
- age  
- marital status  
- status regarding public assistance  
- veteran status  

The Nondiscrimination & Anti-Harassment Policy covers prohibitive and unlawful conduct that falls under the following categories:  
- Hate Crime  
- Bias Harassment  
- Discrimination

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

### Outdoor Events & Space Use
**Outdoor Event Policy**

**Reservations Required:**  
All outdoor spaces on campus, such as the ICF Amphitheater, upper quad, lower quad, and athletic fields, must be reserved through the CD/GSA.

**Serving Food:**  
If your outdoor event includes serving food, it must be done under a tent. Tents can be reserved by the CD/GSA at the time of space reservation.

**Inclement Weather Planning:**  
For events planned outdoors, it is mandatory to reserve an indoor space as a backup in case of poor weather. Notification to Campus Facilities is required if you need to cancel or move your event indoors. The deadlines for notifying are as follows:
- Morning events (7:00am - 11:00am): Notify by 4:00pm the day before.
- Afternoon events (12:00pm - 3:00pm): Notify by 9:00am the day of the event.
- Evening events (4:00pm - 10:00pm): Notify by 12:00pm the day of the event.

For assistance, contact Facilities Management at facilities@stthomas.edu or (651) 962-6530.

**Outdoor Event Amplified Sound Policy:**  
To host an event or musical performance outdoors with amplified sound, a request must be submitted in 25Live at least two weeks in advance. The event must comply with St. Thomas music policy and City of St. Paul codes to ensure sound does not disrupt nearby activities and residences. Permitted locations for such events include the Upper Quad and ICF Amphitheater.

**Sources:**
- `Event Policy_ Outdoor Events_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`
