### Programming & Events
To host an event featuring a movie or documentary, you must obtain approval from the Associate Director of Residence Life, Erica Thompson, at least two weeks prior to the event. You can contact her via email at ekthompson@stthomas.edu. If you are considering organizing such an event, it is advisable to consult with your Community Director (CD) or Graduate Student Assistant (GSA).

A public performance license is required to show any movie or documentary at an event. Licenses for most major films can be purchased through Swank Motion Pictures. It is important to note that you cannot use your personal copy or streaming service for the event, as these licenses are restricted to personal use only. Similarly, resources from the St. Thomas film library also require a public performance license, as their viewing licenses are intended solely for personal use.

Streaming a film over Zoom is strictly prohibited without the appropriate licenses, as it is considered illegal. However, you may use services like Teleparty to watch TV shows or movies online with others. Teleparty synchronizes video playback and includes a group chat feature for platforms such as Netflix, Disney+, Hulu, HBO Max, and Amazon Prime. This method remains legal because each participant must log into their personal account to join the viewing.

**Sources:**
- `Event Policy_ Movies & Documentaries_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### Sexual Misconduct Policy
Sexual Misconduct Policy – Prohibited Conduct

The Sexual Misconduct Policy prohibits the following types of conduct. All definitions and examples below are illustrative and not exhaustive.

1. Harassment  
   - Includes quid pro quo behavior (e.g., implying that academic, employment, or housing benefits are conditioned on sexual conduct).  
   - Includes unwelcome sexual comments or jokes.  
   - Includes mis-gendering.  
   - Includes creating or contributing to a hostile work environment through sexual or sex-based conduct.

2. Sexual Assault  
   - Non-consensual or forced touching.  
   - Non-consensual or forced kissing.  
   - Non-consensual or forced penetration.

3. Sexual Exploitation  
   - A catch-all category that includes:  
     - Recording sexual content without consent.  
     - Sharing sexual content without consent.  
     - Watching a sexual act without consent.  
     - Exposing oneself or streaking without consent.

4. Stalking  
   - Repeated and unwanted communication.  
   - Repeated and unwanted gift-giving.  
   - Repeated and unwanted following of someone.  
   - These behaviors must cause emotional distress to the person targeted.

5. Dating Violence and Domestic Violence  
   - Stalking, harassment, or assault that occurs within a domestic relationship.  
   - Stalking, harassment, or assault that occurs within a dating relationship.

6. Sex Discrimination  
   - Discrimination based on sex stereotypes.  
   - Discrimination based on gender identity.  
   - Discrimination based on sexual orientation.  
   - Discrimination based on sex characteristics.  
   - Discrimination based on pregnancy.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Sexual Misconduct, Nondiscrimination, and Anti‑harassment (RA Responsibilities & Resources)
**Sexual Misconduct, Nondiscrimination, and Anti‑harassment: Resident Advisor Responsibilities and Resources**

This section outlines the key areas of knowledge and practice you are expected to understand and apply as a Resident Advisor when dealing with misconduct that is sexual in nature.

**1. Policy Knowledge**

- You must be familiar with the **Sexual Misconduct Policy**.  
- You must be familiar with the **Nondiscrimination and Anti-harassment Policy**.  

These policies guide how you recognize, respond to, and report incidents of sexual misconduct, discrimination, and harassment.

**2. Your Responsibilities as a Resident Advisor**

- Understand **your responsibilities as a Resident Advisor when dealing with misconduct that is sexual in nature**.  
- Apply the Sexual Misconduct Policy and the Nondiscrimination and Anti-harassment Policy when responding to such incidents.  
- Ensure that your actions align with institutional expectations and requirements under these policies.

**3. Supporting and Responding to Residents**

- Know **how to support and respond to residents when reports of sexual misconduct are made**.  
- Respond in a way that is informed by the Sexual Misconduct Policy and the Nondiscrimination and Anti-harassment Policy.  
- Provide appropriate support, information, and next steps to residents who disclose or report sexual misconduct.

**4. Creating a Safe Community**

- Use **tools to create a safe community through bystander intervention**.  
- Engage in and utilize **role-playing / scenarios** and **role-playing / scenarios** (including **role -playing / scenarios**) to practice and strengthen your bystander intervention skills.  
- Apply these tools proactively to help prevent sexual misconduct and harassment in your community.

**5. Resources and Referrals**

- Be aware of **available resource and referral to help myself and my residents**.  
- Use **available resource and referral to help myself and my residents** when responding to incidents or supporting residents affected by sexual misconduct, discrimination, or harassment.  

**6. Reflection: “This is a lot – what have we missed?”**

- Recognize that **this is a lot – what have we missed?** is an ongoing reflective question to ensure you are considering:
  - Policy knowledge of the Sexual Misconduct Policy.  
  - Policy knowledge of the Nondiscrimination and Anti-harassment Policy.  
  - Your responsibilities as a Resident Advisor when dealing with misconduct that is sexual in nature.  
  - How to support and respond to residents when reports of sexual misconduct are made.  
  - Tools to create a safe community through bystander intervention and role-playing / scenarios.  
  - Available resource and referral to help myself and my residents.  

Use this question to continually check your understanding and identify any gaps in your knowledge or practice related to these areas.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

### Sexual Misconduct, Title IX, and Nondiscrimination
**Policies: Sexual Misconduct (Title IX) and Nondiscrimination and Anti-Harassment**

The institution maintains two closely related policies that govern expectations for conduct, reporting, and response to concerns involving discrimination, harassment, and sexual misconduct:

1. **Sexual Misconduct (Title IX) Policy**  
   This policy addresses conduct that falls under Title IX, including sexual harassment, sexual assault, dating violence, domestic violence, stalking, and other forms of sex- or gender-based misconduct as defined by federal regulations and institutional standards. It outlines:
   - Prohibited behaviors and definitions of sexual misconduct under Title IX.
   - Rights and responsibilities of students, staff, and faculty.
   - Reporting options, including confidential and non-confidential resources.
   - Procedures for intake, assessment, investigation, and resolution of Title IX complaints.
   - Supportive measures and interim actions that may be provided to involved parties.
   - Protections against retaliation for making a report or participating in a process.

2. **Nondiscrimination and Anti-Harassment Policy**  
   This policy prohibits discrimination and harassment based on protected characteristics (such as race, color, national origin, religion, sex, gender identity or expression, sexual orientation, disability, age, and any other status protected by law or institutional policy). It includes:
   - Definitions of discrimination, harassment, and hostile environment.
   - Examples of prohibited conduct that may or may not fall under Title IX but still violate institutional standards.
   - Procedures for reporting concerns of discrimination or harassment.
   - Processes for review, investigation, and resolution of complaints under this policy.
   - Protections against retaliation for reporting or participating in any related process.

**Relationship Between the Two Policies**  
- Some conduct may be addressed under both the Sexual Misconduct (Title IX) Policy and the Nondiscrimination and Anti-Harassment Policy, depending on the nature of the behavior and applicable laws.  
- When a report is received, the institution will determine which policy or policies apply and will follow the corresponding procedures.  
- Both policies are designed to promote a safe, inclusive, and equitable environment and to ensure that concerns are taken seriously, responded to promptly, and resolved through fair and consistent processes.

Residence life staff should be familiar with both policies, understand how to direct individuals to appropriate reporting channels and resources, and know when to consult with the Title IX office or the office responsible for nondiscrimination and anti-harassment matters.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Student Conduct
St. Thomas Sexual Misconduct Policy (Including Title IX)

The St. Thomas Sexual Misconduct Policy, which includes Title IX, defines prohibited conduct and outlines protections, responsibilities, and resources for the university community.

**Prohibited Conduct**

The policy defines and prohibits the following forms of conduct:  
- Sexual assault  
- Sexual harassment  
- Stalking  
- Dating violence  
- Domestic violence  
- Sexual exploitation  
- Sex discrimination  

These behaviors are prohibited whether they occur on campus or off campus.

**Individuals Covered by the Policy**

The policy protects and addresses concerns involving:  
- Employees  
- Students  
- Visitors  
- Guests  
- Volunteers  

**Scope of Behavior (On and Off Campus)**

The policy applies to behaviors that occur both on campus and off campus. Conduct falling under this policy will be addressed regardless of where it occurs if it affects members of the St. Thomas community or university programs or activities.

**Support Resources**

The policy provides for support resources both on and off campus. Individuals affected by conduct covered under this policy can access assistance, referrals, and support services through university and community resources.

**Reporting Obligations**

All employees, including Resident Assistants (RAs), have required reporting obligations under this policy. Employees who become aware of potential violations must follow established reporting procedures.

**Non-Retaliation Protection**

Individuals who report concerns or incidents in good faith are protected from retaliation. The policy prohibits retaliatory actions against anyone who makes a good-faith report, participates in an investigation, or supports someone involved in a report.

**Amnesty Related to Drug and Alcohol Policies**

To remove barriers to reporting, the policy provides amnesty related to drug and alcohol policies for individuals who report incidents covered by this policy in good faith. This amnesty is intended to encourage reporting and access to support without fear of disciplinary action for personal alcohol or drug use revealed in the course of a report.

**Academic Freedom**

Academic freedom is protected for teaching faculty. Faculty may teach and discuss content that is sexual in nature, including material that may fall under Title IX, as part of legitimate academic instruction. However, faculty may not act or behave in ways that themselves fall under the prohibited conduct defined in this policy.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

### Student Staff Agreements & Expectations
**2025 – 2026 Student Leader Agreement**

As a Student Leader, I am committed to fulfilling my responsibilities as outlined in the agreement and university policies. Below are the key expectations and guidelines:

**J-Term and Breaks:**
- During J-Term, I must seek approval from my Community/Hall Director for any time away, including study abroad, VIA/VISION trips, or family trips.
- I am required to be present for residence hall openings, closings, and safety checks. Halls remain open during J-Term and all breaks, including Thanksgiving, Christmas, Spring, and Easter. I must complete room safety checks, assist in closing the building, and provide duty coverage as needed.
- Duty coverage during breaks is from 8:00 pm to 8:00 am, totaling 12 hours. Travel arrangements for J-Term, breaks, or the end of the academic year must be discussed with my Community/Hall Director beforehand.

**Resident Advisor Responsibilities:**
- My weekly hours may vary, averaging 20-30 hours, excluding inactive "on-duty" time. If responsibilities exceed 30 hours for two consecutive weeks, I will notify my Community/Hall Director.
- In cases of illness or conditions covered under the University's Sick and Safe Time Leave Policy, I will inform my Community/Hall Director promptly. Using sick and safe time does not affect my stipend.

**General Policies and Guidelines:**
- I will familiarize myself with University policies, the Resident Student Handbook, and the Residence Life Student Leader Manual. Violations may lead to disciplinary action, including dismissal.
- I will maintain a University of St. Thomas email account and Outlook calendar, checking my email multiple times daily.
- A background check is required for access to residence hall rooms and keys.
- I will exemplify positive behavior in personal, academic, and social contexts, both on and off campus, including on social media.

**Alcohol Expectations:**
- I will adhere to all alcohol policies set by the University and Residence Life. Violations may result in probation or dismissal.
- If under the legal drinking age, I will not consume alcohol and will remove myself from situations where alcohol is present.
- If of legal drinking age, I will consume alcohol responsibly and model appropriate behavior.
- I will remain alcohol and drug-free while on duty, working the hall desk, performing RA duties, and responding to incidents.

By adhering to these guidelines, I commit to being a positive role model and upholding the mission of the University of St. Thomas.

**Sources:**
- `RA Student Leader Agreement 2025-2026.pdf`

### Title IX & Mandatory Reporting
All University employees, including Resident Advisors (RAs), are required to report any information related to sexual misconduct to the Title IX Coordinator when they learn about something that may constitute sexual misconduct. This reporting obligation applies in nearly ALL instances and does not stop when you are “off duty”; you cannot turn off being an RA.

RAs must clearly and frequently remind residents that they are required reporters and are not confidential resources. Residents should understand that if they share information with an RA that may involve sexual misconduct, the RA is obligated to report it to the Title IX Coordinator.

Some University employees are designated as confidential and therefore do not have an obligation to report sexual misconduct. These confidential resources include CAPS, Health Services, and Clergy. RAs are not confidential and are considered required reporters.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Title IX & Non-Discrimination Policy
Title IX Basics

Title IX is a federal law established under the Education Amendments of 1972. It states:

> “No person in the United States shall, on the basis of sex, be excluded from participation in, be denied the benefits of, or be subjected to discrimination under any education program or activity receiving federal financial assistance.”

In practice, Title IX requires institutions that receive federal financial assistance to:

- Prevent sex-based discrimination.
- Eliminate existing sex-based discrimination.
- Address incidents and patterns of sex-based discrimination.
- Support individuals affected by sex-based discrimination.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Title IX & Sexual Misconduct
Title IX Basics and Institutional Responsibilities

Title IX is a federal law that prohibits discrimination on the basis of sex by any educational program or activity that receives federal funds. As a federally funded institution, St. Thomas has specific obligations under Title IX in three key areas: eliminating and preventing prohibited behaviors, addressing reported concerns, and supporting all impacted individuals.

1. Eliminate and Prevent Prohibited Behaviors  
St. Thomas must:
- Maintain a policy that explicitly prohibits gender discrimination and sexual misconduct.  
- Provide policy education so that students and employees understand what constitutes gender discrimination and sexual misconduct.  
- Provide prevention education aimed at eliminating and preventing these prohibited behaviors.

2. Address Sex Discrimination and Sexual Misconduct  
St. Thomas must:
- Provide mechanisms for addressing issues of sex discrimination and sexual misconduct that affect students and employees.  
- Offer both formal and alternative processes for responding to such concerns.  
- Take action when the institution becomes aware that sex discrimination or sexual misconduct is occurring; the university cannot ignore such situations.

3. Support Impacted Students and Employees  
St. Thomas must:
- Provide support for individuals who report sex discrimination or sexual misconduct.  
- Provide support for all students and employees who are impacted by sex discrimination or sexual misconduct, regardless of whether they choose to pursue a formal process.

These Title IX basics guide how St. Thomas structures its policies, response procedures, and support resources related to sex discrimination and sexual misconduct.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Title IX / Required Reporting
After a required report is made, the only automatic next step is that the Title IX Coordinator will reach out to the affected individual to offer support and explain available options. The affected individual has full control over whether, when, and how to engage with the Title IX Coordinator after that initial outreach. Some individuals may choose to wait before connecting with the Coordinator until they feel ready, but the option to meet and receive support remains available at all times.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

## Procedures
### Bulletin Boards
### Bulletin Boards: Formatting and Presentation Standards

Creating content that residents want to read is essential to ensure they actually engage with the information we are communicating. Although bulletin boards are designed and created by the CD team and your responsibility is only to post them, you are still expected to organize and present the materials in a way that is visually clear and easy to read.

Each bulletin board must meet the following standards:

- **Background**
  - Use a background in **black, white, or purple**.
  - The background must be **replaced when faded**, and **at least once during J-Term** each year.

- **Border**
  - Use a **consistent border** around the board.
  - The border must be **secured around the entire perimeter** of the board.

- **Placement of Materials**
  - All materials must be **placed evenly and in order**.
  - Each item must be **secured with staples in all four corners**.

- **Missing Materials**
  - **Any missing materials must be reported immediately**.
  - Missing items should be **replaced as soon as possible**.

These guidelines apply to all bulletin boards as part of the **Formatting for Delivery: Bulletin Boards** standards.

**Sources:**
- `Community Communications.pdf`

To execute Community Communications, follow the instructions and materials provided by your supervisor, using the designated channels (email, bulletin boards, GroupMe, etc.) as directed.

### Conflict Resolution
WHAT RESIDENTS EXPECT: Conflict Resolution

Residents expect clear, fair, and timely support in resolving conflicts that arise in the residential community. This includes situations involving roommates, suitemates, neighbors, and shared spaces, as well as conflicts related to a single room.

Residents expect that:

- Conflict resolution resources and processes are available when issues occur in a single room (for example, between roommates or among occupants of the same room).
- Staff will listen to all parties involved, treat each person with respect, and maintain appropriate confidentiality.
- The process for addressing conflicts will be explained clearly, including any steps residents are expected to take.
- Efforts will be made to resolve conflicts informally when possible, before moving to more formal interventions.
- Any follow-up actions, room changes, or other outcomes will be communicated in a timely and transparent manner.

**Sources:**
- `Roommate Agreement & Conflict Resolution Process.pdf`
- `Roommate Agreement & Conflict Resolution Process.pdf`

---

### Conflict Resolution Process
### Conflict Resolution Process

Use the following steps as needed, in order. Move to the next step only if the previous one does not resolve the situation.

1. **RA Individual Support Meeting**  
   - First, the Resident Assistant (RA) meets individually with the involved resident(s) to provide support, listen to concerns, and clarify the issues.

2. **RA-Facilitated Roommate Meeting**  
   - If additional support is needed, the RA facilitates a roommate meeting to help the residents discuss concerns, expectations, and possible solutions together.

3. **CD/GSA Individual Coaching**  
   - If the conflict persists, the Community Director (CD) or Graduate Staff Assistant (GSA) provides individual coaching to the resident(s), offering higher-level guidance and strategies for resolution.

4. **CD/GSA Roommate Mediation**  
   - If needed after individual coaching, the CD or GSA conducts a formal roommate mediation, directly mediating the conversation between roommates to work toward an agreement.

**Sources:**
- `Roommate Agreement & Conflict Resolution Process.pdf`

**Section: Documentation & Follow-Up**

The **Roompact Form – Ongoing Resident Notes / Roommate Conflict Tracking Form** is used to:

- Record **ongoing resident notes** related to behavior, concerns, and general residential life interactions.
- Track **roommate conflicts**, including initial concerns, developing issues, and any changes over time.
- Maintain consistent **documentation** of all relevant interactions, observations, and interventions.
- Support appropriate **follow-up** by staff, ensuring continuity and accountability in addressing resident needs and roommate issues.

All entries in the Roompact Form must:

- Be completed in a timely manner whenever there is a relevant resident interaction or roommate conflict development.
- Include sufficient detail to clearly document the situation, actions taken, and any planned or required follow-up.
- Be updated as follow-up occurs, so the record reflects both the initial documentation and all subsequent steps.

### Duty & On-Call Procedures
**RA Response Procedures and Key Use Guidelines**

**Evacuation Areas for Tornado or High Winds:**

- **Brady:** Proceed to the lower level.
- **Dowling:** Move to all lower level areas.
- **Flynn:** Use the parking ramp.
- **Frey:** Go to the lower level of the Parking Garage.
- **Grace:** Stay in the general area of the lower level, away from windows, and lay face down.
- **Ireland:** Head to the lower level wings, keep away from windows, and lay face down.
- **Mid-Campus:** Use the lower level, specifically the interior hallway.
- **Morrison:** Utilize the parking ramp; stairwells are only for necessary use.
- **Murray:** Stay on the lower level, as close to the center as possible.
- **Tommie North:** Use the parking garage.

**Use of Sequential or Master Keys:**

The University of St. Thomas Residence Life Office prioritizes resident privacy. Student leaders must not enter a student's room without explicit permission from at least one resident, except in specific situations:

1. **Emergency Situations:** Such as rule violations in progress or life-threatening conditions.
2. **Noise Issues:** If there is persistent noise (e.g., a ringing telephone or alarm clock) from an apparently empty room, follow these steps before entry:
   - Attempt to locate a resident.
   - Confirm entry with your supervisor or the CD/GSA on call.
   - Ensure another student leader or staff member accompanies you.
   - Knock loudly and identify yourself before entering. If unanswered, knock again, announce your intent to key in, open the door partially, and identify yourself before proceeding.
   - After entry, send a follow-up email detailing the reason for entry, and CC your supervisor. (This does not apply to mass room entries like routine health and safety inspections.)

3. **Routine Inspections:** Entry for scheduled inspections, such as pre-vacation or break period checks, is generally conducted by two Student Leaders.

**Master Key Access:**

- RAs must obtain CD/GSA permission before keying into a room.
- Never loan out a master key; always unlock the door personally.
- Keep the master key in a secure location and use it judiciously, strictly for emergencies.

**Sources:**
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`

### Event Attendance Tracking
**Event Attendance Tracking Procedures**

Attendance tracking is mandatory for all Residential Connection Events using TommieLink, while Community Meeting attendance is managed through Roompact. There are three methods to track attendance effectively:

1. **Track Attendance Button**: This option allows for manual attendance management. You can add attendees, modify their status (attended, absent, or excused), and include comments next to each name. Additionally, you can download an Excel file by clicking "export," which provides a list of names, emails, attendance status, and details on who tracked the attendance and when.

2. **Access Code & Check-In App**: To use this method, download the Check-In app from the [Apple Store](https://itunes.apple.com/us/app/campus-labs-event-check-in/id1221503400?mt=8) or [Google Play](https://play.google.com/store/apps/details?id=com.campuslabs.checkins&hl=en). A detailed tutorial is available on the [Event Resource: Using the Check-In App for Attendance](https://stthomas.instructure.com/courses/84343/pages/event-resource-using-the-check-in-app-for-attendance-2) page. Within the app, enter the access code and sign into your UST account. You can then check attendees in and out using their event pass (QR code), email, or by searching their name. The app also allows you to view their RSVP or invited status.

Ensure all attendance is accurately tracked and recorded as per the guidelines provided.

### Event Planning
### Event Planning 101: Budget & Supplies

When planning an event, follow these budgeting and supply guidelines:

1. **Confirm Your Budget**
   - Check in with your CD/GSA regarding the budget available for your area.
   - Discuss event costs and limits before making any purchases or commitments.

2. **Use Available Supplies**
   - Check the resource room for supplies before buying anything new.
   - Utilize existing materials whenever possible to stay within budget.

3. **Food and Snacks**
   - You may only utilize **in-house catering** and **Summit Marketplace** for snacks.
   - Do not purchase food or snacks from outside vendors.

4. **Brainstorming and Support**
   - If you need help brainstorming event ideas or planning details, communicate with your supervisor **in advance**.
   - Use established channels such as:
     - 1:1 meetings  
     - Weekly reports  
     - Emails  

Ensure all budget and supply decisions are reviewed with your supervisor (CD/GSA) ahead of time.

**Sources:**
- `RL-Residential Connection Events1 (1).pdf`

### Event Planning & Programming
Event Creation and Configuration Guidelines

When creating an event, complete all of the following components. Do not skip any required fields or statements.

1. Basic Event Information  
   - Create a clear, descriptive event title.  
   - Select an appropriate theme for the event.  
   - Write an event description that:  
     - Explains what the event is about.  
     - Includes specific instructions on how to locate the event (e.g., building name, room number, landmarks, or online access details).  
     - Lists any important details attendees should know prior to attending (e.g., what to bring, expectations, or preparation needed).  
     - Provides contact information for questions or concerns.  

2. Required Accessibility Statement  
   - All event descriptions must include the following accessibility statement exactly, with the appropriate contact information inserted:  
     - “To make a disability accommodation request, please contact the event coordinator [insert Community/Hall Director name] at [insert Community/Hall Director email address] at least one week prior to the event date.”

3. Time and Place  
   - Enter the event date(s) and start/end times.  
   - If the same activity occurs multiple times, use the “+ Add another date” option to create multiple occurrences of the same event.  
   - Provide location details:  
     - For in-person events, specify the physical location clearly.  
     - For online events, click “Online Location” to add your meeting link and any necessary online instructions.

4. Event Visibility Settings  
   - Select who can see the event by choosing one of the following visibility options:  
     - **The Public**: Both logged in and not logged in users can view the event.  
       - Use this option so parents and families can view events.  
     - **Students & staff**: Only logged in users can view the event.  
     - **Organization Members**: Only members listed on your club roster can view the event.  
     - **Invited users only**: Only those specifically invited can view the event.

5. Event Categories and Perks  
   - Select all applicable event categories and perks.  
   - Proper categorization ensures users can find your event using the event directory search filters.  
   - Example: If a student is searching for a diversity and inclusion event that serves free food, and you have correctly categorized your event under relevant categories and perks, your event will appear in their search and they may attend.

6. RSVP Options  
   - Configure RSVP settings as needed:  
     - Select who can RSVP.  
     - Set how many people can RSVP (capacity limits).  
     - Indicate whether guests are allowed.  
     - Add any custom questions for attendees, such as:  
       - Food preferences  
       - Dietary restrictions  
       - Guest information  
       - Shirt sizes  
   - Ensure all necessary information for planning (e.g., catering, materials, apparel) is captured through these custom questions.

7. Off Campus Activity Release  
   - If the event involves off-campus activity, ensure the Off Campus Activity Release process is addressed in accordance with applicable procedures.

**Sources:**
- `Event Resource_ Creating Events in TommieLink_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

---

Backwards Scaffolding Event Planning 101

Use the following backwards-planning timeline to prepare and execute your event. All steps, deadlines, and requirements must be followed as listed.

**One Month Before the Event**
- Brainstorm event ideas.
- Run your ideas by your CD/GSA for feedback and approval to move forward.

**Three Weeks Before the Event**
- Review all event guidelines.
- Submit your TommieLink event for approval.
  - Be sure to include all supply requests.
  - Be sure to include all catering requests.

**Two Weeks Before the Event**
- Begin marketing your event.
- Use 3 unique methods to market (e.g., different platforms or strategies).

**One Week Before the Event**
- Confirm that you have all necessary supplies for the event.
- Start promoting the event in GroupMe.
- Continue promoting the event via word of mouth.

**Day of the Event**
- Arrive early to set up the space and materials.
- Take at least one photo during the event.
- Clean up the event space after the program concludes.
- Submit the Roompact Form following the event.

**Sources:**
- `RL-Residential Connection Events1 (1).pdf`

### Event Planning & Promotion
Any event occurring off campus must include the Activity Release questions in the RSVP section. Only event approvers (CD/GSAs) are able to add these Activity Release questions to the RSVP section.

**Post-Event Feedback (Page 3)**  
Create a custom survey in Tommie Link to collect feedback from attendees after the event. On the survey setup page, check the box at the top to automatically send your survey to attendees after the event.

**Event Cover Photo (Page 4)**  
The Event Cover Photo is the image displayed on your event page. If you do not have a prepared image or you skip this page, a default photo supplied by the system will be used, based on the event theme you selected.

Your Cover Photo helps draw attention to your event. To ensure your image looks great, choose a high-resolution photo that is eye-catching, high quality, and has a clear central focal point.

Guidelines for image files:
- **Dimensions:** Image should be 1300px by 780px or larger and horizontal in orientation. All images will be cropped to a 1.67:1 aspect ratio.
- **File Type:** JPG, JPEG, GIF, PNG, and PDF are accepted.
- **File Size:** Use a photo that is no larger than 10MB.
- **General:**
  - Avoid images that contain text or logos.
  - PDF files will not have a preview and cannot be cropped.

**Custom Fields (Page 5)**  
Continue to the Custom Fields section if any of the following applies to your event:
- You need to indicate who is hosting the event (e.g., RA, CEB).
- You need to add additional event details.

**Sources:**
- `Event Resource_ Creating Events in TommieLink_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### Event Planning & Submission
Event Submission: Additional Details, Budget, and Review Process

When submitting an event, you must complete the additional details and budget sections accurately and then review your submission before final confirmation. The information you provide here is used by the CD/GSA to review your event and to share relevant university policies with you. If any information in this section does not match your event description, your event will be denied and you will be required to resubmit with all questions answered properly.

1. Additional Event Details (Page 6)

On this page, you must provide specific information about your event, including:

- **Communities this event is for (select all that apply)**  
  Indicate all communities that the event is intended to serve.

- **Location reserved appropriately**  
  Confirm that the event location has been reserved correctly and is appropriate for the event.

- **Partnering / Co-Hosting**  
  Indicate whether you are partnering or co-hosting with another group, office, or organization.

- **Offering Food**  
  Specify whether food will be offered at the event.

- **Promotion outside TommieLink**  
  Indicate if you will promote the event outside of TommieLink.

- **How will you facilitate connection among residents?**  
  Describe how the event will foster connection and interaction among residents.

2. Budget & Event Supplies Request (Page 6)

You must also provide complete budget and supply information:

- **Total budget requested**  
  State the total amount of money you are requesting for the event.

- **Provide a cost breakdown of the budget requested**  
  Itemize how the requested funds will be used (e.g., supplies, food, decorations).

- **Link to specific supplies**  
  Include links to the specific supplies you plan to purchase.

These questions help the CD/GSA gather the additional information they need to review the event and to provide you with information on university policies that apply to your specific event.

3. Review and Submission (Page 7)

On the final page:

- **Review your submission for final confirmation of your event details.**  
  Carefully check all sections (event description, additional details, budget, and supplies) to ensure they are accurate and consistent.

- **If you are satisfied with everything, click on “submit” at the bottom of the page.**  
  Submitting indicates that you have verified all information and that it matches across all sections.

4. CD/GSA Review, Denials, and Fixing Errors

After you click “submit,” the CD/GSA will review your event using the information you provided, including:

- Intended communities
- Location and reservation status
- Partnering/co-hosting details
- Food plans
- Promotion plans (including outside TommieLink)
- Plans to facilitate connection among residents
- Total budget, cost breakdown, and supply links

If the information in your event description does not match your responses in the additional details and budget sections, or if questions are not answered properly, your event will be denied. In that case:

- You will be notified that the event has been denied.
- You will be asked to correct the errors or inconsistencies.
- You must resubmit the event with all questions answered properly and with matching information across all sections.

Carefully completing Page 6 and thoroughly reviewing your submission on Page 7 reduces the likelihood of denial and the need to resubmit.

**Sources:**
- `Event Resource_ Creating Events in TommieLink_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### General Residence Life Operations Procedures
Procedures for Residence Life Operations

The following outlines the procedures for residence life operations, ensuring a smooth and efficient management of residential facilities. These procedures are designed to maintain a safe, welcoming, and organized environment for all residents.

1. **Check-In Process**: Residents must complete the check-in process at the designated times provided by the residence life office. This includes verifying personal information, receiving room keys, and signing the housing agreement. Any discrepancies in personal information should be reported immediately to the residence life staff.

2. **Room Assignments**: Room assignments are determined based on the preferences submitted during the application process, subject to availability. Changes to room assignments can be requested through the residence life office but are not guaranteed and depend on space availability and administrative approval.

3. **Maintenance Requests**: Residents are responsible for reporting any maintenance issues in their rooms or common areas. Maintenance requests should be submitted through the online portal or directly to the residence life office. Emergency maintenance issues, such as water leaks or electrical failures, should be reported immediately to the on-call staff.

4. **Community Standards**: All residents are expected to adhere to the community standards outlined in the residence life handbook. This includes respecting quiet hours, maintaining cleanliness in shared spaces, and treating fellow residents and staff with respect. Violations of community standards may result in disciplinary action.

5. **Safety and Security**: Residents must ensure that their rooms are locked when unoccupied and report any security concerns to the residence life office. The residence halls are equipped with security cameras and require keycard access for entry. In case of an emergency, residents should follow the evacuation procedures posted in each building.

6. **Check-Out Process**: At the end of the academic year or upon termination of the housing agreement, residents must complete the check-out process. This includes returning room keys, ensuring the room is clean and free of personal belongings, and completing a room inspection with a residence life staff member. Any damages or missing items may result in additional charges.

These procedures are essential for maintaining a well-functioning residential community and ensuring the safety and satisfaction of all residents. For any questions or further clarification, residents should contact the residence life office directly.

**Sources:**
- `Enforcing Policies and Following Procedures.pdf`

### Meetings & Attendance Follow-Up
Attendance at required meetings will not always be 100%; there will be students who do not attend. The information shared in these meetings is still necessary for all students, including those who were absent, so you must follow up with them.

Follow-up should include:
- 1:1 conversations with students who did not attend.
- Sharing all relevant information from the meeting with those students after you speak with them.

If many people did not attend the meeting, consider hosting the meeting a second time. In that situation, you should also talk to your supervisor about why there may have been low attendance.

**Sources:**

### Programs & Meetings
Program/Meeting Procedure

For all programs or meetings, follow this standard process:

1. **TommieLink Setup**
   - Create and publish the event on TommieLink **one week in advance**.

2. **Attendance Tracking**
   - Take attendance using the designated **Roompact Form** during the event.

3. **Roompact Submission**
   - After the event, **submit the completed attendance form in Roompact**.

4. **Feedback Form**
   - If a feedback form is required for the event, **submit the feedback form** as applicable.

5. **Resident Follow-Up**
   - **Follow up with residents who could not make it** to share key information, resources, or next opportunities to engage.

6. **Environment**
   - Maintain **good vibes** throughout planning, implementation, and follow-up to foster a positive community atmosphere.

**Sources:**

### RA Touch Base Meetings
Resident Assistants (RAs) have significant flexibility in how they schedule and complete their responsibilities, including Touch Base Meetings. RAs should work directly with their Community Director (CD) and/or Graduate Student Assistant (GSA) to create a plan for scheduling and completing Touch Base Meetings that works well for both the RA and their residents.

**Sources:**
- `Touch Base Meetings.pdf`

---

**Touch Base Meeting Expectations**

Resident Assistants are expected to complete a Touch Base Meeting with all residents and to prepare thoroughly in advance. The following guidelines apply:

1. **Completion with All Residents**
   - A Touch Base Meeting must be completed with every resident.
   - For single rooms, the meeting must be completed with the individual resident.
   - For rooms with more than one resident, the meeting must be completed with all roommates present.
   - If it is not possible to meet with all roommates together (due to schedules or other conflicts), you may complete the meeting with roommates at separate times.

2. **Preparation and Ongoing Responsibilities**
   - Prepare appropriately for each Touch Base Meeting.
   - Regularly check Roommate Agreement completion.
   - Track group chat membership to ensure residents are included as needed.
   - Review the facilitation guide before conducting meetings.
   - Practice ahead of time using the facilitation guide and any related materials.

**Sources:**
- `Touch Base Meetings.pdf`

---

### Room Access, Keys & Lockouts
**RA Response Procedures**

1. **Room Access for Non-Residents:**
   - Do not open a room for someone who is not a resident of that room. If a non-resident needs access, they must wait for the room's resident to return. Direct any questions to the CD/GSA or Public Safety.

2. **Room Access Verification:**
   - Do not open a room for a resident you do not recognize without verifying their identity. Use a picture ID and cross-check it against the Residence Life roster or Roompact to confirm they are the actual occupant.

3. **Room Entry and Search:**
   - Refer to the Resident Student Handbook section on Room Entry and Room Search for detailed information. Always consult with the CD/GSA on duty, your CD/GSA, or another professional staff member before entering a room.

4. **Master Key and Access Card Protocol:**
   - Notify your CD/GSA immediately if you lose the master key or sequential key. All RAs have access to a master key. In buildings with card access, your student ID serves as the master key. In communities using physical keys, each RA receives an individually numbered access key that fits one of two locks on the sequential lock, with the other lock holding the master key. Your CD/GSA will demonstrate how to access this lock.
   - If you temporarily lose or misplace your ID card with master access, contact the CD/GSA on duty immediately. If you lose your key or ID card (without master access), notify your CD/GSA immediately via email.
   - RAs are financially responsible for replacing any keys or access cards they possess or check out, as well as for all cores accessed if a key or access card is lost.

5. **Lost Keys and ID Cards:**
   - If a resident loses their key, instruct them to report it to the Residence Life Office in Koch 120, where they will be charged accordingly. Residence Life can also provide a spare key.
   - If a resident loses their ID card, direct them to the card office (open Monday – Friday, 8:30 am – 4:00 pm). Outside office hours, they should go to Public Safety.

6. **Lockouts:**
   - If a student is locked out of their room, they should seek assistance from an RA or CD/GSA. If they cannot find one, they can go to the Residence Life Office in Koch 120, where limited staff will be available to assist with lockouts.

**Sources:**
- `RA Manual 2025-2026.pdf`

---

### Student Conduct
RA-Facilitated Roommate Meeting

Use this structure when conducting an RA-facilitated meeting with roommates:

1. Getting Started  
   - Begin the meeting and explain its purpose.

2. Introductions  
   - Have all participants introduce themselves.

3. Talking Piece  
   - Introduce and explain the use of a talking piece so only the person holding it speaks.

4. Ground Rules  
   - Establish ground rules for the conversation (e.g., respect, listening, no interruptions).

5. Roommate Agreement (if not completed)  
   - If a Roommate Agreement has not yet been completed, work with the roommates to complete it at this time.

6. Facilitated Conversation  
   - Guide a structured conversation that includes:
     - Biggest concerns: Ask each roommate to share their biggest concerns.  
     - Clarification: Help clarify any misunderstandings or unclear points.  
     - Suggesting solutions: Support roommates in suggesting possible solutions to the concerns raised.

7. Agreements  
   - Help the roommates reach clear agreements based on the discussion and document what has been agreed upon.

**Sources:**
- `Roommate Agreement & Conflict Resolution Process.pdf`

---

### Touch Base Meetings
**Touch Base Meetings – Goals and Purpose**

Touch Base Meetings are brief, intentional check-ins between RAs and residents. The goals of these meetings are to:

- Briefly check in with every resident.  
- Connect residents to their RA and begin building a relationship.  
- Ensure residents know what supports and resources RAs can offer.  
- Support residents in completing their roommate agreements.  
- Provide an opportunity for residents to ask any lingering questions.  
- Ensure residents are in the Group Chat.

**Sources:**
- `Touch Base Meetings.pdf`

---

If you are using scheduled sign‑ups for Touch Base Meetings and arrive at a room where only one resident is present because they did not check their roommate(s)’ schedule when signing up, you should still engage with the resident who is there. Conduct the Touch Base Meeting with the present resident, and then follow up separately to ensure you also meet with the absent roommate(s). Do not skip the opportunity to connect with the resident who did show up, but make sure you later coordinate with the missing roommate(s) to complete their required meeting(s).

If you are using Touch Base Meeting Hours and have most of your meetings completed, but you do not have anything scheduled for a particular evening and notice two roommates hanging out near the laundry room waiting for their laundry to finish—and you have not yet met with them—you should take advantage of this opportunity. Approach them, explain that you still need to complete their Touch Base Meetings, and, if they are willing and it is an appropriate time, conduct their meetings then. This allows you to complete required meetings efficiently and build rapport in a casual setting, even outside of pre‑scheduled hours.

**Sources:**
- `Touch Base Meetings.pdf`

---

**Touch Base Meetings – Options for Completion**

You have several options for completing your Touch Base Meetings with residents. You may use one or a combination of the following:

1. **Hold “Touch Base Meeting Hours” in a Common Area**  
   - Schedule specific hours when you are available in a common area.  
   - Residents come to meet with you during these designated hours.

2. **Door-to-Door Meetings**  
   - Knock on resident doors and complete Touch Base Meetings in their rooms or at their doors.

3. **Scheduled Meetings via Sign-Up Link**  
   - Schedule Touch Base Meetings in advance using Bookings or another sign-up link.  
   - Residents select a time slot that works for them.

4. **Impromptu Meetings**  
   - Conduct unscheduled Touch Base Meetings when everyone is present (for example, when residents are already gathered).  
   - This option should **not** be your primary plan.  
   - Use impromptu meetings only as needed to complete your meetings, and only if you are adequately prepared in the moment.

**Sources:**
- `Touch Base Meetings.pdf`

## Safety
### Collaborating with Public Safety
COLLABORATING WITH PUBLIC SAFETY – SLED 2025

This section outlines expectations and practices for collaborating with Public Safety in the context of SLED 2025. All residence life staff should understand how and when to work with Public Safety to support student safety, uphold community standards, and respond to incidents in and around the residence halls.

Collaboration with Public Safety under SLED 2025 includes:

- Recognizing Public Safety as a key partner in maintaining a safe and secure residential environment.
- Following all established SLED 2025 protocols when contacting or coordinating with Public Safety.
- Communicating clearly, promptly, and professionally with Public Safety officers during any incident response.
- Supporting Public Safety’s role while maintaining appropriate residence life responsibilities and boundaries.
- Ensuring that any required documentation, reporting, or follow-up related to Public Safety interactions is completed in accordance with SLED 2025 guidelines.

All staff are expected to be familiar with current SLED 2025 procedures and to apply them consistently when collaborating with Public Safety.

**Sources:**
- `Collaborating with Public Safety.pdf`

- Responding to and addressing policy violations in the residence halls

### Security & Police Operations
Body-Worn Cameras

We are dedicated to holding our officers to the highest standard, and body‑worn cameras are a key part of that commitment. These cameras support accountability, transparency, and continuous improvement in our response to calls.

Purpose and Use
- Body‑worn cameras allow us to review footage when we need more information for a report.  
- Footage is reviewed if we receive a complaint about how a call was handled.  
- Cameras are also used to debrief officers so we can improve our response and overall practices.  
- Since issuing these cameras to officers, we have seen a significant decrease in complaints.  
- The use of cameras has helped build trust with individuals who may have had poor experiences with law enforcement in the past.

Activation and Recording
- Officers activate body‑worn cameras on every call, including escorts and access requests.  
- The cameras are always buffering (pre‑recording). When activated, they save all video footage from up to 2 minutes prior to activation.  

Indicator Lights
- A red blinking light means the camera is actively recording.  
- A solid green light means the camera is buffering (pre‑recording) but not yet actively recording.

**Sources:**
- `2025 Res Life Presentation.pdf`

## Student Conduct
### Amnesty and Protection from Retaliation
**Amnesty and Protection from Retaliation**

A student who reports sexual misconduct in good faith—whether as a reporting party or as a witness—will not be subject to disciplinary action for drugs and alcohol use under the St. Thomas student code of conduct. In addition, any student who reports sexual misconduct in good faith is protected from retaliation under the Sexual Misconduct policy.

These protections are intended to remove barriers to reporting and to seeking help when relationship or sexual violence has occurred.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Behavior Evaluation Criteria
When evaluating behavior, several key considerations must be taken into account. These include the impact of the behavior, the scope of the behavior, and any ongoing patterns of behavior. Additionally, it is essential to reference relevant university and position documents, policies, and agreements to ensure comprehensive understanding and alignment with institutional standards.

**Sources:**
- `RA Assessment & Learning Process + RA Accountability.pdf`

---

### Behavior and Performance Accountability
**Behavior and Performance Concerns**

A recurring pattern or multiple instances of Level 3 behaviors within a single incident, failure to perform critical responsibilities, inappropriate use of position (such as misuse of access to spaces, coercion, or breaches of confidentiality), and Code of Conduct violations that result in conduct status up to and including expulsion, are all considered serious behavior and performance concerns.

**Accountability Measures**

The following accountability measures may be applied in response to these concerns:

1. **Dismissal** from the student leader position.
2. **Rescindment** of future position offers.
3. Placement on **probation** in the student leader position.
4. Implementation of a **Written Performance Plan**.
5. **Ongoing Coaching** at Level 4.

These measures are designed to address and rectify the identified issues while maintaining the integrity and safety of the residence life community.

**Sources:**
- `RA Assessment & Learning Process + RA Accountability.pdf`

---

### Bias & Discrimination / Title IX
In the 2024–25 academic year, a total of 49 bias and discriminatory concerns were reported to the Title IX Coordinator, including 22 student bias/discriminatory concerns. All impacted parties were provided with resources for supportive and safety measures, as well as information about process options to address their concerns.

Supportive and safety measures offered included:

- Mutual no contact orders, which prohibit direct and indirect contact between two individuals.
- Academic accommodations, such as modified assignments, altered exam or homework submission deadlines, changes in small group assignments, moves to a different course section, or end-of-semester incompletes.
- Workplace accommodations, such as modified work schedules or temporary leave.

Why this matters: These reports demonstrate that bias and discrimination is happening at St. Thomas.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

### Conflict Resolution & Mediation Expectations
WHAT RESIDENTS EXPECT: Conflict

Residents expect staff to recognize that conflict is a normal part of community living and to respond in ways that are fair, consistent, and respectful to everyone involved. They expect staff to:

- Acknowledge that disagreements, misunderstandings, and tension between roommates, suitemates, and neighbors will occur in shared living environments.
- Treat all parties impartially, without taking sides, making assumptions, or showing favoritism.
- Listen actively to each person’s perspective and allow everyone involved in the conflict to be heard.
- Maintain privacy and handle conflict-related information discreetly, sharing details only with those who need to know as part of resolving the situation.
- Respond in a timely manner when conflicts are brought to their attention, rather than ignoring or minimizing concerns.
- Provide clear information about relevant policies, community standards, and possible outcomes so residents understand how decisions are made.
- Encourage direct, respectful communication between residents whenever it is safe and appropriate to do so.
- Facilitate conversations or mediations when residents request help or are unable to resolve issues on their own.
- Focus on problem-solving and finding workable, mutually acceptable solutions rather than assigning blame.
- Follow established procedures consistently, including any required documentation, follow-up, or referrals.
- Set reasonable expectations for behavior and hold residents accountable to community standards in a way that is transparent and predictable.
- Offer resources and referrals (such as campus support services) when conflicts are complex, ongoing, or beyond the scope of basic mediation.
- Check in after a conflict has been addressed to ensure agreements are being honored and to see whether additional support is needed.
- Remain calm, professional, and composed, even when residents are upset, frustrated, or emotional.
- Clearly explain any next steps, timelines, or further actions that may occur as a result of the conflict and its resolution process.

Overall, residents expect staff to help create an environment where conflicts are addressed constructively, everyone is treated with respect, and the community remains safe and livable for all.

**Sources:**
- `Roommate Agreement & Conflict Resolution Process.pdf`

---

### Conflict Resolution Process & RA Involvement
ROAD MAP: Connecting to Our “Why” – Conflict Concepts, Process, RA Involvement, and Roompact Form

This section outlines the ROAD MAP for addressing conflict in the residential community by connecting to our “why,” clarifying key conflict concepts, defining the conflict resolution process, specifying Resident Assistant (RA) involvement, and documenting the use of the Roompact form.

1. Connecting to Our “Why”  
The ROAD MAP begins by grounding all conflict work in our core purpose (“why”):  
- Support a safe, respectful, and inclusive living environment.  
- Help residents develop conflict resolution skills and personal responsibility.  
- Strengthen community through open communication and accountability.  

All conflict-related actions, decisions, and communications should be intentionally connected back to this “why.”

2. Conflict Concepts  
Staff should use consistent conflict concepts when working with residents, including:  
- Recognizing that conflict is a natural part of community living.  
- Distinguishing between disagreement, conflict, and policy violations.  
- Understanding the impact of conflict on individuals, roommates, and the broader community.  
- Emphasizing communication, empathy, and problem-solving as core tools in conflict situations.  

These concepts should guide how staff frame conversations, interventions, and follow-up.

3. Conflict Process  
The conflict process follows the ROAD MAP structure and must be applied consistently:  
- Identify the conflict: Clarify the issue, who is involved, and how it is affecting the living environment.  
- Connect to the “why”: Explain to residents why addressing the conflict matters for them and the community.  
- Explore perspectives: Allow each person to share their experience and concerns.  
- Clarify expectations: Review relevant community standards, expectations, and any applicable policies.  
- Develop a plan: Collaboratively identify next steps, agreements, or changes in behavior.  
- Follow up: Check in after the initial resolution attempt to assess progress and adjust the plan if needed.  

4. RA Involvement  
Resident Assistants play a central role in the conflict process and must be involved as follows:  
- Serve as the first point of contact for residents experiencing conflict.  
- Facilitate conversations between residents using the conflict concepts and process outlined above.  
- Connect the conflict to the community “why” during discussions with residents.  
- Document the situation and outcomes accurately using the Roompact form.  
- Elevate conflicts to professional staff when they exceed RA scope, involve safety concerns, or require formal conduct action.  

RAs must remain neutral facilitators, support all residents involved, and ensure that the process is followed.

5. Roompact Form  
All conflict-related interactions that require documentation must be recorded using the Roompact form:  
- Use the designated conflict or incident type within Roompact as directed by departmental guidelines.  
- Include all relevant details of the conflict, the steps taken in the process, RA involvement, and any agreements or follow-up plans.  
- Ensure that the documentation is timely, accurate, and objective.  
- Use the Roompact record to support continuity of care, follow-up, and any necessary referrals or escalations.  

The Roompact form is an essential part of the ROAD MAP, ensuring that conflict work is documented, trackable, and aligned with our “why.”

**Sources:**
- `Roommate Agreement & Conflict Resolution Process.pdf`

---

### Consent Policy
CONSENT

Consent is clear conduct or words that indicate a person freely agrees to engage in a sexual act at the time of the act.

Consent is not effective when:
- Force, threat, or coercion is used.
- The recipient party is incapacitated, asleep, or unconscious.
- There is silence, non-communication, failure to resist, or simply an absence of “no.”

Additional guidelines regarding consent:
- Consent to one type of sexual activity does not imply consent to any other type of sexual activity.
- Past consent does not equal future consent.
- Consent can be withdrawn at any time.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Documentation
Complete an Ongoing Resident Note

Use an Ongoing Resident Note to document continuing or developing situations, patterns, or concerns related to a resident over time. This note type is intended for matters that are not fully resolved in a single interaction and may require follow-up, monitoring, or additional action.

- `Creating Connections & Touch Points 25-26.pptx`

### Documentation & Reporting
**Ongoing Resident Notes**

Use the Ongoing Resident Notes to document resident behavior and your responses over time. Each entry must include all of the following:

- **Resident name** – The full name of the resident involved.  
- **Your name** – Your full name as the staff member completing the note.  
- **Date of behavior** – The specific date on which the behavior occurred.  
- **Behavior notes** – A clear description of the behaviors observed.  
- **Concerns** – Any concerns you have related to the behavior (e.g., impact on the resident, community, or safety).  
- **What has already happened in response?** – A summary of actions already taken in response to the behavior.  
- **What offices or resources have you already referred the student to?** – List all campus offices, departments, or resources to which the student has been referred.  
- **What follow up/next steps do you recommend?** – Your recommended follow-up actions or next steps based on the situation.

**Sources:**
- `Creating Connections & Touch Points 25-26.pptx`

### Other Key Concepts and Considerations
### Other Key Concepts and Considerations

The following concepts are essential to understand and apply in all residence life operations and responses:

- **Consent**  
  Consent must be present, clear, and ongoing in any interaction. Staff should be attentive to whether consent has been freely given, can be withdrawn at any time, and is not assumed or implied.

- **Incapacitation**  
  Incapacitation affects a person’s ability to give consent or make informed decisions. Staff must consider whether an individual’s capacity is impaired (for example, by alcohol, drugs, or other conditions) when assessing situations and responding to incidents.

- **Amnesty**  
  Amnesty refers to practices or policies that encourage reporting of concerns or incidents by limiting certain conduct-related consequences for those who come forward. Staff should be aware of amnesty provisions that may apply so residents feel safe seeking help or reporting issues.

- **No Retaliation**  
  No retaliation means that individuals who report concerns, participate in investigations, or support others in doing so must not be subjected to adverse treatment or consequences. Staff must monitor for and address any behavior that could be considered retaliatory.

- **Bystander Intervention**  
  Bystander intervention involves recognizing potentially harmful situations and choosing safe, effective ways to intervene or seek help. Staff should promote and model proactive bystander behavior, encouraging residents to look out for one another and to act when they see risk or harm.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### RA Reporting Requirements / Sexual Misconduct Reporting
### RA Reporting Requirements for Sexual Misconduct and Related Concerns

Resident Assistants (RAs) have specific obligations regarding when they must report information learned in the course of their role. The guidelines below distinguish clearly between situations where reporting is required and where it is not required.

---

#### When RAs **Must Report** (Required)

You are required to report information when:

- The information is learned or shared with you **in the context of your RA role**.  
- You are **on duty** when you learn the information.  
- The **incident occurred on your floor**, regardless of how you learned about it.  
- The information is **revealed one-on-one by a resident** directly to you.  
- In **all other instances** that are not specifically listed as “not required” below, you must report.

If a situation does not clearly fall into a “not required” category, you should treat it as **required to report**.

---

#### When RAs **Do Not Have to Report** (Not Required)

You are **not required** to report in the following situations:

- **You personally experienced sexual violence.**  
  - You do **not** need to share this experience.  
  - However, seeking supportive and safety measures is **encouraged**.

- **You are not the primary, exclusive, or intended receiver of the information.**  
  - For example, you hear disclosures at a **“Take Back the Night” event** or another **public awareness event** where information is shared broadly and not directed specifically to you in your RA capacity.

- **You learned about misconduct prior to your RA role.**  
  - If the information was obtained **before** you became an RA, you do **not** need to report it, **unless** you have gained **recent information while in the RA role**.  
  - If you do gain new or additional information about that same situation while serving as an RA, that **new information must be reported**.

---

In summary, RAs must report information related to incidents or misconduct when it is learned in their RA capacity, especially in duty contexts, on their floor, or through direct one-on-one disclosures, except in the specific “not required” circumstances listed above.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Responding to Disclosures of Sexual Misconduct
**Responding to Potential or Actual Disclosures of Sexual Misconduct**

As a non-confidential resource, you must both support community members and fulfill your reporting obligations. Use the guidance below before and after a disclosure of sexual misconduct. Do not change any of these steps or requirements.

---

### Before a Disclosure (When You Sense Someone May Disclose)

If it seems like someone is about to share an experience that could involve sexual misconduct, you must clearly state your reporting obligations before they continue:

- Say something like:  
  “I really want to hear what you have to say, but before you go any further, I want to remind you that I am not a confidential resource. If you disclose experiencing sexual misconduct, I’ll need to share that with the Title IX Coordinator. If you’d rather speak with a confidential resource about this, I can help connect you…”

- Remind community members of your reporting obligations often, not just once.
- Remind them about the amnesty policy.
- Remind them about the prohibition of retaliation.

If they prefer to speak with a confidential resource, help connect them to that resource.

---

### After a Disclosure (Once Someone Has Shared)

If a community member discloses experiencing sexual misconduct:

1. **Acknowledge and Thank Them**
   - “Thank you for telling me.”
   - Clearly state: “I’m obligated to share this with the Title IX Coordinator.”

2. **Offer Emotional Support**
   - Sit with them, listen, and validate their experience.
   - Ask supportive, safety-focused questions:
     - “Do you feel safe right now?”
     - “Do you need medical attention?”
     - “How can I help in this moment?”

3. **Offer to Connect Them to Resources**
   - Ask: “Would you like me to help you reach out to the Title IX Coordinator (or some other resource) right now?”

4. **Emphasize Their Control and Choices**
   - Remind them that they are in control and can choose how to engage, if at all, from here.  
   - Even though you must report to the Title IX Coordinator, they decide how and whether to participate in any follow-up process.

Throughout, continue to:
- Remind them of the amnesty policy.
- Remind them of the prohibition of retaliation.
- Uphold your obligation to share the disclosure with the Title IX Coordinator.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Sexual Misconduct & Harassment Reporting
Resident Assistants (RAs) must report any conduct that could be considered sexual harassment or sexual misconduct. This includes, but is not limited to, the following:

- Sexual harassment  
- Sexual assault  
- Fondling or groping  
- Rape  
- Statutory rape  
- Incest  
- Dating violence  
- Domestic violence  
- Stalking  
- Sexual exploitation  

(Reporting procedures for bias and discrimination will be addressed separately.)

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

### Sexual Misconduct Policy
### Incapacitation

Incapacitation is defined as the inability to understand the facts, nature of, or extent of a sexual situation.

When assessing incapacitation, consider whether the person can meaningfully understand and answer the basic questions about the sexual situation:

- Do they know **who** is involved?
- Do they know **what** is happening?
- Do they know **when** it is happening?
- Do they know **where** it is happening?
- Do they know **why** it is happening?
- Do they know **how** it is happening?

A person may be incapacitated if they are:

- Asleep  
- Unconscious  
- Blacked out  
- Seemingly unaware of what is happening around them  

Common warning signs of possible incapacitation include:

- Slurred speech  
- Stumbling or difficulty walking  
- Slumped over  
- Unable to zip a jacket or put on mittens  

Being drunk does **not** always equal incapacitation; however, incapacitation can result from alcohol or other substances.

**Important note:** Being intoxicated or under the influence of any substance at the time of sexual contact is **never** an excuse for violating this policy.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Sexual Misconduct Support & Reporting
FAQ – Sexual Misconduct Support and Response (RA Guidance)

This section outlines key questions related to Resident Assistants’ (RAs) roles in sexual misconduct support and response. It explains when and how RAs report, what they report, whether they are confidential, how they best support residents, and what happens after a report is made.

1. When do RAs report?  
RAs report when they become aware of information related to sexual misconduct. This includes any situation in which a resident or other individual shares information that suggests sexual misconduct may have occurred, regardless of when or where it happened, or who was involved.

2. How and where do RAs report?  
RAs follow the established institutional reporting process for sexual misconduct. They submit reports through the designated reporting channel(s) identified by the institution (for example, an online reporting form, a specific office, or a designated staff member), in accordance with residence life and institutional procedures.

3. What do RAs report?  
RAs report all information related to the disclosure of sexual misconduct that they receive in their role. This includes any details shared with them about the incident, the individuals involved, and any immediate safety or support concerns, consistent with institutional policy and reporting requirements.

4. Are RAs confidential?  
RAs are not confidential resources. They cannot promise confidentiality when a resident discloses information related to sexual misconduct. Instead, they are required to share that information through the appropriate reporting process, as outlined by institutional policy.

5. How do RAs best support residents?  
RAs best support residents by listening, expressing care and concern, and explaining their role and reporting obligations clearly. They should provide information about available support resources and options, while maintaining a supportive, nonjudgmental presence throughout the interaction.

6. What happens when an RA reports?  
When an RA submits a report, the information is routed through the institution’s designated process for sexual misconduct response. The appropriate office or staff member then reviews the report and follows institutional procedures, which may include outreach to the impacted individual, safety and support measures, and any additional steps required under policy.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

### Sexual Misconduct, Bias, and Hate Impacts
Sexual Misconduct, Bias, and Hate: Impact on Student Success

Sexual misconduct, bias, and hate have significant negative effects on student success. These experiences are associated with:

- Decreased academic performance  
- Learning problems  
- PTSD (post-traumatic stress disorder) and suicidal ideation  
- Lower GPA  
- Increased attrition (students leaving the institution)

Of note: More than 50% of college sexual assaults occur in the first 4 months of the academic year (August–November).

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Title IX & Sexual Misconduct
Title IX Coordinator Response

The Title IX Coordinator is responsible for responding to all reports and ensuring that safety and support are prioritized for all parties involved.

Upon receiving a report, the Title IX Coordinator:

- Responds to all reports received.
- May contact a Resident Assistant (RA), Community Director (CD), or Graduate Student Assistant (GSA) to gather additional information about the report before reaching out to the student.
- Reaches out to the involved parties and allows them to share their experiences.
- Works together with the parties to determine next steps, with safety and support as the primary priorities.
- Discusses resolution options, including both formal and alternative resolution processes.

In collaboration with the parties, the Title IX Coordinator:

- Considers and develops supportive and safety measures.
- Coordinates academic accommodations as needed.
- Can implement a no-contact order when appropriate.
- Provides referrals to on- and off-campus resources, which may include:
  - Center for Well-Being  
  - Priest  
  - Clergy  
  - Victim’s advocate  
  - Police

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Title IX / Sexual Misconduct
In the 2024–25 academic year, seventy-two (72) student incidents of sexual misconduct/Title IX were reported to the Title IX Coordinator. For every report in which an impacted student’s name was shared, the student was provided resources for supportive and safety measures through the Title IX (TIX) process.

As part of these responses:
- Twenty-one (21) no contact orders were put in place.
- Twenty-three (23) students received academic accommodations in one or more courses. These accommodations included, as applicable:
  - Modified assignments  
  - Altered exam or homework submission deadlines  
  - Changes in small group assignments  
  - Moves to a different course section  
  - End-of-semester incompletes  

Every student who made a report was offered options for addressing their concerns, including both a formal investigation/adjudication process and an alternative resolution process.

These data and responses underscore that sexual misconduct is occurring at St. Thomas and highlight the importance of the institution’s Title IX response, support measures, and resolution options.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Title IX Overview
Title IX Work: Big Picture Overview

Title IX efforts focus on three core areas:

1. **Eliminate / Prevent**
   - Eliminate and prevent concerning or questionable behaviors.
   - Proactively address behaviors that may lead to Title IX or bias-related concerns.

2. **Address**
   - Address concerns through both formal and alternative processes.
   - Utilize appropriate procedures to respond to reported incidents, including:
     - Title IX Report Response
     - Bias Incident Report Response

3. **Support**
   - Support all impacted students and employees.
   - Ensure that anyone affected by Title IX or bias-related incidents has access to appropriate resources and assistance.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

### Bystander Intervention
Bystander Intervention

Bystander Intervention is recognizing a potentially harmful situation or interaction and choosing to respond in a way that could positively influence the outcome.

When you notice a concerning situation, you can use any of the following approaches:

- **DISTRACT**: Take an indirect approach to de-escalate the situation.  
- **DELEGATE**: Get help from someone else.  
- **DOCUMENT**: Keep evidence of the situation, such as screenshots, writing, and emails.  
- **DELAY**: After the incident is over, check in with the person who was harmed or at-risk.  
- **DIRECT**: Assess your safety first. Speak up about the harmful situation. Ask clarifying questions. Stay calm but be firm and clear.

*ihollaback.org*

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`
