### Clery Act / Campus Security Authority (CSA)
### Clery Act Compliance and Your Role as a Campus Security Authority (CSA)

Under federal law (the Clery Act), all residence life staff are considered **Campus Security Authorities (CSAs)**. As a CSA:

- You are **required to report any crimes that occur on campus** to **Public Safety**.
- You **are not a confidential resource**; you may not promise confidentiality regarding reportable incidents.

You will receive mandatory **CSA training** via email from **Public Safety Manager Aaron Fimon**. You must complete this training and take it seriously.

Noncompliance with the Clery Act carries severe financial penalties:  
- A violation of the Clery Act is **$72,000 for each violation**.  
- As an example of the seriousness of enforcement, **Liberty University recently set a record for the highest fine in history: $14 million**.

**Sources:**
- `2025 Res Life Presentation.pdf`

### Communication & Conflict Foundations
### Assumptions and Challenges in Creating Shared Meaning

When working with others, especially in residence life settings, it is important to recognize several core assumptions about communication and perception:

- **Meanings are in people, not in words.**  
  The same word, phrase, or message can be understood very differently by different people. The interpretation lives in the listener, not in the language itself.

- **Perception trumps intention.**  
  How a message is perceived has more impact than what was intended. Even if someone’s intention is positive, the effect of their words or actions is determined by how others experience them.

- **We tend to see things not as they are, but as we are.**  
  People interpret situations through their own experiences, identities, values, and assumptions. This shapes how they understand events, comments, and behaviors.

These assumptions highlight core **challenges in creating shared meaning**. Because individuals bring their own perspectives and interpretations, the same situation or statement can evoke very different reactions—for example, one person might think, “DAMN!” another “WOW!” and another “That’s really BAD!” Recognizing these dynamics is essential for effective communication and conflict navigation.

(Adapted from materials by Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com)

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

### Communication & De-escalation Skills
### Collaborating Together on a Solution

After listening and understanding the situation, create space for the other person to respond and actively collaborate on next steps. Collaboration should focus on easing the situation together, not imposing a solution.

Use the following approaches to invite collaboration:

- Offer an explicit opportunity to respond or to work together on a plan.
- Ask what they might need from you in the moment:
  - Do they want you just to listen?
  - Would they like some suggestions?
- Ask how you can work together to ease the situation, emphasizing shared problem-solving.
- Provide a few possible solutions or options, clearly and concretely.
- Ask what they are willing to do to help ease the situation, inviting their commitment and participation.

Always give them adequate time to respond. Do not rush their answers or move on before they have had a chance to think and speak.  

(ANA, 2014; Goh, 2020)

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

---

### Communication & Dialogue Skills
Polarization: Dialogue Across Difference  
Adapted from Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com  

In a time of increased polarization, many people are asking how to have productive conversations with colleagues, friends, and loved ones who see the world differently. The guidance below outlines how to prepare for and engage in dialogue across difference.

---

### Preparation

- **Clarify your goal for the conversation.**  
  Decide what you are hoping to accomplish:  
  - Reinforce or clarify your own worldview?  
  - Build or strengthen a relationship?  
  - Encourage someone to consider another perspective?  
  The clearer you are about your purpose, the more effectively you can choose strategies that fit that purpose. If you are not clear about your purpose, consider postponing the conversation until you are.

- **Know your triggers.**  
  It is difficult to stay clear, purposeful, and adaptive when you are triggered.  
  - Identify the physiological signals that tell you you are not centered.  
  - Notice your typical responses when triggered: Do you withdraw, attack, go numb, or appease?  
  Be prepared to recognize these reactions in the moment and to manage them.

- **Suspend judgment and cultivate curiosity.**  
  Enter the conversation ready to be curious rather than to judge.  
  Remember that suspending judgment is not a one-time decision; it may be an ongoing task throughout the conversation, especially as triggers emerge.

- **Reflect on what you know about the other person.**  
  Before engaging, consider:  
  - Who are they?  
  - What matters to them?  
  - What are their values?  
  - What struggles have they faced?  
  - Who in their life (whom they care about) might be impacted by the concerns or issues you most care about?

- **Use your intercultural knowledge and skills.**  
  People whose primary orientation is Polarization/Defense (“Us vs. Them”) often experience difference with fear that something they hold dear (a belief, a way of life, etc.) is being threatened.  
  The developmental intervention for people with a Polarization mindset is to help them find commonalities, even when commonalities with those they perceive as “them” are not immediately obvious.

- **Focus on authentic relationship-building.**  
  Authentic relationships tend to be built on curiosity, dialogue, and empathy—not on ideology, jargon, or the transmission of data alone.

- **Remember that transformation takes time.**  
  Shifting someone’s mindset will likely require more than one conversation.  
  Check in with yourself about your energy, your level of commitment, and your capacity to stay engaged over time.

---

### Engagement

- **Begin with inquiry.**  
  Invite the other person to share:  
  - For example: “If you are willing to share, what about (this topic) is important to you?”  
  Listen to understand, not just to prepare your response. Continue listening until the other person feels understood.

- **Stay curious.**  
  Ask about:  
  - Their concerns and fears  
  - Their hopes and visions for the future  
  - The values that are important to them  

- **Offer authentic empathy.**  
  Empathize to the extent you can do so genuinely. Reflect back what you are hearing and acknowledge their feelings and experiences.

- **Share your own experience.**  
  When appropriate, share:  
  - How you are feeling  
  - Your concerns and fears  
  - Your hopes and visions for the future  
  - The values that are important to you  
  Be real and grounded in your own experience.

- **Use stories to build connection.**  
  Tell stories that connect the other person’s experiences, issues, or concerns to those of someone (or a group) they may have perceived as a threatening “them.”  
  Aim to help them discover commonalities that can support a shift from “us vs. them” to “we.”

- **Find constructive stopping points.**  
  Look for natural points to pause so you can end the conversation in a place of understanding. This allows you to revisit the conversation later if possible, rather than pushing past the limits of what either of you can handle in the moment.

- **Deepen the dialogue over time.**  
  Once rapport is established, follow-up conversations can go deeper.  
  Sharing additional experiences and stories can support the development of empathy and a more complex, nuanced worldview.

**Sources:**
- `Polarization Dialogue across difference.pdf`

### Communication Skills
DESCS: Describe – Express – Specify – Collaborate – Summarize  
(Adapted from *Asserting Yourself* by Bower & Bower; ANA, 2014; Bower & Bower, 2004; Goh, 2020)

DESCS is an assertive communication framework consisting of five steps:

1. **Describe**  
   Objectively describe the situation or behavior, focusing on observable facts rather than judgments or interpretations.

2. **Express**  
   Express your feelings, reactions, or concerns about the situation in a clear and direct way.

3. **Specify**  
   Specify the change you are requesting or the outcome you would like to see, stating it in concrete, behavioral terms.

4. **Collaborate**  
   Collaborate with the other person to explore options, negotiate solutions, and agree on a plan that addresses the needs of everyone involved.

5. **Summarize**  
   Summarize the agreed-upon actions, expectations, and next steps to ensure mutual understanding and clarity.

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

---

### Active Listening Skills

Active listening involves two main components: **attending skills** and **reflective skills**. Both are necessary to fully understand and support the person speaking.

---

#### 1. Attending Skills

Use these behaviors to show you are present, engaged, and focused on the speaker:

- **Eye contact**  
  Maintain appropriate eye contact to convey attention and interest.

- **Open position**  
  Sit or stand in an open, non-defensive posture (e.g., uncrossed arms) to appear approachable and receptive.

- **Incline your body**  
  Lean slightly toward the speaker to demonstrate engagement and concern.

- **Responsive motions/gesture**  
  Use small, natural gestures (such as nodding) to show you are following and understanding what is being said.

- **Calm, relaxed state**  
  Maintain a calm, relaxed demeanor to help the speaker feel comfortable and safe sharing.

---

#### 2. Reflective Skills

Use these skills to help the speaker feel heard and to deepen understanding:

- **Validate feelings**  
  Acknowledge and accept the speaker’s emotions as real and important.

- **Open ended questions**  
  Ask questions that cannot be answered with a simple “yes” or “no” to encourage fuller responses and more detail.

- **Invitations to continue talking**  
  Prompt the speaker to share more (e.g., “Tell me more about that”) so they feel encouraged to keep talking.

- **Reflect back what you have heard and seen**  
  Summarize or restate both the content and the emotions you observe to confirm your understanding.

- **Paraphrasing**  
  Restate the speaker’s message in your own words to check accuracy and show that you are listening closely.

- **Allow silence**  
  Give space for pauses and silence so the speaker has time to think, process, and continue at their own pace.

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

---

Express Your Feelings and Thoughts  

When addressing a situation or behavior, focus on expressing your own feelings and thoughts rather than accusing or blaming the other person. Phrase your statements using “I” instead of “You.”  

Using “You” statements often puts people on the defensive, which makes them less open to the conversation. In contrast, “I” statements help keep the discussion constructive and reduce defensiveness (ANA, 2014; Bower & Bower, 2004).

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

---

Use brief, clear summary statements to conclude or refocus conversations. Summarizing can be used to:

- Wrap up a discussion  
- Review key points that were shared  
- Express appreciation or thanks  

Effective summarizing helps prevent rambling or repeated points and shows the student you have listened and understood. For example:

“Thanks for sharing with me. I am hearing that it’s been hard to keep up with everything, and you’re feeling a lot of stress.” (ANA, 2014; Goh, 2020)

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

---

### Barriers to Effective Listening

When engaging with residents or colleagues, several common barriers can interfere with effective listening. Be aware of the following:

- **Too many distractions**: Environmental noise, phones, side conversations, or other competing stimuli can pull attention away from the speaker.  
- **Mind wandering / drifting away**: Allowing your thoughts to drift, losing focus on what the other person is saying.  
- **Thinking about what to say next instead of really listening**: Planning your response, argument, or next question while the other person is still talking, rather than fully attending to their words.  
- **Jumping to advice giving / attempting to resolve the problem too quickly**: Moving immediately into problem-solving or offering solutions before fully understanding the situation or the person’s perspective.  
- **Neglecting to ask if what you’ve heard is accurate**: Failing to check your understanding by summarizing or confirming what the speaker has said, which can lead to misunderstandings.  
- **Strong emotional reactions or judgment**: Reacting emotionally, making quick judgments, or forming opinions about the person or situation while they are speaking, which can prevent open, nonjudgmental listening.

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

---

### Community Building & Engagement
**Connection and Community**

In the context of community, *connection* refers to the relationships, interactions, and shared experiences that help individuals feel a sense of belonging and comfort within their environment. At St. Thomas, connection is closely tied to how residents experience and participate in the campus community.

When considering connection in relation to community, reflect on the following:

- **Definition of Connection**  
  How would you define *connection* in relation to community? Think about what it means for residents to feel known, supported, and included in their living environment and on campus.

- **Events and Experiences**  
  What events or experiences contributed to your own sense of community at St. Thomas? Consider programs, traditions, informal gatherings, or shared challenges that helped you feel part of something larger.

- **People Who Helped You Connect**  
  Who helped you feel comfortable or connected on campus? Reflect on the roles of peers, RAs, faculty, staff, student leaders, or other community members who made a difference in your transition and sense of belonging.

- **Role of Events in Building Community**  
  How do events play a part in building community? Consider how structured programs, social activities, and intentional gatherings create opportunities for residents to meet, interact, and form relationships.

- **RA Role in Fostering Connections**  
  How can RAs help residents make connections? Think about strategies such as:
  - Introducing residents to one another.
  - Encouraging participation in events and experiences that build community.
  - Creating welcoming spaces where residents feel comfortable engaging.
  - Being a point of contact who helps residents navigate campus and meet new people.

These questions and reflections are intended to guide RAs in understanding and actively fostering connection as a core component of community at St. Thomas.

**Sources:**
- `RL-Residential Connection Events1 (1).pdf`

---

When planning community interactions, be intentional about your goals and preparation:

- Clarify your purpose:
  - Is the goal to meet people?
  - Build deeper connections?
  - Learn more about each other?
  - What does your community need?

- Prepare yourself:
  - BEE engaged!
  - Check attitude.
  - Find connection and joy.
  - Ask! (Invite participation, questions, and input from residents.)

- Prepare your tools:
  - Review the Icebreakers Activity Resource so you have at least one icebreaker prepared in advance.

**Sources:**
- `Community Meetings SLED 2025 (1).pdf`

---

3. Leverage Your Personality  

Use your natural personality to create a welcoming environment. Be personable and fun in your interactions so that residents feel more comfortable and relaxed around you.

**Sources:**
- `Community Meetings SLED 2025 (1).pdf`

---

Informal Touch Points with Residents

Informal touch points are casual, everyday interactions that help build community and rapport with residents. Examples include:

- Knocking on residents’ doors to say hello.  
- Making conversation in the floor GroupMe.  
- Inviting residents to join you for meals.  
- Hosting game nights for residents.  
- Doing your own homework or studying in the community lounge to be visible and approachable.

**Sources:**
- `Creating Connections & Touch Points 25-26.pptx`

---

### Community Building & Inclusion
**Purpose: Belonging and Inclusion**

Residence life staff serve as community leaders with the goal of fostering a strong sense of belonging and inclusion for all residents. This involves:

- **Building rapport** with residents by developing genuine, trusting relationships.
- **Creating opportunities** for residents to connect, engage, and participate in the community.
- **Following through as community leaders** by being reliable, responsive, and consistent in actions and communication.
- **Assessing needs** regularly to understand residents’ experiences, identify gaps in support, and respond appropriately to individual and community concerns.

**Sources:**
- `Creating Connections & Touch Points 25-26.pptx`

---

### Community Building & Resident Support
### Assessing Resident Needs

Regularly assessing resident needs is an ongoing, intentional process. Staff should:

- **Regularly check in with residents**  
  Make a habit of connecting with residents on a consistent basis, not only when issues arise.

- **Ask questions with intention**  
  Use purposeful, open-ended questions to understand residents’ experiences, needs, and concerns more deeply.

- **Pay attention to visibility in the hall**  
  Notice who you are seeing frequently and who you are not seeing around the hall, as both can be indicators of how residents are doing.

- **Follow up on voiced concerns or issues**  
  When residents share concerns or problems, follow up with them to see how things are progressing and whether additional support is needed.

- **Communicate concerns with CD/GSA**  
  Share any concerns you have about residents or community dynamics with the Community Director (CD) and/or Graduate Staff Assistant (GSA) so they are informed and can help address them.

- **Seek feedback from residents**  
  Proactively ask residents for feedback about their experience, community climate, and any needs or suggestions they may have.

**Sources:**
- `Creating Connections & Touch Points 25-26.pptx`

---

How to Identify What Your Residents Need

Understanding resident needs is an ongoing, proactive process. Staff should use multiple methods to learn what residents require in order to feel safe, supported, and successful in the community. This includes:

- **Direct Communication**
  - Ask residents individually how they are doing and what they need.
  - Hold regular conversations during informal interactions (e.g., in hallways, lounges, or at programs).
  - Encourage residents to share concerns, questions, and suggestions openly.

- **Observation**
  - Pay attention to resident behavior, participation, and mood.
  - Notice changes in routines, social patterns, or engagement that may indicate emerging needs.
  - Observe how residents interact with one another and with staff.

- **Community Feedback**
  - Use surveys, suggestion forms, or informal check-ins to gather input from residents.
  - Listen for recurring themes or repeated concerns that point to broader community needs.
  - Invite feedback after programs or floor meetings to understand what is working and what is missing.

- **Program and Resource Utilization**
  - Monitor attendance and engagement at programs and events to see what interests residents.
  - Track which resources (e.g., bulletin boards, online materials, referrals) residents use most often.
  - Note gaps where residents are not engaging and consider whether additional support or different approaches are needed.

- **Collaboration with Staff and Campus Partners**
  - Share observations with other residence life staff to identify patterns across the community.
  - Consult with campus partners (e.g., counseling, academic support, student activities) when you notice needs that extend beyond the residence hall.
  - Use information from conduct, maintenance, and incident reports to identify underlying resident needs.

By combining direct communication, careful observation, community feedback, program data, and collaboration with colleagues, staff can more accurately understand and respond to what residents need.

**Sources:**
- `Creating Connections & Touch Points 25-26.pptx`

---

### Community Building & Student Engagement
TOMMIE HIGH 5: HELPING STUDENTS FEEL SEEN, KNOWN, AND VALUED

The Tommie High 5 initiative is designed to help students feel seen, known, and valued by recognizing their efforts and positive contributions.

Example student response:
- “Thank you so much! This really made my day! I appreciate it!”

Example faculty advisor response:
- “xxxx, that is so cool! Thank you Dr. xxx for taking the time to express this!”

### Community Development & Engagement
Tommie Advantage Events: 1:1s, Community Communications, and Community Meetings

Tommie Advantage events include three key components that support community development and student engagement:

1. **1:1s (One-on-Ones)**  
   - Individual meetings between residence life staff and students.  
   - Used to build relationships, provide support, and connect students to resources.  

2. **Community Communications**  
   - Ongoing communication efforts within the residential community.  
   - May include emails, postings, digital messages, or other updates that share information, promote events, and reinforce community expectations.  

3. **Community Meetings**  
   - Group meetings held within the residential community.  
   - Provide a space to share information, discuss community issues, and foster connections among residents.

### Community Leader Role & Expectations
### Final Considerations for Community Leaders

As a leader in the community, you play a key role in shaping the environment around you. Keep the following in mind:

- **Model the connections you want to see.** Your behavior sets the tone; demonstrate the kinds of relationships and interactions you hope others will build.
- **Use curiosity to show you care.** Asking questions, listening actively, and seeking to understand others communicates genuine interest and concern.
- **Remember that it is okay to set boundaries.** Maintaining clear, healthy limits supports your well-being and helps you sustain your role over time.
- **You always have someone to lean on for support.** You are not alone in this work; reach out to your peers, supervisors, or other support resources whenever you need help or guidance.

**Sources:**
- `Creating Connections & Touch Points 25-26.pptx`

---

### Community Liaison & DEI Specialist Role
Community Liaison and DEI Specialist Role

The Community Liaison and DEI Specialist is responsible for leading community engagement initiatives and building trust with groups across campus. This role focuses on:

- Community Engagement Initiatives  
  - Developing and implementing engagement events that connect residence life with other campus groups.  

- Trust-Building with Campus Groups  
  - Establishing and maintaining relationships with student, staff, and faculty groups.  
  - Building trust through consistent, meaningful engagement and collaboration.  

- Education and Training  
  - Providing education and training opportunities to student, staff, and faculty groups on campus.  
  - Offering sessions such as CPR and First Aid classes as examples of these educational programs.

**Sources:**
- `2025 Res Life Presentation.pdf`

### Community Meetings & Engagement
If you have scheduled a community meeting (for example, on a Tuesday night at 7:00 p.m. in a classroom) and prepared thoroughly—reading through the lesson plan, creating the PowerPoint, planning an activity, and selecting the date and time based on resident feedback indicating that most residents were available—but you arrive and only 2 out of 36 residents are present on time, you must decide how to proceed in the moment.

In this situation, you should:

- Recognize that you have followed the planning expectations:  
  - You scheduled the meeting for a specific day and time (e.g., Tuesday at 7:00 p.m.).  
  - You selected a suitable location (e.g., a classroom).  
  - You reviewed the lesson plan in advance.  
  - You prepared the PowerPoint.  
  - You planned an activity.  
  - You chose the date and time based on what residents previously shared about their availability, and most indicated that this time worked for them.

- Acknowledge that attendance is significantly lower than expected (2 out of 36 residents present on time).

- Use the low-attendance scenario to guide your next steps, which may include:  
  - Proceeding with the meeting content for the residents who arrived on time, adapting the format as needed for a very small group.  
  - Engaging the two residents in conversation about why others may not have attended and whether the time, location, or communication methods were effective.  
  - Considering how you will follow up with the rest of the community (e.g., sharing key information later, rescheduling or offering an additional session, or adjusting future meeting times based on updated feedback).  

This scenario is intended to prompt you to think through how you respond when you have fully prepared and scheduled a community meeting based on resident input, but actual attendance (2 of 36 residents) does not match the expected turnout.

**Sources:**
- `Community Meetings SLED 2025 (1).pdf`

---

### Community Meetings & Engagement Strategies
Community Meetings: Planning and Communication Strategies

Use the following strategies when planning for and informing residents about your community meetings:

- Plan ahead and be intentional about meeting timing, location, and format so they are accessible and appealing to residents.
- Use multiple communication methods (e.g., flyers, posters, door signs, email, group chats, and in-person reminders) to reach as many residents as possible.
- Share meeting details clearly and early, including date, time, location, purpose, and any expectations for participation.
- Provide reminders as the meeting approaches, not just a single announcement.
- Communicate in ways that match your community’s preferences and habits (for example, using platforms or channels residents already use regularly).
- Frame the meeting in terms of its benefits to residents (such as opportunities for input, community-building, or receiving important information).
- Be consistent in how and when you announce meetings so residents know what to expect over time.

**Sources:**
- `Community Meetings SLED 2025 (1).pdf`

---

### Community Meetings & Facilitation
Community Meetings: Purpose, Planning, Facilitation, and Expectations

**1. Community Meeting Purpose**  
Community meetings are held to support and strengthen the residential community. They are used to share information, set expectations, address concerns, build relationships among residents, and create a space for open communication and feedback.

**2. Planning & Logistics**  
When preparing for a community meeting, plan and confirm all logistics in advance. This includes determining the meeting’s goals, selecting an appropriate date, time, and location, preparing any necessary materials or handouts, and ensuring residents are informed about the meeting details.

**3. Facilitation Strategies**  
Use intentional facilitation strategies to guide the meeting. This may include setting a clear agenda, establishing ground rules, encouraging participation from all residents, managing time effectively, and keeping the discussion focused on the meeting’s purpose and goals.

**4. Scenario Questions**  
Incorporate scenario questions into the meeting to prompt discussion and reflection. These questions should be relevant to residents’ experiences and can be used to explore possible situations, responses, and community expectations in a structured way.

**5. Expectations / What NOT to Do**  
Clearly communicate expectations for behavior and participation during community meetings. Also identify what not to do—for example, do not allow side conversations to dominate, do not ignore resident input, and do not let the meeting deviate from its stated purpose. Ensure that all actions and discussions remain respectful, inclusive, and aligned with community standards.

**Sources:**
- `Community Meetings SLED 2025 (1).pdf`

---

### Community Meetings & Programming
If you arrive at the reserved room for your community meeting and discover that the technology is not working at all—even though you have already:

- Reserved the room for your community meeting  
- Edited and personalized the slideshow to make it applicable to your wing  
- Brought eye masks as an activity  
- Sent reminders in your GroupMe  

you should proceed with the meeting using your non-technology-dependent plans and materials. Rely on the eye mask activity and any other parts of your meeting that do not require technology, and continue to engage your community as planned without the slideshow or electronic equipment.

**Sources:**
- `Community Meetings SLED 2025 (1).pdf`

---

### Community Meetings / Facilitation Guidelines
Avoid the following when planning and facilitating your meeting:

- Do not wait until the last minute to tell your residents about the meeting; avoid only informing them right before it takes place.  
- Do not arrive unprepared or attend the meeting without having reviewed the facilitation guide in advance.  
- Do not tell residents that the meetings “aren’t important” or “not a big deal.”  
- Do not make up an icebreaker on the spot or skip the icebreaker altogether.  
- Do not tell your supervisor you “got it” if you still have questions; ask for clarification instead.  
- Do not forget to submit the required form in Roompact (if applicable).  
- Do not tell residents who could not attend that they now do not get to know the information.  
- Do not approach the meeting with a bad attitude.

**Sources:**
- `Community Meetings SLED 2025 (1).pdf`

### Community Values & Expectations
How We Practice and Implement Our Values as Community Leaders

As community leaders, we are responsible for actively modeling, reinforcing, and integrating our shared values into everyday residence life. Practicing these values is not a one-time action, but an ongoing commitment reflected in how we interact with residents, colleagues, and the broader campus community. Implementation involves:

- Demonstrating our values consistently in daily behavior, communication, and decision-making.
- Using our values as a guide when responding to resident concerns, conflicts, and community issues.
- Incorporating our values into programs, events, and initiatives to help residents understand and experience them in action.
- Encouraging residents to reflect on these values and how they can apply them in their own lives and interactions.
- Holding ourselves and others accountable to these values in a respectful, constructive manner.
- Providing support, resources, and guidance that align with and reinforce our values.
- Regularly reflecting as a staff team on how well we are living out our values and identifying areas for improvement.

By intentionally applying our values in these ways, we help create a residence environment where expectations are clear, community standards are upheld, and all members feel supported in contributing to a positive, values-driven living experience.

**Sources:**
- `Creating Connections & Touch Points 25-26.pptx`

### Conflict Management & Communication
In conflict situations, the body, emotions, cognition, and behavior all respond in distinct ways. Physically, the body may exhibit various reactions to the triggering event, such as increased heart rate or tension. Emotionally, individuals experience specific feelings tied to the conflict, which can range from anger to anxiety. Cognitively, perceptions and thoughts about the conflict are formed, influencing how the situation is understood and processed. Behaviorally, actions are taken in response to the conflict, which can include confrontation or avoidance. The communication process in a conflict involves a sender and a receiver, each with their own filters that affect how the message is conveyed and interpreted, ultimately influencing the response.

**Sources:**
- `FY & SY Room. Agr. & Con. Res. RA Handout (2.a, 2.d, 3.b).pdf`

---

### Conflict Management / Mediation Basics
CONFLICT: A WHOLE-BODY EXPERIENCE

Conflict is a whole-body experience that involves four interconnected types of responses to a triggering event:

1. **Triggering Event**  
   - A specific situation, interaction, or incident that initiates or activates the experience of conflict.

2. **Physical Responses**  
   - How the body reacts to a conflict situation.  
   - May include changes such as increased heart rate, muscle tension, sweating, or other bodily sensations.

3. **Emotional Responses**  
   - The feelings experienced in conflict.  
   - Can include emotions such as anger, frustration, fear, anxiety, sadness, or defensiveness.

4. **Cognitive Responses**  
   - Perceptions and thoughts about conflict.  
   - Involves how individuals interpret the situation, assign meaning, make judgments, and form beliefs about what is happening and why.

5. **Behavioral Responses**  
   - Actions taken in response to conflict.  
   - Includes what a person says or does during or after the conflict situation, such as withdrawing, confronting, problem-solving, or escalating the interaction.

All four response areas—physical, emotional, cognitive, and behavioral—are activated by a triggering event and together shape how a person experiences and manages conflict.

**Sources:**
- `Roommate Agreement & Conflict Resolution Process.pdf`

---

### Conflict Resolution / Response Preparation
Prepare

As with any situation, preparation is important. Before responding, ask yourself these three questions:

1. What’s the problem?  
   - Example: A resident has come to you and shared that their roommate is not leaving their room and has not taken their trash out since the start of the semester.

2. How do I feel about it?

3. What do I want to be different?

(I, American Nurse Association [ANA], 2014)

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

6. **Follow Through**

### Core Values & Intercultural Development
Rooted in Dignity  
Core Value: Care & Compassion  

Care & Compassion  
- Leading with care and compassion means acknowledging the sacredness of every person and responding to their needs with empathy.  
- This value is rooted in the Catholic belief that each individual is made in the image of God and deserves to be treated with dignity and love.  
- Care and compassion remind us to uphold and honor the dignity of all people in our daily actions.  

Self-Reflection  
Use the following questions for personal reflection or group sharing:  
- Who taught you what dignity looks like in action?  
- Share about a time when someone treated you with deep care and respect.  
  - What did they do?  
  - What about it felt respectful to you?  

Intercultural Developmental Continuum (IDC)  
Mindset Activity & Small Group Discussion  

1. Small Group Instructions  
   - In your small groups, read the description of your assigned “mindset” on the Intercultural Developmental Continuum (IDC).  
   - Discuss the scenario question below from the perspective of that assigned “mindset.”  
   - Record your group’s thoughts.  
   - Select one reporter to share your ideas with the larger group.  

2. Scenario Practice  
   - Scenario: Imagine a student is upset after feeling ignored in a group meeting. As a student leader, how would you respond with empathy, from your assigned “mindset”?  

3. Individual Reflection  
   After the small group discussion, reflect on the assignment from your own (real) point of view and consider:  
   1. What advantages can you see to approaching this situation from the “mindset” of your assigned group?  
   2. What are the limitations or disadvantages of approaching it from this particular “mindset”?  

NOTES:  
Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC HO 2025.pdf`

### DEI & Identity Development
### 16. Reversal: Dualistic “Them vs. Us”

**Core Description**

The Reversal stage is a dualistic “them vs. us” orientation in which the usual us/them polarization is flipped:

- “They” (the out‑group) are seen as superior.
- “We” (one’s own identity group) are stereotyped in negative ways.
- There is an overemphasis on differences between oneself and some part of one’s own identity group.
- This can involve internalized oppression.
- It can also show up as performative allyship.

The primary emotional and cognitive experience in this stage is **shame**.

*(Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com)*

---

### Primary Developmental Learning Tasks in Reversal

At the Reversal stage, key learning tasks include:

- Recognizing the **stereotypical nature** of one’s perceptions of one’s own identity group(s).
- Exploring **commonalities** with one’s own identity group.
- Developing a **more complex lens** through which one’s own identity group(s) are viewed.

---

### Key Developmental Question

Individuals in the Reversal stage are invited to reflect on:

- **Can I view my own identities with more complexity?**  
- **Can I find commonality with the people and/or qualities I’d rather disavow?**

*(Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com)*

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

### 19. MINIMIZATION

**Core Characteristics**

- Exercise of power and acceptance of institutionalized privilege.
- Overemphasis on the use of positional power.
- Tendency to minimize or downplay the impact of racism and other forms of oppression.

**Primary Developmental Learning Tasks**

- Increasing cultural self-awareness.
- For individuals from the dominant culture:
  - Managing feelings of guilt, emotional discomfort, dissonance, and anxiety when dealing directly with issues of racism and other forms of oppression.
- For individuals from non-dominant cultures:
  - Re-grounding in one’s own identity and cultural self-awareness.

**Key Developmental Questions**

- How do I take responsibility for my cultural lens?
- How do I see what I currently don’t see?

**What Minimization Looks Like**

- Reliance on institutionalized privilege and positional power in interactions.
- Use of power in ways that obscure or downplay cultural differences and systemic inequities.
- Difficulty fully acknowledging the realities of racism and oppression, resulting in a tendency to minimize their impact.

_Source: Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com_

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

### DEI, Empathy & Communication Skills
### The Power of Perspective & Being Rooted in Dignity

“We cannot see what is ‘out there’ merely by looking around. Everything depends on the lenses through which we view the world. By putting on new lenses, we can see things that would otherwise remain invisible.”  
— Parker Palmer  

This section highlights how our perspectives—or “lenses”—shape what we notice, how we interpret situations, and how we respond to others. By intentionally “putting on new lenses,” we can recognize experiences and concerns that might otherwise remain invisible to us.

Being **rooted in dignity** means approaching others with the assumption that their experiences, feelings, and perspectives are worthy of respect, even when we disagree or do not fully understand. Honoring someone’s dignity requires us to:

- Acknowledge that our own perspective is limited by our lenses.
- Stay curious about how others see and experience the world.
- Respond in ways that affirm the other person’s worth, especially in moments of conflict or misunderstanding.

**Reflect:**  
- What does it mean to honor someone’s dignity when you disagree with them?  
- What does it mean to honor someone’s dignity when you don’t understand their perspective?

**Scenario Practice:**  
Imagine a student is upset after feeling ignored in a group meeting. As a student leader, how would you respond with empathy?

In this scenario, responding with empathy involves recognizing the student’s dignity, listening to their experience through a lens of curiosity rather than judgment, and seeking to understand how the situation felt from their point of view.

Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW  
www.bethzemsky.com

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

### Diversity & Inclusion / Intercultural Competence
### 12. Organizational Impact: Diversity and Inclusion

When diversity and inclusion are ignored, organizations often default to a “one size fits all” approach. This can be reinforced by:

- A reliance on perceived “best practices” that are assumed to work for everyone.
- Strong traditions that go unquestioned.
- A primary learning emphasis on basic cultural awareness rather than deeper skill-building.

These patterns can limit an organization’s ability to recognize and respond effectively to cultural differences.

### Cultural Awareness and the IDC Distribution

The Intercultural Development Continuum (IDC) describes stages of intercultural sensitivity, moving from ethnocentric to ethnorelative orientations. The stages are:

- **Ethnocentric Stages**
  - **Denial** – Limited recognition of cultural differences; begin to notice cultural differences.
  - **Defense**
  - **Minimization**

- **Ethnorelative Stages**
  - **Acceptance**
  - **Adaptation**

A sample IDC distribution shows the following percentages across stages:

- **Denial:** 3.05%  
- **Defense:** 15.55%  
- **Minimization:** 65.25%  
- **Acceptance:** 14.65%  
- **Adaptation:** 1.55%

This distribution illustrates that the majority of individuals are in **Minimization**, with relatively few in **Denial** or **Adaptation**. Understanding where individuals and groups fall on this continuum is critical for planning effective diversity and inclusion efforts and for moving from basic cultural awareness toward more advanced intercultural competence.

_Source: Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com_

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

### Diversity & Inclusion / Intercultural Development
**Stage 22: Acceptance – Resident Advisors (IDI Profile, N = 80)**  
Source: Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com (slides 41, 47, 48, 56)

---

### Organizational Impact
- Characterized by **curiosity and exploration** of cultural differences.  
- Demonstrates **cognitive understanding of cultural perspectives** of some cultures.  
- May involve **“talking the talk” without fully integrating the “walk”** (i.e., understanding concepts without consistently aligning behavior and practice).

---

### Best Practice
- Use **community-based assessments and research** to inform practice, programming, and decision-making.

---

### Primary Learning Emphasis
- Provide **culturally specific information** that is directly connected to **practical application**.  
  - Emphasize **practice and skill-building** (“Practice!!!”) rather than only conceptual learning.  
- Support the **ability to maintain ethical commitment in the face of relativity**, i.e., holding clear ethical standards while recognizing and navigating multiple cultural perspectives.

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

### Diversity & Intercultural Competence
### The Iceberg Model of Culture and Intercultural Competency

The Iceberg Model of Culture illustrates that only a small portion of culture is visible “above the surface,” while a much larger portion operates primarily out of awareness.

#### Primarily in Awareness (Visible Aspects of Culture)
These are the more obvious, surface-level elements of culture that people tend to notice first:

- Fine arts  
- Food  
- Clothing  
- Language  
- Traditional dances  
- Cultural symbols  
- Facial expressions  
- Arrangement of physical space  

These visible aspects represent only a small part of the overall cultural “iceberg.”

#### Primarily Out of Awareness (Below the Surface)
Most cultural patterns operate implicitly and are not immediately visible. These include:

- Incentives to work  
- Concept of justice  
- Patterns of relations  
- Patterns of handling emotions  
- Patterns of group decision making  
- Conception of cleanliness  
- Conception of status mobility  
- Eye behavior  
- Attitudes toward the dependent  
- Approaches to problem solving  
- Ordering of time  
- Patterns of handling conflict  
- Body language  
- Roles related to status by age, sex, class, occupation, kinship, etc.  
- Notions of leadership  
- Nature of friendship  
- Conception of past and future  
- Tempo of work  
- Theory of disease  
- Notions of logic and validity  
- Preference for competition or cooperation  
- Social interaction rate  
- Patterns of communication  
- And much more that shapes how people think, feel, and behave  

These “below the surface” elements strongly influence how individuals interpret and respond to situations, often without conscious awareness.

---

### Intercultural Competency

Intercultural competency involves developing both deeper cultural self-understanding and deeper cultural other-understanding.

#### Deeper Cultural Self-Understanding
This is the ability to recognize and reflect on how one’s own culturally learned perceptions, values, and practices shape:

- How one makes sense of cultural differences  
- How one responds to cultural differences  

It requires awareness of the “below the surface” aspects of one’s own culture as described in the Iceberg Model.

#### Deeper Cultural Other-Understanding
This is the ability to recognize and understand:

- The different ways people from other cultural groups make sense of cultural difference  
- The different ways they respond to cultural difference  

It involves appreciating that others’ perceptions, values, and practices are also shaped by their own “below the surface” cultural patterns.

---

*Adapted from materials by Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW (www.bethzemsky.com).*

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

### Diversity, Equity & Inclusion
Illustration Description: Identity Wheel and Key Concepts

This illustration depicts a wheel with 11 spokes, each representing a different aspect of identity:

1. Ethnicity  
2. Socio-economic status  
3. Gender  
4. Sex  
5. Sexual orientation  
6. National origin  
7. First language  
8. Physical/emotional/developmental (dis)ability  
9. Age  
10. Religious or spiritual affiliation  
11. Race  

© Recipes for Wellbeing

The wheel is used to explore and reflect on:

- **Dignity** – recognizing the inherent worth of each person across all identity dimensions.  
- **Salient Identities** – understanding which aspects of identity are most prominent or significant for an individual in a given context.

**Sources:**
- `Overview of SDIS.pdf`

---

### Intercultural Competency

Intercultural competency is a developmental process focused on how we make sense of our experiences across cultures. It involves:

- Developing the capacity to experience commonalities and differences with increasing complexity.  
- Building the capacity to shift both behavior and mindset based on the cultural context.

This ability to adjust one’s behavior and thinking in response to different cultural contexts is referred to as **adaptation** and **cultural agility**.  
(Source: Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com)

---

### Diversity: “The Differences That Make a Difference”

**Diversity** refers to the differences that make a difference. It includes:

- The numerical representation of differing people, viewpoints, and experiences.  
- The idea that you can “count the people” to understand who is represented and who is not.

Planning question for your context:

- **What are some of the “differences that may make a difference” in your context?**

(Source: Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.beth.zemsky.com)

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

Cultural and Identity Considerations

When supporting residents, be intentional about cultural and identity factors that may shape their experiences and comfort with seeking help.

- Honor and consistently use each person’s self-identified pronouns.
- Be aware of any privilege you hold by virtue of race, gender, sexual orientation, ethnicity, or religious tradition, and how that privilege may affect interactions with residents.
- Consider the impact of racism, homophobia, and transphobia on residents’ well-being, help‑seeking behavior, and trust in staff or institutions.
- Recognize that some cultures may have different beliefs about, or stigma surrounding, seeking help for emotional distress, and approach these situations with sensitivity and respect.

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

---

### Diversity, Equity & Inclusion (DEI) Concepts
### 18. Minimization: Overemphasis on Commonality and Similarity  

**Core Characteristics**

- Focus is on minimizing differences and emphasizing how people are alike.  
- Interactions are “comfortable” and “insistently nice,” prioritizing ease over addressing real differences.  
- There is a conscious effort not to denigrate others and to avoid stereotypes.  
- Individuals have moved beyond experiencing culture as a threat.  
- A common belief is: “Deep down, we’re all the same.”  
- One’s own cultural lens is used as the primary reference point.  
- One’s own cultural lens and assumptions are projected onto others.  

**Primary Emotional/Cognitive Approach**

- The dominant approach is **comfort and tolerance**, rather than active engagement with cultural difference.  

*(Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com)*  

---

### 39. Minimization: Organizational Impact  

**Hiring and Onboarding**

- Organizations may **hire for difference but onboard for similarity**, emphasizing comfort and fit with the dominant culture.  
- There is pressure for individuals to **assimilate to the dominant culture**.  

**Bias, Conflict, and Culture**

- **Implicit bias** contributes to **micro-aggressions**.  
- The organization tends to be **conflict avoidant**.  
- Organizational processes are:  
  - Designed from the perspective of the **current organizational culture**, and  
  - Structured to **support** that existing culture.  

**Change and Best Practices**

- There is a desire for **change** without changing anything about **ourselves**.  
- “Best practices” are often defined through:  
  - Use of **“expert” data** that assesses **generally accepted/dominant cultural standards**.  

*(Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.beth.zemsky.com)*

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

### Diversity, Equity & Inclusion (DEI) Training
### Intercultural Developmental Continuum (IDC)  
Adapted from Dr. Mitch Hammer | Summary by Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW (www.bethzemsky.com)

The Intercultural Developmental Continuum (IDC) describes stages of how individuals and organizations experience and respond to cultural difference. Each stage includes a description, key interventions or skills, and organizational implications.

---

#### 1. Denial
**Description**  
- Orientation is primarily to what is familiar.  
- Individuals are comfortable with the familiar and have limited awareness of cultural difference.  
- There is a possibility of exploitation when cultural differences are not perceived or understood.

**Intervention / Skill Focus**  
- Build awareness through exposure to cultural difference.

**Organizational Implications**  
- Risk that people and communities may be exploited or harmed because difference is not recognized or considered.

---

#### 2. Defense: Polarization & Reversal
**Description**  
- Dualistic “us vs. them” or “them vs. us” thinking.  
- Strong emphasis on difference framed in oppositional terms.  
- Commonality is often ignored or minimized.  
- Stereotyping is common, along with denial of the consequences of discrimination.

**Intervention / Skill Focus**  
- Emphasize and explore commonality while still acknowledging difference.

**Organizational Implications**  
- Stereotypes and denial of discrimination’s impact can shape decisions, relationships, and practices.

---

#### 3. Minimization
**Description**  
- Overemphasis on commonality across cultures.  
- Tendency to downplay or overlook meaningful cultural differences.

**Intervention / Skill Focus**  
- Develop cultural self-awareness, including awareness of one’s own cultural norms, assumptions, and experiences.  
- Increase awareness of institutionalized privilege and how it operates.

**Organizational Implications**  
- Organizational focus is on **Diversity** (e.g., representation, numbers) without fully addressing power, privilege, or systemic inequities.

---

#### 4. Acceptance
**Description**  
- Recognition and valuing of cultural differences.  
- Curiosity about culture-specific information and perspectives.  
- Ability to analyze the implications of power and privilege, but with limited action; this can result in “liberal paralysis” (understanding without change).

**Intervention / Skill Focus**  
- Deepen curiosity about specific cultures and contexts.  
- Strengthen capacity to connect analysis of power and privilege to concrete behavior and decision-making.

**Organizational Implications**  
- Organizational focus is on **Inclusion** (e.g., creating environments where differences are acknowledged and welcomed), though systemic change may still be limited.

---

#### 5. Adaptation
**Description**  
- Ability to shift perspective and behavior in response to cultural context.  
- Demonstrated empathy and frame-shifting (seeing from another cultural standpoint).  
- Ability to recognize and respond to power in context.

**Intervention / Skill Focus**  
- Practice empathy and frame-shifting in real interactions.  
- Intentionally adjust behavior and communication across cultural differences.

**Organizational Implications**  
- Organizational focus is on **Equity**.  
- Changes in policy and practice are made to address power, privilege, and systemic inequities in concrete, ongoing ways.

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC HO 2025.pdf`

---

### Diversity, Equity & Inclusion / Intercultural Competence
### Adaptation

**Mindset: Ability to shift perspective while maintaining one’s own identity and values**

Adaptation involves developing:

- **Cultural self-awareness and curiosity**  
  - Ongoing reflection on one’s own cultural background, assumptions, and values.  
  - Active interest in learning about other cultures and perspectives.

- **Development of empathy and analysis**  
  - Capacity to understand and share the feelings and experiences of people from different cultural contexts.  
  - Ability to analyze situations through multiple cultural lenses without abandoning one’s core values.

- **Primary emotional/cognitive approach**  
  - Flexibility in thinking and feeling when encountering new or unfamiliar situations.  
  - Tolerance for ambiguity, including comfort with not having immediate clarity or certainty.

---

**Skillset: Behavioral Code Shifting**

Adaptation also requires concrete behavioral skills, including:

- **Development of communication skills that enable mutually beneficial interactions across culture**  
  - Adjusting language, tone, and style to communicate effectively with people from different cultural backgrounds.  
  - Focusing on outcomes that work for all parties involved.

- **Ability to shift behavior based on context**  
  - Reading the cultural and situational context and modifying behavior accordingly.  
  - Doing so in a way that remains authentic and aligned with one’s own identity and values.

---

**Guiding Questions for Adaptation**

In practicing adaptation, continually consider:

- **What is our goal?**  
  - Clarify the purpose and desired outcomes in a given cross-cultural interaction or situation.

- **What is our role in relation to this goal?**  
  - Identify responsibilities, influence, and limits within the context.

- **What do we do without losing our soul?**  
  - Determine how to adapt perspectives and behaviors while preserving core identity, integrity, and values.

---

*Adaptation concepts referenced from: Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com / www.beth.zemsky.com*

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

### Diversity, Equity & Inclusion Development
### Stage: Defense

At the Defense stage, diversity and inclusion are often perceived as problems and obstacles rather than as strengths. This can result in:

- **Organizational impact**
  - Diversity and inclusion are seen as difficulties to be managed rather than as benefits.
  - Culture segregation develops based on a perceived need for protection of one’s own group.
  - Backlash can occur, leading to policy and practice retrenchment (pulling back from or weakening prior diversity and inclusion efforts).

- **Perception of “best practice”**
  - There is reliance on “expert” data that:
    - Overemphasizes risk and cost.
    - Stresses differences between one’s own group and others.
  - Cognitive patterns such as:
    - **Confirmation bias** (seeking information that confirms existing beliefs).
    - **Backfire effect** (becoming more entrenched in beliefs when presented with contradictory information).

- **Exercise of power**
  - Denial of the consequences of discrimination.
  - Opposition to attempts to provide equal opportunity and to other efforts to remedy past discrimination.
  - A “blame the victim” mentality.
  - Overemphasis on the use of **coercive power**.

- **Primary developmental learning tasks**
  - Support people in finding commonalities where they believe none exist.
  - Use cooperative activities and team-building.
  - Implement processes that help individuals and groups discover shared needs and goals.

- **Key developmental question**
  - “How do I find anything in common between me and them?”

_Source: Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW — www.beth.zemsky.com_

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

### Diversity, Equity & Inclusion Training
MINDSETS ABOUT CULTURE AND DIFFERENCE  
(Adapted from Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com)

This framework outlines several common mindsets people may hold about culture, difference, and interaction across cultural backgrounds. Each mindset is presented as a distinct perspective; individuals or groups may move among these over time or hold elements of more than one at once.

**Mindset: Group A**  
“I get that cultural differences exist, and I certainly don’t want to offend anyone! However, since we’re all essentially the same under the surface, it makes more sense to focus on what we have in common.”

- Recognizes that cultural differences exist.  
- Expresses a desire not to offend others.  
- Emphasizes the belief that “we’re all essentially the same under the surface.”  
- Prioritizes focusing on commonalities rather than differences.

**Mindset: Group B**  
“I’m not really interested in experiencing ‘cultural differences.’ I’m fine just the way I am, thanks.”

- Shows little or no interest in engaging with or experiencing cultural differences.  
- Expresses satisfaction with one’s current way of being (“I’m fine just the way I am”).  

**Mindset: Group C**  
“Some cultures and points of view are obviously more right than others. If you deny that, you’re just being dishonest.”

- Asserts that some cultures and viewpoints are “obviously more right” than others.  
- Views disagreement with this hierarchy as dishonest.  

**Mindset: Group D**  
“I have my own unique cultural background, and so does everyone else. No one point of view is better than another; the more different voices we can include, the better off we’ll be.”

- Recognizes that everyone, including oneself, has a unique cultural background.  
- Holds that no single point of view is better than another.  
- Values inclusion of many different voices.  
- Believes that including more diverse perspectives makes the group “better off.”

**Mindset: Group E**  
“I am not really proud of my culture. Nor am I proud of members of my culture. We do so much harm – and have for a very long time. I feel very strongly that other cultures are in a much better place than my culture and we could really learn from them.”

- Expresses a lack of pride in one’s own culture and in members of that culture.  
- Emphasizes that one’s culture has done “so much harm – and have for a very long time.”  
- Believes other cultures are “in a much better place” than one’s own culture.  
- Strongly feels that one’s culture could “really learn from” other cultures.

**Mindset: Group F**  
“It’s important to approach people from where they are, every time. The last thing I want to do is to unknowingly force my cultural view on someone else!”

- Prioritizes meeting people “from where they are” in every interaction.  
- Intentionally seeks to avoid imposing or “unknowingly forc[ing]” one’s own cultural view on others.

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC HO 2025.pdf`

---

### Intercultural Development Continuum: Primary Orientations

The Intercultural Development Continuum describes a progression of primary orientations toward cultural difference. It is modified from the Developmental Model of Intercultural Sensitivity (DMIS), developed by M. Bennett in 1986, and further articulated in the Intercultural Goals & Challenges framework (RG 31–43) by Mitchell R. Hammer, Ph.D. (Copyright 2007–2016). Related training and materials are also associated with Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW (www.bethzemsky.com).

The continuum moves from a **monocultural mindset** to an **intercultural mindset**, with the following primary orientations:

1. **Denial**  
   - Orientation: Monocultural mindset  
   - Core stance: *Misses difference*  
   - Individuals at this stage tend not to recognize or perceive cultural differences.

2. **Polarization**  
   - Orientation: Monocultural mindset  
   - Core stance: *Judges difference*  
   - Individuals at this stage recognize cultural differences but evaluate them in “better–worse” or “us–them” terms.

3. **Minimization**  
   - Orientation: Transitional between monocultural and intercultural mindsets  
   - Core stance: *De-emphasizes difference*  
   - Individuals at this stage acknowledge some differences but tend to downplay their importance, emphasizing similarity instead.

4. **Acceptance**  
   - Orientation: Intercultural mindset  
   - Core stance: *Deeply comprehends difference*  
   - Individuals at this stage understand and appreciate cultural differences in values, perceptions, and behaviors.

5. **Adaptation**  
   - Orientation: Intercultural mindset  
   - Core stance: *Bridges across difference*  
   - Individuals at this stage can shift perspectives and adapt behavior to communicate and interact effectively across cultural differences.

Across this continuum, growth involves increasing **ability to perceive** cultural differences and to respond to them more effectively.

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

### Diversity, Equity, and Inclusion Framework
**Diversity, Inclusion, Equity, and Intercultural Competence**

This framework outlines how diversity, inclusion, equity, and intercultural competence relate to one another and how each is measured and experienced within our community.

---

### 1. Commonality and Difference

Community dynamics often move along a spectrum that includes:

- **Connection vs. Fragmentation**
  - Connection and engagement versus fragmentation and an “us vs. them” mentality.
- **Innovation vs. Conformity/Assimilation**
  - Innovation and engagement versus conformity and assimilation.

Understanding where a community falls on this spectrum helps identify whether differences are being leveraged constructively or suppressed.

---

### 2. Diversity: “The Mix” of Differences

- **Definition:**  
  Diversity refers to the “mix” of differences present in a community (e.g., identities, backgrounds, perspectives).

- **Primary Focus:**  
  The *impact* of differences.

- **How It Is Measured:**  
  - Demographic analysis.  
  - “Count the people” to understand who is represented and in what proportions.

---

### 3. Inclusion: The Experience of the Mix

- **Definition:**  
  Inclusion is about the “mix” feeling valued and engaged.

- **Primary Focus:**  
  The *experience* of people within the community.

- **How It Is Measured:**  
  - Satisfaction.  
  - Engagement.  
  - Retention.

- **Key Idea:**  
  - “The People Count” – not just who is present, but whether they feel they belong and are actively involved.

---

### 4. Equity: Outcomes and Disparities

- **Definition:**  
  Equity focuses on addressing disparities and ensuring fair outcomes across different groups.

- **Primary Focus:**  
  - Addressing disparities.  
  - Being outcome-focused.

- **How It Is Measured:**  
  - Goal attainment.  
  - “The Outcomes Count” – whether different groups are achieving comparable results.

---

### 5. Intercultural Competence: Making the Mix Work

- **Definition:**  
  Intercultural competence is the “how” of making the mix work—our capacity to navigate and effectively bridge cultural differences.

- **Primary Focus:**  
  Building *capacity* to work across differences.

- **How It Is Measured:**  
  - Intercultural Development Inventory (IDI).

---

### 6. How These Elements Work Together

- **Diversity** = The “mix” of differences (who is here).  
- **Inclusion** = The “mix” feeling valued and engaged (how people experience being here).  
- **Equity** = Addressing disparities and focusing on outcomes (what results people get).  
- **Intercultural Competence** = The capacity to make the mix work (how effectively we bridge differences).

These elements are interrelated:

- **Diversity + Inclusion + Equity + Intercultural Competence** lead to:
  - Greater capacity to connect with families and communities.
  - More positive work experiences.
  - Increased ability to attract and retain more diverse talent.
  - Greater capacity to navigate and effectively bridge cultural differences.

---

*Adapted from materials by Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com*

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

### Diversity, Inclusion, and Mission Integration
**In This Together: Glossary of Terms**

**Accompaniment**  
The act of walking with others in their unique journeys with empathy, dignity, and presence. Rooted in the “art of accompaniment” taught by Pope Francis, it reflects the Church’s call to honor each person’s experience, support their growth, and foster belonging through compassionate relationship and mutual respect.

**Belonging**  
A feeling of being accepted, included, and valued by a group or community. In Catholic tradition, belonging reflects our call to communion and unity with others.

**Catholic Social Teaching**  
A body of doctrine developed by the Catholic Church on matters of social justice, human dignity, and the common good in society.

**Common Good**  
The benefit or interests of all people in society. Catholic social teaching emphasizes working for the good of all, especially the marginalized.

**Culture of Encounter**  
A Catholic call to move beyond superficial exchanges and genuinely engage with others through listening, empathy, and relationship. As Pope Francis says, this involves “listening to one another, sharing different views, coming to know each other and understanding one another.” Dialogue becomes a sacred space of bridge-building and love in a divided world.

**Dialogue**  
Respectful and open communication that promotes understanding across differences, rooted in the belief that every person has inherent dignity.

**Dignity**  
The belief that every person has inherent worth because they are made in the image of God. This is a central theme in Catholic teaching.

**Inclusion**  
The intentional act of creating spaces where all individuals feel welcomed, respected, and supported.

**Justice**  
Promoting fairness, equity, and respect for all, especially those who are disadvantaged or marginalized. In the Catholic context, justice includes both charity and structural change.

**Leadership as Service**  
A model of leadership focused on serving others, uplifting voices, and advancing the mission of justice and dignity.

**Principles of Good Practice**  
Guidelines created to align student affairs work at Catholic colleges and universities with Catholic values, emphasizing inclusion, dignity, and dialogue.

**Solidarity**  
Standing with others in mutual respect and support, especially with marginalized or oppressed communities. Catholic teaching encourages acting as one human family.

**Sources:**
- `In This Together_ Glossary of Terms_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

---

### Educational Plan & Learning Strategies
Residence Life Educational Plan  

This plan outlines how, when, and by what means Residence Life staff facilitate and measure student learning in the residential community.

---

### 1. Strategies: HOW We Facilitate Learning  

Residence Life staff facilitate learning through multiple, intentional strategies:

- **1:1 Conversations**  
  Individual conversations between staff and residents focused on personal, academic, and community development.

- **Touch Base Meetings (NEW)**  
  Newly implemented, brief check-ins designed to maintain ongoing connection and support between staff and residents.

- **Community Meetings**  
  Group meetings with residents on a floor, wing, or community to share information, build community, and address collective needs.

- **Events**  
  Planned programs and activities that promote learning, engagement, and connection among residents.

- **Roommate Agreement & Conflict Resolution**  
  Guided processes to help roommates establish expectations, address concerns, and resolve conflicts constructively.

- **Community Communications**  
  Intentional messaging (e.g., emails, postings, digital communications) used to share information, reinforce learning, and support community expectations.

---

### 2. Sequencing: WHEN We Facilitate Learning  

Learning is facilitated according to a planned sequence that aligns with:

- **Student Populations**  
  - First-Year (FY) students  
  - Second-Year / Upper-Division (SY/UD) students  

- **Academic Calendar & Student Needs**  
  Timing of strategies is coordinated with key points in the academic calendar and anticipated student needs (e.g., transitions, exams, breaks).

- **Tommie Advantage Dates**  
  Specific dates and timelines are aligned with Tommie Advantage.  
  - See Tommie Advantage Dates for detailed scheduling and alignment.

---

### 3. Lesson Plans: FACILITATING Learning  

Lesson plans provide a structured, step-by-step guide for implementing learning strategies. Each lesson plan:

- Offers a **step-by-step guide to facilitate learning**.
- Focuses on **one specific part of the learning outcomes** at a time.
- Specifies:
  - **Which strategy** is being used (e.g., 1:1 conversation, event, community meeting).
  - **Who is facilitating** (e.g., RA, professional staff).
  - **How to prepare** (materials, timing, communication, space, etc.).
  - **What needs to happen after** the interaction or program (follow-up, documentation, referrals, continued engagement).

---

### 4. Assessment: MEASURING Learning  

Assessment determines whether learning has occurred and to what extent:

- **Purpose**  
  - To determine: *Did the learning happen and to what degree?*

- **Types of Assessment**  
  - **Direct** measures (evidence that demonstrates learning explicitly).  
  - **Indirect** measures (evidence that suggests or reflects learning, such as perceptions or self-reports).

- **Tools**  
  - Rubrics are used to guide and standardize assessment.  
  - See Rubrics for specific criteria and measurement tools.

**Sources:**
- `Tommie Advantage Overview.pdf`

### Equity & Inclusion Foundations
### Universal Design: Integrating Equity and Justice in Residence Life

Universal Design is an approach to planning and practice that integrates equity and justice, originating from the Disability Rights Movement. The core principle is that when we intentionally design for those who are most marginalized, we create environments, systems, and processes that ultimately benefit everyone.

The key steps in applying Universal Design are:
- Determine who is currently most marginalized in a given context.
- Identify the specific needs of these people and communities.
- Design strategies using an equity lens to increase access and remove structural barriers.

When planning using a Universal Design framework, consistently return to these guiding questions:
- Who is currently most marginalized in this context?
- How do we hold these people and communities at the center of our planning?

This work is grounded in an understanding of culture. Culture is about shared patterns that help us make meaning of our environment and determine appropriate behavior. It is often experienced as “it’s just the way we do things around here.”

(Concepts adapted from Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com)

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

### Event Assessment & Reflection
Reflect on the experience by considering several key aspects. Begin by identifying what went well and what you can celebrate. Assess what you learned from the event and evaluate the attendance, analyzing the reasons behind the level of turnout. Finally, determine what changes you should implement for future events to enhance their success.

**Sources:**
- `RL-Residential Connection Events1 (1).pdf`

### Event Planning & Programming
Event Resource: Considerations for Inclusive Event Planning  

Inclusive event planning begins with intentional decisions at every stage of the process. Use the following considerations when planning events.

**1. Event Planning Team**  
- Consider who is on your planning team.  
- Invite individuals who can provide a diverse range of input.  
- A culturally competent and humble team will think to ask the kinds of questions and utilize resources needed for an inclusive event.

**2. Selecting a Date and Time**  
- Consider cultural festivals and religious observances when scheduling your events.  
- Ask whether the time of your event is feasible for everyone you hope will attend.

**3. Choosing a Location**  
When evaluating potential venues, consider whether the space is accessible, comfortable, and not exclusive to others.

Ask: Is the venue accessible and comfortable for those with special mobility, sight, hearing, and/or neurodiversity needs? Could the potential venue be exclusive to others?

Specifically review:  
- Entrances and elevators  
- Accessible parking and transportation  
- Quiet/sensory spaces  
- Temperature and lighting control  
- Single user restrooms  
- Lactation rooms  
- Captioning on media  
- Any religious affiliations of the venue  

**4. Food and Drinks**  
- If your event has food or drinks, include these considerations as part of your overall planning for inclusivity.

**Sources:**
- `Event Resource_ Considerations for Inclusive Event Planning_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### Hall Prep, Opening & Welcome Days
**Hall Prep, Opening, & Welcome Days – Session BCD (Day 1 & Day 2)**

During Session BCD on Day 1 and Day 2 of Hall Prep, Opening, and Welcome Days, staff will:

1. **Practice Addressing Concerns**  
   - Engage in activities focused on practicing how to address resident concerns effectively and professionally.

2. **Title IX, Bias Incidents, and Bystander Intervention Session**  
   - Participate in a session covering:
     - Title IX responsibilities and related procedures.  
     - Identifying and responding to bias incidents.  
     - Bystander intervention strategies and expectations.

3. **Roommate Agreements & Conflict Resolution Process Session**  
   - Attend a session on:
     - Establishing and facilitating roommate agreements.  
     - Understanding and implementing the conflict resolution process.

4. **Personal Commitment to Advocacy**  
   - Reflect on and state a personal commitment to advocacy using the prompt:  
     - **“My Commitment: One way I will advocate for others is:”**  
   - This commitment is grounded in the guiding quote:  
     - **“Justice is what love looks like in public.” — Dr. Cornel West**

### Hall Preparation, Opening & Welcome Days Student Leader Responsibilities
**Hall Preparation, Opening, and Welcome Days: Student Leader Education & Development – August 2025**

This document outlines the procedures and responsibilities for student leaders during the Hall Preparation, Opening, and Welcome Days in August 2025. It serves as a guide to ensure a smooth transition for new and returning students as they settle into residence life.

**Hall Preparation:**
- Student leaders are required to participate in training sessions designed to equip them with the necessary skills and knowledge for effective leadership.
- Leaders must ensure all residence halls are clean, organized, and ready for incoming students. This includes checking communal areas, ensuring safety protocols are in place, and reporting any maintenance issues.

**Opening Days:**
- Leaders are responsible for assisting with the check-in process, which includes verifying student information, distributing keys, and providing orientation materials.
- They should be available to answer questions and provide guidance to students and their families, ensuring a welcoming and supportive environment.

**Welcome Days:**
- Student leaders will facilitate various activities aimed at helping students acclimate to their new environment. These activities are designed to foster community building and engagement.
- Leaders are expected to be approachable and proactive in addressing any concerns or issues that may arise during this period.

Overall, student leaders play a crucial role in the successful execution of Hall Preparation, Opening, and Welcome Days. Their involvement is essential in creating a positive and inclusive atmosphere for all residents.

**Sources:**
- `Hall Prep, Opening, & Welcome Days.pdf`
