### Having Difficult Conversations with Residents
When initiating a conversation with a resident about a concern or behavior, clearly and specifically describe what you have observed. For example:

“I notice that you haven’t taken your trash out since the semester started and there is a smell coming from your room. How are you doing, and how can I help?” (ANA, 2014; Bower & Bower, 2004; Goh, 2020)

This approach names the concrete behavior (e.g., not taking out trash, noticeable odor) and then immediately invites the resident to share how they are doing and what support they may need.

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

---

### Helping Students in Distress
Helping Students in Distress

When supporting a student who appears to be in distress, follow these guidelines:

- Stay calm. Maintain your composure so you can respond thoughtfully and help the student feel more at ease.  
- Be respectful of individual culture and identities. Approach each student with sensitivity to their background, experiences, and self-identification.  
- Listen. Give the student your full attention, allowing them to share their concerns without interruption.  
- Talk. Engage in a calm, open conversation to better understand what the student is experiencing.  
- Extend empathy. Acknowledge the student’s feelings and validate their experience without judgment.  
- Maintain boundaries. Offer support within your role, but do not take on responsibilities beyond your position or training.  
- Refer. When appropriate, direct the student to additional resources that can provide further help.  
- Consult with your Community Director or CAPS. If you are unsure how to proceed, or if the situation seems serious, consult with your Community Director or Counseling and Psychological Services (CAPS) for guidance and support.

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

### In This Together / Social Justice & Inclusion
**In This Together: Agents of Justice**

**Theme:** Advancing the Common Good  
**Core Values:** Justice & Solidarity; Education for the Common Good  

Justice and solidarity are central to Catholic social teaching. We are called to stand with those who are marginalized and to work toward systems that promote the flourishing of all. Principle 4 (https://stthomas.instructure.com/courses/84343/files/10566170?wrap=1) (https://stthomas.instructure.com/courses/84343/files/10566170/download?download_frd=1) challenges us to confront inequality with compassion and action. Student leaders must learn to identify and respond to the ways in which our communities may fall short of these ideals.

Refer to the **In This Together: Glossary of Terms** (https://stthomas.instructure.com/courses/84343/pages/in-this-together-glossary-of-terms) as needed to support your understanding and use of shared language.

**Student Leader Role and Responsibilities**

- Listen critically and pay careful attention in your community to notice how identity might be impacting student experiences.  
- When a resident tells you about, or you yourself notice, a barrier to accessibility in our community, in our physical buildings, or in our policies, your role is to share this information with your CD/GSA.  
- Your CD/GSA will in turn notify the Associate Director and Director of Residence Life.  
- By sharing this information, you ensure that the people who can address the concern are aware and that a discussion can be opened about what to do.

**Reflection & Discussion Prompts**

- How will you listen critically and pay attention carefully in your community to notice how identity might be impacting student experiences?

**Connection in SLED**

During August SLED, we cover advancing the common good through justice, solidarity, and education during:

- Duty Expectations Session  
- Policies & Procedures Session

### In This Together / Values & Philosophy
### In This Together: Rooted in Dignity  
**Theme:** Leading with the Dignity of the Human Person  
**Core Values:** Care & Compassion  

#### What It Means

Leading with care and compassion means:

- Acknowledging the sacredness of every person.  
- Responding to others’ needs with empathy.  
- Recognizing that each individual is made in the image of God and deserves to be treated with dignity and love, in alignment with Catholic belief.  

This approach aligns with **Principle 4**, which calls us to uphold and honor the dignity of all people in our daily actions. For additional context and shared language, refer to the **In This Together: Glossary of Terms** (https://stthomas.instructure.com/courses/84343/pages/in-this-together-glossary-of-terms). You can review Principle 4 at:  
- https://stthomas.instructure.com/courses/84343/files/10566170?wrap=1  
- https://stthomas.instructure.com/courses/84343/files/10566170/download?download_frd=1  

#### Reflection & Discussion Prompts

Use the following prompts for personal reflection, supervision conversations, staff meetings, or training discussions:

- What does being treated with dignity and love look like in action? How do you know? Who taught you?  
- Describe a time when someone treated you with deep care and respect.  
- What does it mean to honor someone’s dignity across differences in experience, identity, culture, or opinion?  
- Imagine a resident comes to you upset because they feel their roommate is ignoring them due to their experience or identity. How would you respond with empathy in that moment?

> **Note:** There are specific procedures you may need to follow once you learn this kind of information from a resident. For the purposes of this reflection, focus on how you would respond with empathy in the moment, not on the procedural steps.

#### Connection in SLED

During **August SLED**, we emphasize leading with the dignity of every human person through care and compassion in nearly all sessions, including those facilitated by offices across campus. This theme—“In This Together: Rooted in Dignity”—is woven throughout August SLED programming and is given particular focus in designated sessions that highlight how to lead with dignity, care, and compassion in residence life contexts.

### Inclusion & Equity Principles
**Inclusion and Equity in Decision-Making and Program Planning**  
(Adapted from materials by Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com / www.beth.zemsky.com)

---

### Inclusion: Embracing Differences

**Definition**

Inclusion refers to the actions, behaviors, policies, and practices that ensure the differing experiences and perspectives of “diverse” people are actively taken into account. It emphasizes that **people count** and that their varied viewpoints are intentionally considered in all aspects of our work.

**Core Elements**

- Focus on embracing differences rather than ignoring them.  
- Ensure that the experiences and perspectives of diverse individuals and groups are incorporated into:  
  - Decision-making processes  
  - Program planning and implementation  
  - Policies and everyday practices  

**Planning Question for Inclusion**

- **How do we make sure we are taking differing experiences and perspectives into account in our decision-making and program planning?**

This question should be used consistently when designing, reviewing, or revising any program, policy, or operational decision.

---

### Equity: Outcomes and Resource Allocation

**Definition**

Equity is **outcomes focused** and is geared toward ameliorating the disparities caused by structural and systemic impediments to access and opportunity. It emphasizes that **the outcomes count** and that we must address how resources are distributed, especially for marginalized communities.

**Resources Considered in Equity Work**

Resources include, but are not limited to:

- Money  
- Power  
- Access  
- Information  
- Human resources  

**Core Elements**

- Addresses the disparity of resources and how they are **structurally allocated** to marginalized communities.  
- Seeks to reduce or eliminate inequities that arise from systemic barriers to access and opportunity.  
- Focuses on who benefits from decisions and programs, and whether those benefits are equitably distributed.

**Planning Question for Equity**

- **What are the outcomes you seek? For whom?**

This question must guide all planning and assessment processes, ensuring that:

- Desired outcomes are clearly defined.  
- The specific individuals or groups who should benefit are explicitly identified.  
- Structural and systemic barriers to those outcomes are considered and addressed.

---

### Using Inclusion and Equity Together in Practice

When engaging in decision-making or program planning:

1. **Apply an Inclusion Lens**  
   - Ask: *How do we make sure we are taking differing experiences and perspectives into account in our decision-making and program planning?*  
   - Ensure diverse voices and lived experiences inform the process from the outset.

2. **Apply an Equity Lens**  
   - Ask: *What are the outcomes you seek? For whom?*  
   - Examine how money, power, access, information, and human resources are allocated.  
   - Identify and address structural and systemic impediments to access and opportunity, particularly for marginalized communities.

By consistently integrating both inclusion (people count) and equity (outcomes count), operations and programs can more effectively support diverse community members and reduce disparities rooted in structural and systemic barriers.

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

### Inclusive & Accessible Event Planning
When planning and marketing events, ensure that all aspects are inclusive and accessible to the widest possible range of participants.

**Dietary Considerations**  
- Anticipate and plan for possible dietary requirements of your guests (e.g., Halal, Kosher, vegetarian, vegan, and common food allergies).

**Marketing and Advertising Content**  
- All event descriptions must include the following accessibility statement:  
  *“To make a disability accommodation request, please contact the event coordinator [insert Community/Hall Director name] at [insert Community/Hall Director email address] at least one week prior to the event date.”*  
- Review your advertisement to ensure it is not unintentionally or indirectly discriminating. Consider whether:
  - The event name could be seen as offensive or alienating to any segment of the community.  
  - The event description might exclude or marginalize certain groups.  
  - The images or overall design could be experienced as offensive, alienating, or unwelcoming.  
- Ensure that marketing materials do not use stereotypes or language that stems from stereotypes about any racial or ethnic group.  
- Have multiple people, with different perspectives, review the advertisement to identify potential issues and improve inclusivity.  
- When translating marketing materials into multiple languages, consider using professional translation services or well-qualified professionals to ensure accuracy and cultural appropriateness.

**Advertising Channels and Visibility**  
- Consider where you are advertising your event and whether those locations and methods reach a wide range of people.  
- Use multiple ways to advertise, such as:
  - Tommie Link  
  - Newsroom  
  - Pavement chalking  
  - Atrium banners  
  - Other visible and accessible signage and platforms as appropriate  

**Digital Accessibility**  
- Digital accessibility addresses the ability of people to access digital content (e.g., internet resources, virtual class materials, software) by considering the needs of:
  - Individuals with visual, auditory, motor, and/or cognitive disabilities  
  - Individuals with changing abilities due to aging, accidents, or illness  
- Accessibility involves “design for all” with proactive, upfront planning. It requires anticipating diversity and disabilities without designing only for specific, known individuals.  
- Learn and develop skills to create accessible digital content by:
  1. Visiting the Badging and Microcredentials page on OneStThomas:  
     https://one.stthomas.edu/sites/badging/SitePageModern/93816/home  
  2. Going to “Current Badges Available”:  
     https://one.stthomas.edu/sites/badging/SitePageModern/94739/current-badges-available  
  3. Selecting the “Digital Accessibility” badge to begin training.

**Sources:**
- `Event Resource_ Considerations for Inclusive Event Planning_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

---

### Inclusive Communities & Belonging
**In This Together: Belonging & Brave Spaces**  
**Theme:** Creating Inclusive Communities  
**Core Values:** Belonging & Inclusion  

Belonging and inclusion are foundational to building a community that reflects God’s love for all creation. As Catholic institutions are called to be places of radical hospitality, Principle 1 (https://stthomas.instructure.com/courses/84343/files/10566170?wrap=1 and https://stthomas.instructure.com/courses/84343/files/10566170/download?download_frd=1) urges us to foster a climate where all feel welcomed and valued.

As a student leader, your role is to make visible, intentional efforts to include others—especially those who are overlooked or marginalized. This includes consistently noticing who may be on the margins, reaching out to them, and creating opportunities for them to participate fully in the life of the community. Use the *In This Together: Glossary of Terms* (https://stthomas.instructure.com/courses/84343/pages/in-this-together-glossary-of-terms) as needed to support your understanding and language around belonging, inclusion, and identity.

Your leadership in the RA role should actively welcome residents who may feel left out or overlooked on campus. This involves both everyday practices (greeting residents by name, inviting them into conversations, encouraging participation in floor or hall events) and more intentional outreach to those who seem disconnected or isolated.

If a resident comes to you and shares that their neighbor told them a joke targeting their identity, you must follow established procedures for what you need to do once you learn this information. Those procedures are covered elsewhere and must be followed. In addition to those required steps, you are expected to focus on how you will foster inclusion and make your residents feel welcome going forward. Consider how you will:

- Rebuild or strengthen a sense of safety and belonging for the resident who was targeted.  
- Address the broader community climate so that all residents feel welcomed and included.  
- Model inclusive language and behavior in your daily interactions.  

Use the following reflection and discussion prompts to guide your thinking and practice:

- Describe a moment you felt a deep sense of belonging.  
- How can your leadership in the RA role actively welcome residents who may feel left out or overlooked on campus?  
- Imagine a resident came to you noting that their neighbor told them a joke targeting their identity. What are some ways you can make all your residents feel welcomed and included in the future?  

**Connection in SLED:**  
During August SLED, we consistently refer to sense of belonging—you will likely hear it and/or reflect on it in almost every session. In addition to individual sessions, belonging is explicitly covered during August SLED programming as part of our ongoing emphasis on creating inclusive communities and brave spaces in the residence halls.

**Sources:**
- `In This Together_ Belonging & Brave Spaces_ Resident Advisors 2025-2026.pdf`

### Intercultural Communication / DEI Training
In Intercultural Contexts

In intercultural contexts, shared meaning cannot be assumed. People bring different cultural backgrounds, experiences, and interpretations to any interaction, so it is not safe to presume that words, actions, or intentions will be understood in the same way by everyone involved.

Instead, shared meaning must be:

- Discovered  
- Negotiated  
- Created  

This process requires intentional effort to explore how others understand a situation, clarify one’s own meaning, and collaboratively arrive at a common understanding.

The quote, “Well of course I did it in cold blood, Iʼm cold-blooded!” illustrates how a person’s self-understanding, language, and intent may not be immediately clear or shared by others. Without discovering, negotiating, and creating shared meaning, such statements can easily be misunderstood in intercultural interactions.

Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW  
www.bethzemsky.com

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

### Intercultural Competence
INTERCULTURAL CONFLICT STYLES  

There is no single “best” intercultural conflict style. Different styles may be more or less effective depending on the people involved, the cultural context, and the specific situation. Staff should recognize and respect a range of conflict styles rather than assuming that one preferred approach is universally correct or superior.

**Sources:**
- `Roommate Agreement & Conflict Resolution Process.pdf`

---

### Intercultural Competence / DEI Frameworks
**Stage: Defense / Polarization (Dualistic “Us vs. Them” Thinking)**  

This stage is characterized by a dualistic, polarized worldview in which people are divided into clear in‑groups (“us”) and out‑groups (“them”). A common example of this mindset is captured in the taunt:  
“Neanderthals, Neanderthals! Can’t make fire! Can’t make spear! Nyah, nyah, nyah…!”  

Key features of the Defense/Polarization stage include:

- **Dualistic “Us vs. Them” orientation**  
  - The world is viewed in terms of clear, opposing groups.  
  - There is a strong sense of separation between “us” and “those people.”

- **Denigration and superiority**  
  - Out‑groups are often described or thought of in dismissive or demeaning ways (e.g., “Those people…”).  
  - One’s own group is seen as superior, more advanced, or more correct.

- **Strong commitment to one’s own worldview**  
  - Individuals hold tightly to their existing beliefs, values, and ways of doing things.  
  - Alternative perspectives are often rejected or minimized.

- **Distrust of different behavior or ideas**  
  - There is some level of distrust or suspicion toward people who behave differently or hold different ideas.  
  - Differences are not simply noticed; they are treated as potentially problematic.

- **Recognition of difference with negative evaluation**  
  - Differences between groups are clearly recognized.  
  - These differences are evaluated negatively rather than neutrally or positively.

- **Perception of threat to beliefs or way of life**  
  - Different behaviors, ideas, or groups are experienced as threatening to one’s beliefs, values, or way of life.  
  - This perceived threat reinforces the “us vs. them” divide.

- **Primary emotional/cognitive approach: fear and anger**  
  - Emotional responses are dominated by fear and anger.  
  - These emotions shape how information is interpreted and how others are treated.  

(Source: Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com)

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

---

### Leadership & Professional Development
Values-Based Leadership

Values-based leadership is leadership that is consistently guided by a clear set of moral and ethical values. These values include, but are not limited to:

- Compassion  
- Respect  
- Responsibility  

Leaders who practice values-based leadership use these principles to inform their decisions, behaviors, and interactions with others.

### Learning Outcomes & Curriculum
**Connecting Learning Outcomes**

The following outlines the progression of student abilities in connecting with individuals and communities, engaging with empathy, navigating conflict, and building interdependent relationships. The outcomes are categorized into four levels: No Evidence, Beginning, Middle, and Advanced.

**2.a Identify Connections with Individuals and Communities**
- **No Evidence**: Students are unable to identify connections.
- **Beginning**: Students can describe potential connections with individuals and communities.
- **Middle**: Students can describe existing connections with individuals and communities.
- **Advanced**: Students can estimate the actions needed to maintain these connections.

**2.b Demonstrate the Ability to Engage Others with Empathy**
- **No Evidence**: Students are unable to engage others with empathy.
- **Beginning**: Students recognize strategies to engage others with empathy and identify situations where empathy is required.
- **Middle**: Students apply strategies to engage others with empathy.
- **Advanced**: Students propose strategies for others to engage with empathy in group discussions.

**2.c Employ Strategies to Navigate Conflict in a Healthy Manner**
- **No Evidence**: Students are unable to recognize or navigate conflict without escalation.
- **Beginning**: Students recognize strategies to navigate conflict in a healthy manner.
- **Middle**: Students employ strategies to navigate conflict without escalation.
- **Advanced**: Students resolve conflict without escalation using a variety of strategies.

**2.d Build Interdependent Relationships with Mutual Accountability**
- **No Evidence**: Students are unable to participate in interdependent relationships with mutual accountability.
- **Beginning**: Students recognize strategies to build interdependent relationships with mutual accountability.
- **Middle**: Students apply strategies to build such relationships.
- **Advanced**: Students evaluate existing relationships for interdependence and mutual accountability.

This framework is designed to guide students in developing these essential skills progressively.

**Sources:**
- `Tommie Advantage Overview.pdf`

---

### Lesson Planning & Facilitation
When developing a lesson plan for residence life operations, it is essential to follow a structured approach that ensures clarity and effectiveness. Begin by outlining the Lesson Plan Structure, which includes the Learning Strategy, Target Audience, and Date & Time of the session. Clearly define the Learning Goals & Outcomes, specifying what participants are expected to achieve by the end of the lesson.

Incorporate the Strategy Level Outcomes, which align with broader educational objectives, and identify any Additional Intended Outcomes that may enhance the learning experience. Provide a Rationale (the WHY) to explain the importance of the lesson and its relevance to the participants.

Thorough Planning & Preparation is crucial for a successful session. Develop a Facilitation Guide to assist in delivering the content effectively. Include an Assessment & Reporting component to evaluate participant understanding and measure the success of the lesson.

Finally, outline Supervision Expectations to ensure that the lesson is conducted in a supportive and controlled environment. This comprehensive approach ensures that all aspects of the lesson plan are addressed, leading to a productive and engaging learning experience for the target audience.

**Sources:**
- `Tommie Advantage Overview.pdf`

### Mission & Professional Development
PRINCIPLES OF GOOD PRACTICE FOR STUDENT AFFAIRS AT CATHOLIC COLLEGES AND UNIVERSITIES  
(Principle Two and Principle Nine)

---

### Principle Two  
**Equips student affairs professionals with knowledge and perspectives to contribute to the Catholic mission of the institution. Grounds practice in the living tradition of the Church, as the people of God on a journey to the truth.**

Student affairs professionals at Catholic colleges and universities are expected to ground their work in the Catholic mission of the institution and in the living tradition of the Church. This principle emphasizes that:

- Practice is rooted in the understanding that we are the people of God on a journey to truth.  
- Student affairs professionals engage in regular review and discernment of how the university’s policies, practices, and decisions draw on the Catholic mission and tradition.  
- Components of the Catholic tradition that inform this work include, but are not limited to, reflections on Scripture, Church teaching, theology, spiritual writings, and ethics.  
- Many institutions also benefit from the charism, spirituality, and personal witness of their founding religious congregation.  
- Student affairs staff development focuses on deepening understanding and awareness of the Catholic mission and tradition.

To implement this principle, student affairs professionals and units should ask:

1. **What does this principle mean for me, my functional area, and my institution?**  
   - Clarify how your specific role, department, and the broader institution are called to embody and advance the Catholic mission and the wisdom tradition of the Church.

2. **How do we animate this principle through programs, policies, and practices?**  
   Consider the following guiding questions:  
   a. How do we use professional development to deepen our understanding and awareness of the Catholic mission of the institution?  
   b. How do we regularly review and discern how the university’s policies, practices, and decisions reflect the wisdom tradition of the Church?  
   c. How do we engage components of the tradition of the Church, including reflections on Scripture, Church teaching, theology, spiritual writings, and ethics?  
   d. How do we benefit from the charism, spirituality, and personal witness of our founding religious congregation?  
   e. How do we see ourselves as the people of God on a journey to truth?

3. **What evidence do we have to judge the effectiveness of our efforts?**  
   - Identify data, observations, assessments, and feedback that indicate how well programs, policies, and practices reflect and advance the Catholic mission and tradition.

4. **What does the evidence tell us about our effectiveness?**  
   - Analyze the collected evidence to determine strengths, gaps, and areas for growth in grounding practice in the Catholic mission and tradition.

5. **What will we do with the information we have gathered about our effectiveness?**  
   - Use the findings to refine, strengthen, or redesign professional development, policies, programs, and practices so they more fully embody the Catholic mission and the wisdom tradition of the Church.

---

### Principle Nine  
**Intentionally develops the profession of student affairs at Catholic colleges and universities as a vital component of Catholic higher education.**

Student affairs professionals at Catholic colleges and universities bear a responsibility for developing the vocation of the profession itself as a vital component of Catholic higher education. This principle emphasizes that:

- We consider what is distinctively Catholic about this work and draw upon the myriad resources in the Catholic tradition to enrich practice.  
- We draw upon our founding charisms, in particular, to shape and direct our work.  
- We invest in professional development, including competencies, to do our important work in Catholic colleges and universities.  
- We recruit, prepare, mentor, and support those who will impact and lead student affairs at Catholic colleges and universities in the future.  
- We leverage our own commitments and practice to inspire others to enter the field.  
- We contribute to the larger profession of student affairs in higher education.  
- Student affairs professionals are teacher practitioners and intellectuals who contribute to the scholarship of the field.  
- We create and teach in graduate and other credentialing programs.  
- We are knowledge creators, contributing through individual talents and experiences enriched by the Catholic mission to the larger profession of student affairs in higher education.

To implement this principle, student affairs professionals and units should ask:

1. **What does this principle mean for me, my functional area, and my institution?**  
   - Clarify how your role and department contribute to the development of student affairs as a vocation and as a scholarly and professional field within Catholic higher education.

2. **How do we animate this principle through programs, policies, and practices?**  
   Consider the following guiding questions:  
   a. How do we consider what is distinctively Catholic about this work and draw upon the myriad resources in this Catholic tradition to enrich the practice?  
   b. How do we draw upon our founding charisms, in particular, to shape and direct our work?  
   c. How do we invest in professional development, including competencies, to do our important work in Catholic colleges and universities?  
   d. How do we recruit, prepare, mentor, and support those who will lead student affairs at Catholic colleges and universities in the future?  
   e. How do we leverage our own commitments and practice to inspire others to enter the field?  
   f. How do we contribute to the larger profession of student affairs in higher education?

3. **What evidence do we have to judge the effectiveness of our efforts?**  
   - Identify data, observations, assessments, and feedback that indicate how well we are developing the profession, supporting future leaders, and contributing to the broader field.

4. **What does the evidence tell us about our effectiveness?**  
   - Analyze the collected evidence to determine strengths, gaps, and areas for growth in professional formation, leadership development, and scholarly contribution.

5. **What will we do with the information we have gathered about our effectiveness?**  
   - Use the findings to enhance recruitment, preparation, mentoring, professional development, and scholarly engagement so that student affairs at Catholic colleges and universities continues to grow as a vital, mission-driven profession.

**Sources:**
- `ASACCU Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs.pdf`
- `ASACCU Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs.pdf`

---

### Mission & Professional Practice Principles
4 Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs at Catholic Colleges and Universities  
(Residence Life Operations Reference)

Our work in student affairs at Catholic colleges and universities is grounded in the mission of our institutions and is both urgent and complex. Our students and institutions are simultaneously facing a global pandemic, a national reckoning with racism, deep political and social divisions, a growing mental health crisis, the ever-increasing costs of an education, and public speculation about the value of a college degree. In this context, it is more important than ever that we educate the whole student.

Student affairs professionals must walk with students in new and innovative ways as they pursue purpose, develop their faith lives, and build relationships – across difference – in pursuit of the common good. For institutions and for student affairs professionals, this urgent and complex work can and should be inspired and guided by an ongoing dialogue between:

- Our Catholic tradition  
- The distinctive charisms of founding religious congregations  
- The unique gifts and needs of our students and colleagues  

These principles aim to immerse student affairs professionals in the center of this dialogue so that they can contribute to the work, provide leadership, inform practice, and be enriched and inspired by the process.

The academic communities we call home are richly diverse. We can expect, therefore, that our many institutions and the many individuals within our schools will embody these principles in unique ways. While our expression of these principles may be unique, we are bound together by a compelling and transcendent shared mission. The principles are intended to inspire and guide our work as we purposely, collectively, and distinctively pursue our shared Catholic mission.

Purpose and Use of the Principles  
The 4 Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs at Catholic Colleges and Universities are intended to:

- Provide a framework for student affairs professionals at Catholic colleges and universities in their reflection and conversation  
- Guide staff development efforts  
- Inform planning and assessment  

These principles are born out of our Catholic tradition and are shared across our many and diverse institutions. This guide is a tool to support and assist the transformative work of student affairs practice and can be used to catalyze discussion with a broad array of leaders across Catholic higher education.

**Sources:**
- `ASACCU Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs.pdf`

---

### Mission, Values, & Community Development Principles
**Principle Seven: Facilitating Relationship Building, Dialogue, and the Pursuit of Common Ground**

**Statement of the Principle**

Principle Seven states that student affairs at Catholic colleges and universities:

> Facilitates relationship building, dialogue, and the pursuit of common ground among individuals and communities with differing world view, contributing in the work for a more just world.

It is the role of Catholic colleges and universities to engage the great questions facing humanity. This work is done humbly and always with love, grounded in the Catholic belief that all human persons are created in the image of God. Student affairs professionals create space for those who disagree with one another to hear and consider others’ thoughts and lived experiences. It is never enough for a university community to critique the present order; we must become part of the solution. This requires ongoing self-examination and continued conversation, and attention to how the culture of student affairs practices, policies, and structures might embody the justice and love we hope for the larger world.

---

**Guiding Questions for Implementation**

To apply this principle in student affairs work at Catholic colleges and universities, consider the following questions:

1. **Meaning and Implications**
   - What does this principle mean for me, my functional area, and my institution?

2. **Animating the Principle in Practice**
   - How do we animate this principle through programs, policies, and practices?
     - How do we address the great questions facing humanity?
     - How do we reflect humility with love and the dignity of each person?
     - How do we create space for those who disagree to hear and consider the others’ thoughts and lived experiences?
     - How do we promote our becoming part of the solution?
     - How do we encourage self-examination to be aware of our need for continued conversation?

3. **Assessment and Evidence**
   - What evidence do we have to judge the effectiveness of our efforts?
   - What does the evidence tell us about our effectiveness?
   - What will we do with the information we have gathered about our effectiveness?

---

**Assessing the Principle**

When assessing Principle Seven, student affairs professionals should:

- Examine how effectively they are facilitating relationship building, dialogue, and the pursuit of common ground among individuals and communities with differing world views.
- Review how programs, policies, and practices engage the great questions facing humanity with humility, love, and respect for the dignity of each person.
- Evaluate whether spaces are genuinely created for disagreement, listening, and consideration of others’ thoughts and lived experiences.
- Consider the extent to which the community moves beyond critique of the present order to becoming part of the solution.
- Reflect on how self-examination and continued conversation are encouraged and practiced.
- Attend to the ways in which the culture of student affairs practices, policies, and structures embodies the justice and love desired for the larger world.
- Use gathered evidence to make informed decisions about strengthening alignment with this principle and improving effectiveness over time.

**Sources:**
- `ASACCU Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs.pdf`

---

### Mission, Values, and Inclusive Practice Principles
**Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs at Catholic Colleges and Universities**  
**Principle One and Principle Four**

---

### Principle One  
**Embraces all students as valued members of an inclusive and diverse community that embodies God’s love for all.**

Student affairs professionals at Catholic colleges and universities:

- Create policies, practices, and programs that result in developing a student’s sense of belonging.  
- Design equity-minded initiatives that reflect the manifestation of God’s love.  
- Support campus cultures that reflect social justice and an inclusive campus that holds sacred the dignity of each person.

To assess and apply this principle, student affairs professionals should ask:

1. **Meaning and implications**
   - What does this principle mean for me, my functional area, and my institution?

2. **Implementation through programs, policies, and practices**
   - How do we animate this principle through programs, policies, and practices?  
     a. To what extent are we prepared to embrace all students?  
     b. Do our policies, practices, and programs result in a student’s sense of belonging?  
     c. Have we incorporated an equity mindset in the design of our initiatives and programs?  
     d. Do our actions reflect the manifestation of God’s love for all?  
     e. Do our initiatives reflect the manifestation of God’s love for all?

3. **Evidence of effectiveness**
   - What evidence do we have to judge the effectiveness of our efforts?

4. **Interpretation of evidence**
   - What does the evidence tell us about our effectiveness?

5. **Use of evidence for improvement**
   - What will we do with the information we have gathered about our effectiveness?

---

### Principle Four  
**Inspires and equips students to live solidarity and commit to the dignity of all people, embracing the goals of diversity, equity, inclusion, and justice in a community of love.**

Catholic social teaching calls upon all people of good will to care for one another and to live in solidarity and compassion, especially with those whom the world undervalues. This call includes:

- Committing to the sacredness and dignity of each human person.  
- Recognizing and honoring the various intersections that make up each individual, so that all might flourish.  

Work for racial justice, respect for life, human rights, gender equality, LGBTQ+ rights, sheltering refugees, eradicating poverty, climate sustainability, and other matters of human concern are ways of realizing this call in the present day.

In the university context, student affairs professionals:

- Assist students in their attempts to better understand and promote awareness of injustice at the local and global levels.  
- Support students in promoting transformational change.

To assess and apply this principle, student affairs professionals should ask:

1. **Meaning and implications**
   - What does this principle mean for me, my functional area, and my institution?

2. **Implementation through programs, policies, and practices**
   - How do we animate this principle through programs, policies, and practices?  
     a. How do we reflect lives of compassion and care?  
     b. How do we reflect the sacredness and dignity of each human person?  
     c. How do we facilitate a deepening awareness of injustice at the local and global levels?  
     d. How do we promote transformational change?  
     e. How are we informed by principles of Catholic social teaching?

3. **Evidence of effectiveness**
   - What evidence do we have to judge the effectiveness of our efforts?

4. **Interpretation of evidence**
   - What does the evidence tell us about our effectiveness?

5. **Use of evidence for improvement**
   - What will we do with the information we have gathered about our effectiveness?

**Sources:**
- `ASACCU Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs.pdf`
- `ASACCU Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs.pdf`

---

### Mission, Values, and Student Development Framework
**Principle Three and Principle Six: Accompanying Students in Their Search for Truth, Meaning, Integration, and Relationship with God**

Catholic colleges and universities foster the development of the whole person. In addition to supporting students’ intellectual and social development, there is a particular emphasis on students’ faith and spiritual development. Student affairs professionals support learning throughout the student experience and provide learning opportunities for the search for meaning, for conscience formation, and for contributing to the human family. In this work, faith is integrated with the intellectual life, bringing faith and reason into dialogue as students integrate these core aspects of their lives and development.

Student affairs professionals share responsibility with campus ministers to support students’ spiritual growth. We offer inclusive, developmentally appropriate activities such as liturgy, sacraments and other prayer opportunities, faith sharing groups, retreats, spiritual direction, and instruction in the faith. Students enter our campuses today with both religious and nonreligious identities. As student affairs professionals in Catholic contexts, we believe it is our responsibility to support all students in their search for meaning and for the Transcendent by providing varying resources and community connections for this purpose.

Within this shared mission, two principles guide practice:

- **Principle Three:** *Accompanies students on their search for truth, meaning, and integration through curricular and co-curricular learning.*  
- **Principle Six:** *Invites and accompanies all students to develop an active and meaningful relationship with God.*

### Core Commitments in Practice

To animate these principles through programs, policies, and practices, student affairs professionals:

- **Cultivate vibrant learning communities on campus** that support the development of the whole person and integrate curricular and co-curricular learning.
- **Link personal accountability to self-reflection and its impact on community**, helping students understand how their choices affect others.
- **Create student engagement opportunities** where students learn empathy and a sense of their place in the world, and how their actions can lead to a more equitable society.
- **Connect students’ personal interests with the common good**, helping them see how their gifts and passions can serve others.
- **Share responsibility with campus ministers to support students’ spiritual growth**, coordinating efforts across functional areas.
- **Offer inclusive, developmentally appropriate spiritual and religious activities**, including:
  - Liturgy  
  - Sacraments and other prayer opportunities  
  - Faith sharing groups  
  - Retreats  
  - Spiritual direction  
  - Instruction in the faith
- **Support all students in their search for meaning and the Transcendent**, regardless of whether they arrive with religious or nonreligious identities, by providing varying resources and community connections for this purpose.
- **Celebrate religious diversity on our campuses**, recognizing it as integral to spiritual growth.
- **Combat all forms of religious discrimination**, affirming that religious diversity cannot tolerate any form of religious discrimination.
- **Invite students into conversations with all people of good will** to foster understanding, respect, and shared action for the common good in a religiously pluralistic world.

### Guiding Questions for Implementation

When applying these principles, student affairs professionals, their functional areas, and their institutions should ask:

1. **Meaning and relevance**
   - What does this principle mean for me, my functional area, and my institution?

2. **Programs, policies, and practices**
   - How do we animate this principle through programs, policies, and practices?
   - How do we cultivate vibrant learning communities on campus?
   - How do we link personal accountability to self-reflection and its impact on community?
   - How do we create student engagement opportunities where students learn empathy and a sense of their place in the world?
   - How do we promote student actions that can lead to a more equitable society?
   - How do we connect students’ personal interests with the common good?
   - How do we share responsibility with campus ministers to support students’ spiritual growth?
   - How do we fulfill our responsibility to support all students in their search for meaning and the Transcendent by providing varying resources and community connections for this purpose?
   - How do we celebrate religious diversity on our campuses?
   - How do we combat all forms of religious discrimination?
   - How do we invite students into conversations with all people of good will to foster understanding, respect, and shared action for the common good in a religiously pluralistic world?

3. **Assessment and improvement**
   - What evidence do we have to judge the effectiveness of our efforts?
   - What does the evidence tell us about our effectiveness?
   - What will we do with the information we have gathered about our effectiveness?

These questions guide the ongoing assessment of **Principle Three** and **Principle Six**, ensuring that student affairs practice consistently accompanies students on their search for truth, meaning, and integration, and invites and accompanies all students to develop an active and meaningful relationship with God.

**Sources:**
- `ASACCU Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs.pdf`
- `ASACCU Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs.pdf`

### Professional Development & Communication Skills
To Level Up: Approaching Problems with Courage and Care

When you notice a problem, choose to address it rather than avoid it. Avoiding the problem may cause it to worsen over time. “Leveling up” in these situations requires both courage and care.

**Have Courage**
- Reflect on your fear: identify what feels difficult or uncomfortable about addressing the issue.
- Reflect on whether addressing the problem is the right thing for you, your team, and others involved.
- Do not avoid the problem simply because it is challenging.

**Do Not Avoid the Problem**
- The problem may worsen if it is ignored.
- Ask someone for help if you are unsure how to proceed.

**Act with Care**
- Consider the other person’s feelings when you address the issue.
- Consider their perspective, including their identities and context.
- Approach the situation thoughtfully, balancing your courage with empathy and respect.

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

### Professional Development & Philosophy
3 Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs at Catholic Colleges and Universities  
Re-Imagining the Principles: Process and Participants
------------------------------------------------------

### Writers’ Retreat Participants

The following individuals participated in the writers’ retreat to re-imagine the 3 Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs at Catholic Colleges and Universities:

- Dr. Esmilda Abreu-Hornbostel, Associate Vice President and Dean of Students, Manhattan College  
- Tshombé Brown, Moreau Center, Program Manager, Service & Community Partnerships, University of Portland  
- Kathy Byrnes, J.D., Vice President for Student Affairs, Villanova University  
- Dr. Sandy Estanek, Professor Emerita; Interim Vice President for Student Affairs, Canisius College  
- Dr. Craig Ford, Jr., Assistant Professor of Theology and Religious Studies, St. Norbert’s College  
- Dr. Chris Haug, Vice President for Student Affairs, Neumann University  
- Joe Henry, Dean of Students, King’s University College (Canada)  
- Rev. Dennis H. Holtschneider, CM, President, Association of Catholic Colleges and Universities  
- Dr. Michael James, Lecturer; Director, Institute for Administrators in Catholic Higher Education, Boston College  
- Dr. Dave Johnson, Associate Provost and Chief Student Affairs Officer, Xavier University  
- Dr. Linda McMurdock, Vice President for Student Affairs, Mount Saint Mary’s University, Los Angeles  
- Dr. Todd Olson, President, Mount Mercy University  
- Dr. Elizabeth Ortiz, Vice President, Diversity and Equity, DePaul University  
- Dr. Rebecca Sawyer, Vice President, Association of Catholic Colleges and Universities  
- Bennie D. Williams, Assistant Dean of Students and Director of the Multicultural Student Center, Canisius College  

### Timeline for Re-Imagining the Principles

- **2019**  
  - The 2019 ASACCU Board includes refreshing the Principles in its strategic plan, igniting a new process.  
  - **October 2019:** ACCU, ASACCU, and JASPA leaders meet to discuss refreshing the Principles.  
  - **November 2019:** ASACCU Senior Student Affairs Officers roundtable discussion provides feedback.

- **2020**  
  - **February 2020:** Planning group meets during the ACCU meeting in Washington, DC.  
  - **March 2020:** A survey seeking feedback from ASACCU and JASPA members is sent and analyzed.  
  - **March 2020:** COVID-19 shutdown delays the process until 2021.

- **2021**  
  - **February 2021:** Over 100 participants join an online listening session to add to the 2020 survey results.  
  - **September 2021:** Writers meet at the Inn at Villanova, PA.

- **2022**  
  - **February 2022:** The final draft of the refreshed Principles is presented to the presidents at the ACCU 2022 Annual Meeting.  
  - **Spring 2022:** The Principles are made available for broad distribution.

**Sources:**
- `ASACCU Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs.pdf`

### Professional Philosophy & Principles
**Principle Eight: Vocational Discernment and Connection of Gifts to the World’s Needs**  
*(From the 13 Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs at Catholic Colleges and Universities)*

**Principle Statement**  
Principle Eight states that student affairs assists students in discerning and developing their vocational directions and guides students in reflecting on how their gifts and talents connect with the world’s needs. Christians believe that their life work is accomplished in partnership with the God who gives life and purpose. At Catholic colleges and universities, student affairs professionals engage students in conversations that help them search for meaning and purpose by integrating their beliefs, gifts, ambitions, and hopes with the world’s needs. Through this work, student affairs professionals guide students in their discernment process to integrate professional, personal, and relational commitments over the course of their lives.

---

### Applying the Principle

When implementing Principle Eight, student affairs professionals, functional areas, and institutions should consider the following guiding questions:

1. **Meaning for Roles and Contexts**  
   - What does this principle mean for me, my functional area, and my institution?

2. **Animating the Principle in Practice**  
   - How do we animate this principle through programs, policies, and practices?  
     a. How do we guide students in their discernment of life choices?  
     b. How do we assist students to balance and integrate professional, personal, and relational commitments over the course of their lives?  
     c. How can we help students in their search for meaning and purpose?  
     d. How do we help students integrate their beliefs, gifts, ambitions, and hopes with the world’s needs?

---

### Assessing the Principle

To evaluate how effectively Principle Eight is being implemented, student affairs professionals and departments should address the following assessment questions:

3. **Evidence of Effectiveness**  
   - What evidence do we have to judge the effectiveness of our efforts?

4. **Interpretation of Evidence**  
   - What does the evidence tell us about our effectiveness?

5. **Use of Assessment Results**  
   - What will we do with the information we have gathered about our effectiveness?

These questions support ongoing reflection and improvement in assisting students with vocational discernment and in helping them connect their gifts and talents with the needs of the world in a Catholic higher education context.

**Sources:**
- `ASACCU Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs.pdf`

---

### Professional Values & Philosophy
**Principle Five: Personal Integrity, Reverence, and Care for Self, One Another, and the Community**  
*(From the 10 Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs at Catholic Colleges and Universities)*

**Core Meaning**

Principle Five calls and challenges students to personal integrity, reverence, and care for self, one another, and the community. Student affairs professionals have a responsibility to:

- Teach students about the inherent dignity of the human person.  
- Model “care for the other” in day-to-day professional practice.  
- Foster environments where students can learn and grow as whole persons.  
- Build students’ capacity to treat themselves and others with care, respect, and integrity.  
- Accompany students as they learn about their own agency and gain perspectives on how to make choices in coherent and integral ways.

In practice, this principle should be interpreted at three levels:
1. **Individual level:** What this principle means for me as a professional.  
2. **Functional area level:** What this principle means for my specific functional area.  
3. **Institutional level:** What this principle means for the institution as a whole.

---

**Animating the Principle in Programs, Policies, and Practices**

Student affairs staff are expected to bring this principle to life through concrete programs, policies, and practices by addressing the following:

- **Teaching inherent dignity:**  
  - How do we teach students about the inherent dignity of the human person?

- **Modeling holistic care:**  
  - How do we model care for holistic well-being and education?

- **Collaborative partnerships:**  
  - How do we partner with religious leaders; mental health professionals; diversity, equity, and inclusion professionals; and others to call and equip students to act in ethical and loving ways?

- **Forming holistic environments:**  
  - How do we foster environments where students can learn and grow as whole persons and build capacity to treat themselves and others with care, respect, and integrity?

- **Accompanying student agency and decision-making:**  
  - How do we accompany students to learn about their own agency and gain perspectives on how to make choices in coherent and integral ways?

These questions should guide the design, implementation, and ongoing refinement of all student affairs initiatives related to this principle.

---

**Assessing the Principle**

To ensure Principle Five is effectively implemented, student affairs professionals should engage in ongoing assessment guided by the following questions:

1. **Meaning and application**  
   - What does this principle mean for me, my functional area, and my institution?

2. **Evidence of effectiveness**  
   - What evidence do we have to judge the effectiveness of our efforts?

3. **Interpretation of evidence**  
   - What does the evidence tell us about our effectiveness?

4. **Use of findings**  
   - What will we do with the information we have gathered about our effectiveness?

Assessment should be used to confirm where Principle Five is being lived out effectively and to identify areas where programs, policies, and practices need to be strengthened so that students are consistently called and equipped to live with personal integrity, reverence, and care for self, one another, and the community.

**Sources:**
- `ASACCU Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs.pdf`

---

### Programming & Community Building
**Recruitment & Marketing**

Use multiple methods to promote the event and encourage resident participation:

- **Have residents hype it up**  
  Ask current residents to spread the word informally and share their excitement with others.

- **Flyers & large signs**  
  Create and post flyers and large signs in high-visibility areas to advertise the event.

- **Door knocking and hallway chats**  
  Go door-to-door and engage residents in brief conversations in the hallways to personally tell them about the event.

- **GroupMe / Email / Calendar invite**  
  Send reminders and details through GroupMe, email, and calendar invitations so residents have the event information saved and accessible.

- **Personal invitation**  
  Whenever possible, invite residents individually to increase the likelihood they will attend.

**Sources:**
- `RL-Residential Connection Events1 (1).pdf`

### Programming & Events
### Types of Events

Residence Life programming includes several distinct event types. Each type has specific expectations regarding who plans the event and which residents it is intended to serve.

1. **Individual RA Events**  
   - Each individual RA plans and hosts events specifically for their own small community (i.e., their assigned residents).  
   - These events are designed primarily for the residents of that RA’s area.

2. **Paired RA Events**  
   - Each RA also plans and hosts events in a pair with another RA.  
   - In a few cases, events may be planned and hosted by a group of three RAs on odd-numbered RA teams.  
   - These events are for the smaller combined communities of the RAs involved (their residents), but all residents are welcome to attend.

3. **Building/Community Events**  
   - Each building or community hosts events intended for the entire community.  
   - These events may be planned and executed by a small group of RAs, with support from the rest of the RA team.

4. **RCT Events (Residential Connection Intern Events)**  
   - Residential Connection Interns (RCTs) lead programming that is open to and intended for all of campus.  
   - The department will support these events.

**Sources:**
- `RL-Residential Connection Events1 (1).pdf`

### QPR & Mental Health Support Training
QPR Practice Session

Before engaging in a QPR (Question, Persuade, Refer) conversation, take a moment to reflect privately and prepare yourself. Consider the following questions:

- How would you approach this conversation?  
- How would you ask about thoughts of suicide?  
- What are ways you can empathize with them?  
- Who would you refer them to?

**Sources:**
- `UPDATED-QPR-GATEKEEPER August 2025 RA.pdf`

---

### QPR & Suicide Prevention
**Pro Tips for Effective QPR**

When practicing QPR (Question, Persuade, Refer), use the following strategies to support the person effectively:

- **Offer clear, direct encouragement.** Examples of helpful statements include:
  - “I want you to live.”
  - “I’m on your side... We’ll get through this.”

- **Involve additional support people.** Do not try to handle the situation alone. Ask the person who else might be able to help, such as:
  - Family
  - Friends
  - Brothers
  - Sisters
  - Pastors
  - Priest
  - Rabbi
  - Imam
  - Bishop
  - Physician

**Sources:**
- `UPDATED-QPR-GATEKEEPER August 2025 RA.pdf`

---

### QPR / Suicide Prevention Training
QPR Practice Session

During this practice session, participants will work in pairs to build skills in recognizing and responding to suicide risk using the QPR method.

1. Break into pairs.
2. In each pair, one person will:
   - Read the provided scenario.
   - Begin the conversation based on that scenario.
3. The other person will:
   - Respond using QPR (Question, Persuade, Refer).

This exercise is designed to give you the opportunity to:
- Practice listening carefully to the kinds of problems that can lead people to think about suicide.
- Ask directly about suicide in a frank and compassionate manner.

**Sources:**
- `UPDATED-QPR-GATEKEEPER August 2025 RA.pdf`

### RA Assessment & Accountability
RA Assessment & Learning Process and RA Accountability Guidelines - SLED, August 2025

The Resident Assistant (RA) Assessment & Learning Process, along with the RA Accountability Guidelines, are integral components of the Student Life and Engagement Division (SLED) as of August 2025. These guidelines are designed to ensure that RAs are effectively evaluated and held accountable for their responsibilities within the residence life framework.

The RA Assessment & Learning Process focuses on the continuous development and performance evaluation of Resident Assistants. This process includes regular feedback sessions, performance reviews, and opportunities for professional growth. RAs are encouraged to engage in self-reflection and seek constructive feedback from peers and supervisors to enhance their skills and effectiveness in their roles.

The RA Accountability Guidelines outline the expectations and responsibilities of RAs, providing a clear framework for maintaining high standards of conduct and performance. These guidelines detail the procedures for addressing any breaches of conduct, including the steps for investigation, documentation, and resolution of issues. The guidelines emphasize the importance of fairness, transparency, and consistency in handling accountability matters.

Together, these processes and guidelines aim to support RAs in their personal and professional development while ensuring a positive and safe living environment for all residents.

**Sources:**
- `RA Assessment & Learning Process + RA Accountability.pdf`

These categories include Community Engagement, Resident Interaction, Student Leader Team, and Administration & Operations. Each of these areas is critical to the overall responsibilities and effectiveness of an RA, ensuring they can successfully fulfill their role within the residence life framework.

### RA Facilitation & Mediation Skills
RA-Facilitated Meeting: Multipartiality & Preparation

When facilitating a roommate or community meeting, RAs should:

1. Practice Multipartiality  
   - Approach the conversation in a multipartial manner, actively supporting all residents in being heard.  
   - Avoid taking sides; instead, validate each person’s perspective and ensure balanced participation.

2. Prepare for the Meeting  
   - Plan the structure and goals of the meeting in advance.  
   - Review relevant information about the situation and any previous agreements or conversations.  
   - Anticipate potential concerns and think through how to guide the discussion productively.

3. Address Needs  
   - Help residents identify and clearly express their individual and shared needs.  
   - Ensure that each person has the opportunity to state what they need to feel comfortable and respected in the space.  
   - Work toward solutions that acknowledge and incorporate all stated needs where possible.

4. Address Policies  
   - Clearly review and explain all applicable residence life and university policies related to the situation.  
   - Connect the discussion of needs and behaviors to these policies so residents understand expectations and boundaries.  
   - Clarify any misunderstandings about rules and consequences.

5. Update the Roommate Agreement  
   - Based on the discussion, revise the Roommate Agreement to reflect any new expectations, boundaries, or arrangements.  
   - Ensure all roommates understand and agree to the updated terms.  
   - Document the changes and confirm that the updated agreement is stored or submitted according to residence life procedures.

**Sources:**
- `Roommate Agreement & Conflict Resolution Process.pdf`

### RA Intercultural Development / IDI Profiles
### Resident Advisors: IDI Profile – Acceptance Stage  
*(N = 80)*  

At the Acceptance stage of the Intercultural Development Inventory (IDI), Resident Advisors demonstrate the following characteristics:

- **Recognition of Cultural Patterns and Context**  
  - Able to recognize cultural patterns in themselves and others.  
  - Able to see these patterns within their broader cultural context.

- **Exploration of Cultural Differences**  
  - Begin to actively explore cultural differences.  
  - Approach other cultures with non‑evaluative curiosity (i.e., without immediate judgment).  
  - Hold the perspective that “more difference means more creative ideas!”

- **Relativism in Behavior, Beliefs, and Values**  
  - **Behavioral Relativism:** Understand that all behavior exists within a cultural context.  
  - **Value Relativism:** Understand that beliefs and values also exist within a cultural context.

- **Primary Emotional/Cognitive Approach**  
  - Curiosity is the primary emotional and cognitive stance toward cultural differences.

_Source: Beth Zemsky, MAEd, LICSW – www.bethzemsky.com_

**Sources:**
- `St Thomas RA IDC 2025 shared.pdf`

### RA Performance Assessment & Development
The Fall Assessment and Learning process for Resident Assistants (RAs) is structured as follows:

1. **RA Fall Self-Assessment**: This takes place from October 27 to 31. During this period, RAs are required to complete a self-assessment using Likert scale responses and provide narratives for each category.

2. **CD/GSA Fall Assessment & Learning**: From November 3 to 7, Community Directors (CDs) and Graduate Student Assistants (GSAs) will write their assessments. This will also involve Likert scale responses and narratives for each category.

3. **Fall Assessment & Learning Meetings**: Scheduled between November 10 and 21, these meetings are designed to discuss the assessments and learning outcomes. If deemed appropriate, a Performance Improvement Plan may be co-created during these discussions.

### RA Position Description & Expectations
**University of St. Thomas Department of Residence Life: Resident Advisor Position Overview 2025 – 2026**

**Title:** Resident Advisor  
**Reports to:** Community/Hall Director

**Eligibility Requirements:**
1. **Academic Standing:** 
   - Maintain a minimum cumulative GPA of 2.5. A drop below this may result in dismissal.
   - A semester GPA below 2.5, even if cumulative GPA is above 2.5, will result in a formal warning. Continued low semester GPAs for two consecutive terms may lead to dismissal or non-renewal.
   - Maintain a full academic load of 12 or more credits during Fall and Spring semesters. J-Term credits do not count towards this requirement. Exceptions may be granted by the Associate Director of Residence Life.
   - Must be an undergraduate student and not on Conduct Probation.

2. **Conduct and Performance:**
   - Adherence to the Student Code of Conduct and University policies is mandatory. Violations may lead to warnings or dismissal.
   - Performance evaluations are ongoing. Failure to meet expectations may result in warnings or dismissal.

**Time Commitment and Important Dates:**
- The RA position is the principal out-of-class activity, running from August 13, 2025, to May 31, 2026, including J-Term and break periods.
- Participation in semester-long study abroad programs, student teaching, or similar demanding practicums/internships during the Fall or Spring semesters is not permitted.
- Evening classes extending beyond 4:30 pm require prior discussion with the Community/Hall Director and may be restricted.
- Anticipated service dates are August 13, 2025 – May 31, 2026.
- Attendance at all move-in, education, and development sessions is required.
- Up to 6 nights away per semester are allowed with prior approval from the Community/Hall Director.

**Administration & Operations:**
- Manage time requirements and responsibilities effectively.
- Complete paperwork and tasks such as Roompact forms, weekly reports, and maintenance requests promptly.
- Fulfill duty responsibilities, including desk service, duty rounds, and policy enforcement.
- Assist in maintaining the living environment, including setting up rooms and organizing common spaces.
- Support health and safety through ongoing checks.

**Education & Development:**
- Active engagement in all education and development sessions is required. This includes team gatherings, one-on-one meetings with the Community Director/Graduate Student Assistant, and institutional sessions from April 2025 through May 2026.

**Compensation:**
- Housing is provided, potentially shared with another RA.
- An Unlimited Meal Plan is included.
- A $450 stipend is paid bi-weekly, subject to state and federal tax deductions.

**Guidelines for Outside Commitments:**
- All formal time commitments must be discussed with the Community/Hall Director or Graduate Student Assistant before engagement.
- Approved outside commitments are limited to 10 hours per week.
- If outside commitments interfere with RA responsibilities, they may need to be reduced or discontinued after consultation with the Community/Hall Director.

**Sources:**
- `RA Student Leader Agreement 2025-2026.pdf`
- `RA Position Overview 25-26.pdf`

### RA Position Overview & Expectations
Resident Advisor Position Overview  
University of St. Thomas • 2025–2026

This overview outlines eligibility limits, time commitments, ongoing responsibilities, and required dates for the RA position. All details below apply for the full duration of your appointment.

---

### Position Eligibility and Concurrent Commitments

While serving as an RA (fall and spring semesters):

- You **cannot** hold a full-time internship, participate in student teaching, or engage in a similarly demanding practicum or internship in either the fall or spring semesters.
- You **cannot** participate in a semester-long study abroad program.
- Because of extensive evening time commitments, you **must** discuss any potential evening classes with your CD/GSA **prior to registration**.
- Due to Student Leader Education & Development in August, you **cannot** enroll in any **Summer Session II** courses or any other course that would interfere with fully participating in the August dates listed below.
- You must be available for residence hall openings and remain through all hall closings and safety checks.
- As halls remain open over all breaks, you may be responsible for duty coverage during all or part of the following breaks: **Fall, Thanksgiving, Winter, J-Term, Spring, and Easter**.
- You may **not** make travel arrangements for any break until you have:
  - Discussed your plans with your CD/GSA, and  
  - Confirmed that all building responsibilities are covered.

Your RA position must be your **primary out-of-class time commitment** throughout the academic year.

---

### Ongoing Responsibilities (Academic Year)

Throughout the academic year, you are expected to:

- Maintain the RA position as your primary out-of-class commitment, with **no more than 10 hours per week total** of outside time commitments (e.g., other jobs, organizations, internships).
- Effectively execute **all expectations** within this position overview.
- Attend:
  - Weekly student leader team meetings.
  - Individual meetings with your CD/GSA.
  - Residence Life all student leader team meetings.
  - Any additional required student leader team events.
- When on duty:
  - Serve the hall desk from **4:30 pm – 8:00 pm**.
  - In buildings without a desk, hold “office hours” in place of serving at a desk.
  - Be on duty from **4:30 pm** until **8:00 am the following day**.
- Provide duty coverage on all breaks, as assigned.
- Attend ongoing education and development opportunities as required.

---

**Important Note:**  
During the entire timeframe **August 13 – September 2, 2025** (including evenings and weekends), RAs **should not plan any other commitments**, including but not limited to classes, work, travel, internships, or similar obligations.
