### RA Position Overview / Expectations
University of St. Thomas  
Resident Advisor Position Overview 2025–2026  

Through intentional relationship and community building, the Department of Residence Life provides an inclusive and supportive educational living environment where students develop skills and knowledge to think critically, act wisely, and work skillfully to advance the common good.

Resident Advisors (RAs) are thoughtful, inclusive, present, and morally responsible peer leaders. They express these values and characteristics through community building, resident interaction, student leader education and development, and administrative responsibilities. RAs are committed to furthering student learning grounded in St. Thomas’ mission and convictions.

The RA position is focused on connection and relationship building; RAs must be willing and motivated to engage, connect with, and outreach to residents. RAs promote student learning through implementation of a variety of educational strategies to achieve the Tommie Advantage learning goals: self-understanding, connecting, embracing our differences, practicing holistic well-being, and acting wisely.

**Position Expectations**

**Community Engagement**  
RAs are expected to:
- Strive to foster an inclusive environment and sense of belonging for all residents. Facilitated education and development will be provided to better prepare RAs for fostering this inclusive environment where residents feel a sense of belonging.  
- Be present and actively engaged with their residential community.  
- Facilitate connection among residents through events and other engagement opportunities.  
- Promote student learning for residents through:
  - Facilitated community meetings  
  - Roommate and community conflict resolution  
  - Community communications  
  - Other Tommie Advantage strategies  
- Support residents in building interdependent relationships and practicing accountability in their communities.  
- Assist in disseminating information to keep residents fully aware of learning opportunities, policies, news, and procedures.  
- Create and maintain virtual community through a group chat.  
- Understand, create, and maintain an environment that supports academic achievement and success.  
- Confront behaviors not in accordance with the student code of conduct and community standards.  
- Appropriately use professional authority while maintaining respectful relationships with residents.  

**Resident Interaction**  
RAs are expected to:
- Demonstrate an understanding of diversity, equity, inclusion, and belonging in order to effectively serve, communicate with, and advocate for all students in our residential population.  
- Engage in intentional, ongoing, meaningful connections with residents in their community, including 1:1 conversations and informal touch points.  
- Support residents and refer them to appropriate campus resources for social, academic, and personal issues.  
- Assist residents in listening with empathy and navigating conflict in a healthy manner.  
- Promptly contact and connect with residents as requested by their supervisor, the Department of Residence Life, or the University of St. Thomas Student Leader Team.  
- Communicate effectively, including engaging in constructive and respectful feedback with their Community Director (CD), Graduate Student Assistant (GSA), and student leader team.  
- Collaborate in a team-focused work environment.

**Sources:**
- `RA Position Overview 25-26.pdf`

---

### RA Role & Community Building
Resident Assistants (RAs) play a crucial role in fostering community and belonging within the residential environment. All humans crave connection and a sense of belonging, and RAs are central to creating and sustaining that experience for residents.

RAs are key contributors to students’ academic belonging by helping them identify and utilize campus and community resources that support their success. In addition, RAs intentionally connect with students across diverse perspectives and identities, ensuring that all residents feel seen, included, and supported as members of the community.

**Sources:**
- `2025StudentLeaderTraining - CSA.pdf`

---

### RA Role & Expectations
RA Role in CARE

Resident Assistants play a key role in supporting student well-being and building a caring community. Your responsibilities in the CARE process include:

- Develop caring relationships with residents  
  - Be approachable, visible, and engaged in your community.  
  - Build trust so residents feel comfortable sharing concerns.

- Trust your gut  
  - Pay attention to your instincts if something feels “off” with a resident or situation.  
  - Take concerns seriously, even if you are unsure of all the details.

- Set boundaries  
  - Maintain professional limits in your relationships with residents.  
  - Know your role and refer to professional staff or resources when issues go beyond your scope.

- Follow-ups and check-ins  
  - Conduct follow-ups with residents after concerns are raised or incidents occur.  
  - Check in regularly with residents who may need ongoing support.

In carrying out these responsibilities, ensure:

- Prompt action  
  - Respond quickly when you notice a concern or receive information about a resident’s well-being.

- Clear and concise notes  
  - Document situations accurately, using specific, objective language.  
  - Keep records organized and easy to understand.

- Prompt response  
  - Reply in a timely manner to communication from residents, supervisors, and campus partners related to CARE concerns.

**Sources:**
- `CARE & DOS Resources.pdf`

---

Your Role as an RA: Response and Support

As a Resident Assistant (RA), you serve as a primary point of contact and support for residents in your community. Your role in response and support includes:

- Being accessible and approachable so residents feel comfortable coming to you with questions, concerns, or problems.
- Listening actively and respectfully to residents, taking their concerns seriously, and maintaining appropriate confidentiality.
- Providing accurate information about residence life policies, campus resources, and community expectations.
- Offering basic support and guidance when residents experience personal, academic, or social challenges, while recognizing the limits of your role.
- Referring residents to appropriate professional resources (such as counseling, health services, academic support, or campus safety) when issues go beyond your scope or require specialized assistance.
- Responding promptly and appropriately to situations that affect resident well-being, safety, or community standards, following all established procedures and protocols.
- Documenting incidents and concerns as required, and communicating relevant information to your supervisor or professional staff in a timely manner.
- Helping to create and maintain a supportive, inclusive, and respectful living environment where residents feel safe, heard, and valued.
- Modeling responsible behavior and adherence to policies, and encouraging residents to do the same.
- Following up with residents after incidents or referrals, when appropriate, to ensure they are connected to the support they need.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Maintaining Good Balance as an RA

As a Resident Assistant, maintaining healthy boundaries and balance is essential to your effectiveness and well-being.

#### Types of Boundaries
Be intentional about setting and maintaining boundaries in the following areas:

- **Time boundaries** – Be clear about when you are available in your RA role and when you are off-duty or focusing on your personal time.
- **Content boundaries** – Be mindful of what topics and types of information are appropriate for you to engage with in your role, and what should be referred to professional staff.
- **Emotional boundaries** – Be aware of your emotional limits, how much you can reasonably take on, and when you need to step back or seek support.

#### Understanding Your Role
- Know **what your role as an RA is**: supporting residents, building community, enforcing policies, and connecting residents to resources.
- Know **what your role as an RA is not**: you are not a therapist, a 24/7 on-call problem-solver for every issue, or a replacement for professional staff and services.

Recognizing the difference between what is and is not part of your RA responsibilities helps you maintain appropriate boundaries and protect your own well-being.

#### Know Your Limitations
- **Recognize and know your limitations.** Understand what you can reasonably handle in terms of time, emotional energy, and the types of issues you are equipped to address.
- When a situation is beyond your role or capacity, you should not attempt to manage it alone.

#### Self-Care and Support
- **Your needs and care for self are important to your role.** Taking care of your physical, mental, and emotional health is necessary for you to be effective and present for your residents.
- **Seek support and help from staff.** Use your supervisor and other professional staff as resources when you encounter situations that are complex, emotionally challenging, or outside your scope as an RA.

**Sources:**

### RA Support & Communication Skills
### RA Individual Support: The CLARA Method

When providing individual support to residents, use the CLARA method. This framework helps you respond in a structured, caring, and effective way:

1. **Calming and Centering**  
   Begin by calming and centering yourself so you can be fully present. Take a moment to regulate your own emotions and focus on the resident’s needs.

2. **Listening**  
   Listen attentively to the resident. Allow them to share their experience without interruption. Focus on understanding their perspective and concerns.

3. **Affirming**  
   Affirm the resident’s feelings and experiences. Acknowledge what they are going through and validate that their emotions and reactions are real and important.

4. **Responding**  
   Respond thoughtfully based on what the resident has shared. Address their concerns directly, using the information you gathered while listening and affirming.

5. **Adding**  
   Add any appropriate resources, information, options, or next steps that may help the resident. This can include referrals, campus resources, or suggestions for follow-up actions.

**Sources:**
- `Roommate Agreement & Conflict Resolution Process.pdf`

All Resident Assistants (RAs), both returning and new to the RA role, are expected to serve as student leaders in residence life and to help strengthen our campus community.

Title IX Basics

The information covered in this session about Title IX is not new, but it remains essential. There is always more to learn, and our campus community can always be stronger.

Key points:

### RA Training & Tommie Advantage Curriculum
Residence Life Tommie Advantage 2025–2026  
Lesson Plan Template & Usage Guide

---

### 1. Lesson Plan Header

Each lesson plan begins with a standardized header that clearly identifies the lesson:

- **Residence Life Tommie Advantage 2025 – 2026**
- **Intended Audience:** Indicate whether the lesson is for **FY**, **SY**, or **FY & SY** residential students.
- **Strategy Type:** Indicate which strategy this is (e.g., **Community Meeting, 1:1 Conversation, Roommate Agreement, Community Communication, Conduct Meeting, Event**).
- **Occurrence Number:** Indicate which occurrence of that strategy this lesson plan is (e.g., **#4, #6**).
- **Learning Outcome(s):** Indicate which Tommie Advantage Learning Outcome(s) the lesson addresses (e.g., **1.a, 4.b**), using the format **(LO #.x)**.

The header provides a concise understanding of:
- Who the lesson is for (FY, SY, or FY & SY),
- What type of strategy it is (Community Meeting, 1:1 Conversation, etc.),
- Which numbered occurrence of that strategy it is,
- Which Tommie Advantage Learning Outcome(s) it addresses.

---

### 2. Lesson Plan First Section: Basic Information

Immediately following the header, the first section of the lesson plan spells out the header information with additional detail:

- **Learning Strategy:**  
  - Indicate which strategy this is: **Community Meeting, 1:1 Conversation, Roommate Agreement, Community Communication, Conduct Meeting, Event**.  
  - Insert the **Strategy Description** from the Residence Life Education Plan.

- **Target Audience:**  
  - Indicate **FY, SY, or FY & SY** Residential Students.

- **Date/Time:**  
  - **Begin:** [enter specific date]  
  - **End:** [enter specific date]

- **Due Date:**  
  - [enter specific date]

- **Tommie Advantage Learning Goal(s) & Outcome(s):**  
  Insert the Learning Goal number and paragraph, then the letter and learning outcome from the Tommie Advantage Curriculum document, using the format below. (Tip: you can right‑click on the automatic numbering in Word and change the number. Repeat for each learning goal and outcome intended with the lesson plan.)

  - **[Number]. [Learning Goal]: [Paragraph Description]**  
    *Each student will be able to:*  
    - **[Letter]. [Learning Outcome]**

The Learning Goal(s) and Learning Outcome(s) addressed in the lesson plan must be included in full text from the Tommie Advantage Curriculum document. As Peer‑Educators, RAs are expected to know and understand the “big picture” learning we are hoping for in each lesson plan.

---

### 3. Planning & Preparation

**Planning and Preparation** includes everything that needs to happen before the implementation of the lesson plan. This section lists specific tasks, materials, and timing requirements that must be completed in advance. Examples of tasks include:

- Determining a **time and date** for implementation.
- Rehearsing the **Facilitation Guide**.
- Practicing identifying responses for the **assessment rubric**.
- Preparing or reviewing **digital materials** (e.g., PowerPoint).
- Any other lesson‑specific tasks or materials needed.

Additional guidance:

- Refer to the **“Supervising the Tommie Advantage”** document for **general preparation and supervision tasks** for each different strategy.
- This Planning and Preparation section will be **specifically edited for each individual lesson plan** to reflect unique needs.
- RAs typically receive lesson plans **at least a week prior to the start of implementation** to allow time for necessary preparation.
- RAs should **review everything listed** under Planning and Preparation as soon as they receive the lesson plan.
- If there is anything unclear in this section, RAs should **ask their CD/GSA** for clarification.

---

### 4. Facilitation Guide

The **Facilitation Guide** takes the lesson plan educator through exactly what to say and do. It is the **content portion** of the learning and should always begin on a **new page**.

**Required elements:**

- **Facilitator:**  
  Indicate who will facilitate this lesson plan (e.g., **CD/GSAs, RAs**, etc.).

- **Duration:**  
  Indicate the anticipated length of this lesson plan. **Overestimating is better than underestimating.** Consider how much time is appropriate to accomplish:
  - The **strategy level outcomes**, and
  - Any **additional outcomes**.

- **Outline:**  
  Insert a **detailed, step‑by‑step guide** for the facilitator. The Outline should:
  - Include **notes for the facilitator** to consider (in *italics*).
  - Include sections in **quotations** for anything the facilitator should read or state **verbatim**. These sections are often:
    - The **introduction** (explaining what will be covered),
    - **Transitions** (explaining what is coming up next),
    - The **closing** (reviewing what was covered).

We use verbatim sections to help learners follow along with us and to ensure clarity and consistency.

---

### 5. Required Components of Every Lesson Plan

All lesson plans should include the following within the Facilitation Guide Outline:

1. **Introduction with an outline of the lesson plan**  
   - Clearly state what will be covered and how the session will proceed.

2. **Explicit statement of intended learning**  
   - Share the **purpose** so that students can approach the session from a **learning orientation** rather than a **task orientation**.

3. **Connection to WHY this learning matters**  
   - Explain the relevance and importance of the content to students’ experiences and development.

4. **Appropriate pedagogical tools**  
   - Incorporate tools such as:
     - **Neuroscience of learning and development**,
     - **Active learning**,
     - **Inclusive pedagogy**, etc.

5. **Transitions between topics or sections**  
   - Use clear transitions to facilitate **flow** and help students follow the progression of the lesson.

6. **Differentiation opportunities**  
   - Based on a **pre‑assessment** or a **check‑in on current understanding**, provide **different paths** to deepen the learning for students at different levels of understanding.

7. **Closing**  
   - Review what was covered,
   - Reinforce key takeaways,
   - Connect back to the stated Learning Goal(s) and Outcome(s).

---

### 6. Expectations for RAs as Peer‑Educators

As Peer‑Educators, RAs must:

- **Understand the content** of the lesson plan thoroughly so they are prepared to teach it through facilitating the strategy.
- Be **confident** in their understanding of:
  - The **Tommie Advantage Learning Goal(s) and Outcome(s)** included in the lesson plan,
  - The **purpose** and **flow** of the Facilitation Guide,
  - The **pedagogical tools** being used.
- **Review the Planning and Preparation section promptly** upon receiving the lesson plan (typically at least a week in advance).
- **Consult their CD/GSA** if they do not understand any part of the Planning and Preparation or Facilitation Guide.

This structure ensures that each lesson plan is clear, consistent, and aligned with the Tommie Advantage Curriculum, and that RAs are well‑prepared to implement it effectively.

**Sources:**
- `Understanding a Lesson Plan.pdf`
- `Understanding a Lesson Plan.pdf`

### Resident Support & 1:1 Conversations
**Supporting a Resident Struggling to Find Community and Connection**

**Scenario Overview**  
During your first 1:1 conversation with a resident, they share that they have been struggling to find community and connection since coming to St. Thomas. At first, they were close friends with their roommates, but now they feel they only co-exist in the space. Their roommate frequently has friends over, and the resident feels left out and unsure how to find their own community.

---

### 1. Where to Start

Begin by using this 1:1 as your starting point. Focus on:

- **Listening and validating**  
  - Allow the resident to fully describe their experience of feeling disconnected and left out.  
  - Acknowledge that it is common and understandable to struggle with finding community, especially after an initial “honeymoon” period with roommates.

- **Clarifying their needs and goals**  
  - Ask what “community” and “connection” look like to them (e.g., a few close friends, a larger social circle, involvement in specific activities).  
  - Explore what has changed in their relationship with their roommates and how that shift is affecting them.  
  - Ask how they feel when their roommate has friends over and what, if anything, they would like to be different.

- **Assessing current involvement and interests**  
  - Ask what they are currently involved in on campus, if anything.  
  - Explore their interests (clubs, organizations, intramurals, faith-based groups, cultural orgs, academic groups, hobbies, etc.) that could connect them with others.  

---

### 2. Formal vs. Informal Touch Point

In this situation, an **informal touch point** is typically more applicable as a starting approach, since:

- You are already in a 1:1 conversation where the resident is voluntarily sharing.  
- The primary need is support, connection, and resource-sharing rather than a structured or documented intervention.

A **formal touch point** may become relevant later if:

- The situation escalates into a roommate conflict that requires mediation.  
- The resident’s well-being appears significantly impacted (e.g., signs of distress that may require referral to professional support).  

---

### 3. What the Informal Touch Point Would Look Like

During this informal touch point in the 1:1:

- **Provide space and empathy**  
  - Listen without interrupting.  
  - Reflect back what you hear (“It sounds like you felt close to your roommates at first, and now you feel more like you’re just sharing a space without really connecting.”).  
  - Normalize their experience of transition and change in friendships.

- **Explore options for connection**  
  - Talk through specific campus resources and opportunities at St. Thomas that align with their interests (e.g., student clubs, hall events, campus events, study groups, service opportunities).  
  - Highlight upcoming residence hall programs or community events where they might meet new people.  
  - Encourage them to attend with a concrete plan (e.g., “Let’s pick one event this week you might try.”).

- **Discuss roommate dynamics (if the resident is open to it)**  
  - Ask whether they want to improve their relationship with their roommates or focus on building community elsewhere (or both).  
  - If they want to address the roommate situation, explore:  
    - Whether they feel comfortable talking with their roommate about how they feel.  
    - What they might say and how to say it respectfully.  
  - Emphasize that it is okay to seek community beyond their immediate roommates.

- **Empower the resident**  
  - Help them identify small, manageable steps they can take (e.g., attending one event, introducing themselves to one person on their floor, joining one group).  
  - Reinforce their strengths (e.g., past experiences where they successfully made friends or joined communities).

- **Clarify your ongoing role**  
  - Let them know you are available to check in again and support them as they try new ways to connect.  

---

### 4. Possible Follow-Up Steps

After the initial informal touch point, consider the following follow-up actions:

- **Schedule a follow-up check-in**  
  - Plan to reconnect with the resident after they have had time to try some of the discussed steps (e.g., after a hall event or campus activity).  
  - Use the follow-up to ask what went well, what felt challenging, and what additional support they might need.

- **Monitor their well-being**  
  - Pay attention to any signs that their isolation or feelings of being left out are significantly impacting their mental health or academic success.  
  - If concerns arise, consider discussing appropriate referrals (e.g., counseling services), following all relevant policies and protocols.

- **Encourage ongoing involvement**  
  - Continue to invite them personally to hall programs or community events.  
  - When appropriate, introduce them to other residents with similar interests to help facilitate connections.

- **Revisit roommate dynamics if needed**  
  - If the resident expresses ongoing concern about the roommate situation, explore whether a more structured conversation or mediation is needed.  
  - At that point, consider whether a more formal touch point is appropriate, following established roommate conflict procedures.

By starting with an informal, supportive 1:1 conversation, focusing on listening, validation, and concrete options for involvement, and then following up intentionally, you can help the resident begin to build the sense of community and connection they are seeking at St. Thomas.

**Sources:**
- `Creating Connections & Touch Points 25-26.pptx`

### Responding to Sensitive Disclosures / Title IX Response Guidelines
### What NOT To Say or Do

When someone discloses a sensitive incident (e.g., a potential Title IX concern), you must avoid the following:

- Do **not** say “Tell me more.”  
  - Do not ask additional questions about the incident.  
  - Do not attempt to investigate in any way.

- Do **not** ask questions that imply blame or judgment, such as “Why didn’t you…?”

- Do **not** confront the accused person.

- Do **not** talk to others about what was shared with you, except with those who **need to know** as part of required reporting or response.

- Do **not** promise confidentiality.

- Do **not** attempt to categorize or label someone’s experience.

- Do **not** pressure someone toward any specific course of action, other than informing them about and/or supporting a meeting with the Title IX Coordinator.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Responding to Sexual Misconduct & Bias/Discrimination
### Your Role as an RA in Responding to Sexual Misconduct and Bias/Discrimination

As a Resident Assistant (RA), you have specific responsibilities and limits when responding to disclosures related to sexual misconduct or bias/discrimination. The following guidelines outline what you must do and what you must avoid.

#### What You Must Do

- **Understand the basics of the policies.**  
  Be familiar with institutional policies related to sexual misconduct and bias/discrimination so you can respond appropriately and make required reports.

- **Inform students of your reporting obligations.**  
  Clearly tell students that you are mandated to share anything related to sexual misconduct or bias/discrimination with the appropriate university officials.

- **Respond supportively to disclosures.**  
  When a student shares something with you:
  - Tell them you believe them.  
  - Thank them for sharing this information with you.  
  - Tell them you want to help, and that making a report is a form of helping.

- **Notify appropriate staff immediately.**  
  Connect with your Community Director (CD) or Graduate Staff Assistant (GSA) and with the Title IX Coordinator right away after receiving a disclosure.

- **Ask for help when unsure.**  
  If you are uncertain how to proceed at any point, reach out to your CD, GSA, or the Title IX Coordinator for guidance.

#### What You Must Not Do

- **Do not investigate.**  
  Do not attempt to investigate the situation or gather additional evidence.

- **Do not confront the accused person.**  
  Do not approach, question, or confront the person alleged to have engaged in sexual misconduct or bias/discrimination about what was shared with you.

- **Do not try to resolve the case yourself.**  
  Do not handle cases of sexual misconduct on your own or attempt to create what you believe to be an amicable solution between the parties.

- **Do not ask blaming or “why” questions.**  
  Avoid asking “why” questions or any questions that could imply blame or judgment toward the person who disclosed.

- **Do not share information inappropriately.**  
  Do not talk to others about what was shared with you, except with those who need to know (such as your CD, GSA, and the Title IX Coordinator).

- **Do not feel pressured to memorize everything.**  
  You are not expected to memorize all the details from trainings or presentations. Instead, focus on knowing your role, your reporting obligations, and where to go for help.

**Sources:**
- `RA Training TIX, SVP, BI 2025.pdf`

---

### Roommate Agreements & Conflict Resolution
IDI Group Debrief Session with Beth Zemsky: Roommate Agreements & Conflict Resolution Process

This session focuses on the Roommate Agreements and Conflict Resolution Process, and is part of the broader IDI Group Debrief work with Beth Zemsky. A key emphasis of this session is developing and practicing listening with empathy as a core skill for all residents.

Listening with empathy is one of the skills we want all of our residents to learn. To support this, we incorporate a Community Communication and Community Meeting specifically focused on empathetic listening and related communication practices. These community-based conversations are designed to help residents better understand one another, navigate differences, and engage constructively in conflict resolution.

RA Commitment  
As part of this process, Resident Assistants are asked to reflect on and articulate how they will model these values in their work. Each RA should complete the following prompt as a personal and professional commitment:

“My Commitment: One way I will lead with compassion as an RA is: ______________________.”

This commitment connects the IDI Group Debrief content, the emphasis on empathetic listening, and the practical work of facilitating Roommate Agreements and conflict resolution within the community.

### Staff Wellness & Self-Care
### Self-Care Isn’t Selfish

Self-care is an essential part of maintaining your overall well-being and effectiveness in your role. It is not selfish to prioritize your own needs. Key areas of self-care include:

- **Sleep** – Maintain consistent, adequate rest to support physical and mental health.  
- **Exercise/Movement** – Incorporate regular physical activity or movement into your routine.  
- **Nutrition** – Make intentional, healthy food choices to fuel your body and mind.  
- **Social Time/Fun** – Spend time with friends and engage in enjoyable activities.  
- **Academic** – Attend to your academic responsibilities and manage your workload.  
- **Spiritual Practice** – Participate in spiritual or reflective practices that are meaningful to you.  
- **Alone Time (even for extroverts)** – Set aside time by yourself to recharge, regardless of your personality type.

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

### Student Affairs Foundations
PRINCIPLES OF GOOD PRACTICE FOR STUDENT AFFAIRS  
AT CATHOLIC COLLEGES AND UNIVERSITIES  
THIRD EDITION WITH DIAGNOSTIC QUERIES  

This section outlines the “Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs at Catholic Colleges and Universities,” as presented in the Third Edition with Diagnostic Queries. These principles are intended to guide student affairs work within Catholic higher education settings, ensuring that programs, services, and operations are consistent with the mission and values of Catholic colleges and universities. The accompanying diagnostic queries are used to examine, assess, and strengthen alignment with these principles in day-to-day practice and long-term planning.

**Sources:**
- `ASACCU Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs.pdf`

---

### Student Affairs Philosophy & Assessment Frameworks
5 Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs at Catholic Colleges and Universities  
Guidance for Formative Self‑Assessment and Strategic Planning

The 5 Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs at Catholic colleges and universities provide a normative framework describing good practices that apply across all Catholic institutions. While the principles themselves are common, their implementation will differ by institution, shaped by each campus’s specific history, culture, and distinct charism.

To support formative self‑assessment and strategic planning, each principle is accompanied by five diagnostic queries. These queries are intended to guide reflection, planning, evidence gathering, and decision‑making within Student Affairs at the levels of the individual, the functional area, and the institution.

### The Five Diagnostic Queries

For each principle, use the following five queries:

1. **What does this principle mean for me, my functional area, and my institution?**  
   - This is the foundational question in the assessment and analysis process.  
   - It asks you to interpret and contextualize the principle in light of your role, your department, and your institution’s mission and charism.  
   - Engaging this “What does this mean to us?” question first is essential. There is always a temptation to jump immediately to “what we do,” but actions and initiatives should proceed from a clear, shared understanding of the principle itself.

2. **How do we animate this principle through programs, policies, and practices?**  
   - This question focuses on how the principle is brought to life in concrete ways.  
   - “Animating the principle” includes examining and designing programs, policies, and day‑to‑day practices that embody the principle.  
   - For each individual principle, there is a subset of more specific questions that further unpack how it can and should be animated in practice.

3. **What evidence do we have to judge the effectiveness of our efforts?**  
   - This query addresses how you will know whether your programs, policies, and practices are effectively animating the principle as you understand it.  
   - It facilitates determining whether what you are doing is coherent with what you believe should be done to effectively animate the principle.  
   - Methods that may be employed to answer this query include, but are not limited to:  
     - Needs assessment  
     - Satisfaction surveys  
     - Usage over time  
     - Outcomes assessment  
     - Change of campus culture  
     - Cost effectiveness

4. **What does the evidence tell us about our effectiveness?**  
   - This question requires analysis and interpretation of the data and information gathered.  
   - It moves beyond collection of evidence to understanding what that evidence indicates about how well you are animating the principle.

5. **What will we do with the information we have gathered about our effectiveness?**  
   - This query focuses on action and follow‑through.  
   - It asks how the results of assessment will be used in strategic planning, decision‑making, and improvement efforts.  
   - Together with the fourth query, it is critical for successful strategic planning that includes concrete steps to utilize assessment results.

### Using the Principles and Diagnostic Queries

- The principles function as normative statements of good practice for Student Affairs at all Catholic colleges and universities.  
- Because each institution has a unique culture and charism, implementation of the principles will necessarily vary; the first query (“What does this mean to us?”) is therefore a crucial starting point.  
- The second query (“How do we animate the principle?”) translates understanding into specific programs, policies, and practices, guided by additional, principle‑specific questions.  
- The third query ensures that efforts are assessed using appropriate forms of evidence (e.g., needs assessment, satisfaction surveys, usage over time, outcomes assessment, change of campus culture, cost effectiveness).  
- The fourth and fifth queries (“What does the evidence tell us about our effectiveness?” and “What will we do with the information?”) are essential to closing the assessment loop and integrating findings into ongoing strategic planning.

In combination, the 5 Principles of Good Practice and their associated diagnostic queries provide a structured approach for formative assessment and strategic planning in Student Affairs at Catholic colleges and universities.

**Sources:**
- `ASACCU Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs.pdf`

### Student Interaction & Communication Skills
Describe the Situation

When addressing a resident’s behavior or level of involvement, focus on being objective and stating only observable facts. Avoid assumptions, judgments, or interpretations.

- Be objective.  
- Stick to just the facts.

Example language:
“I noticed that you haven’t been attending any hall events or meetings. We have had a few different kinds; what keeps you from coming, or what would you be interested in?”

(ANA, 2014; Bower & Bower, 2004)

**Sources:**
- `SLEDRA 2025.pdf`

---

### Student Interaction & Support Scenarios
Scenario #2: Resident Frequently Visiting While on Duty – Seeking Involvement and Connection

A resident repeatedly seeks you out while you are on duty and tends to overstay their welcome at the desk or in your room. They share that they were extremely involved in high school sports and really miss the friends they had from a team. They have expressed an interest in getting involved on campus but do not know where to start, and they have asked if you could help them figure out the next step.

Where to start  
- Begin by acknowledging their past involvement in high school sports and their desire for connection and community.  
- Explore what they enjoyed most about being on a team (e.g., competition, camaraderie, structure, leadership, fitness).  
- Ask open-ended questions to understand what types of activities, organizations, or communities might appeal to them now.  
- Identify any barriers they feel to getting involved (e.g., not knowing where to look, feeling shy, time management).

Formal vs. informal touch point  
- Consider whether a formal or informal touch point is more applicable based on the resident’s needs and the nature of the conversation.  
- An informal touch point may be appropriate to begin building rapport and to have a low-pressure conversation about their interests and options.  
- A formal touch point may be appropriate if the resident wants more structured guidance, if the conversation becomes more in-depth, or if you need to document the interaction as part of your role.

What the touch point might look like  
- Informal touch point:  
  - A casual conversation at the desk or in a common area while you are on duty.  
  - Brief, friendly check-ins focused on listening to their experience, validating their feelings about missing their team, and sharing basic information about campus involvement opportunities.  
  - Gentle boundary-setting if they overstay, while still inviting future, more focused conversations about involvement.  

- Formal touch point:  
  - A planned, scheduled meeting in an appropriate space where you can talk without interruption.  
  - A more structured discussion of their interests, including specific questions about what they are looking for in a group or activity.  
  - Reviewing campus resources together (e.g., student organizations, intramural or club sports, fitness classes, leadership programs, or other involvement opportunities).  
  - Helping them identify a short list of organizations or activities to explore and discussing how to approach first meetings or events.

Follow-up steps to consider  
- Encourage the resident to attend an initial meeting, practice, or event for one or more organizations that match their interests.  
- Offer to help them look up schedules, locations, and contact information for relevant groups or offices.  
- If appropriate, suggest they attend involvement fairs, information sessions, or residence hall programs where they can meet other students.  
- Check in with them after they have tried an activity or event to see how it went and whether it met their expectations.  
- Continue to reinforce healthy boundaries around your duty time and personal space while remaining supportive and approachable.  
- If needed, refer them to additional campus resources (such as student activities, recreation, or counseling services) that can further support their transition and involvement.

**Sources:**
- `Creating Connections & Touch Points 25-26.pptx`

### Student Leader Training
**In This Together – Student Affairs Student Leader Training**  
*Rooted in our Catholic values, built for community*

“In This Together” is a Student Affairs student leader training program grounded in our Catholic mission and values. It is designed to help student leaders recognize the dignity of every person and explore how to build a welcoming, respectful, and inclusive campus community. Through reflection and conversation, participants learn what it means to live in community and support one another, emphasizing that we are truly in this together.

The training is organized around five interlocking concepts:

1. **Dignity** – Leading with care and compassion.  
2. **Inclusion** – Creating welcoming communities.  
3. **Dialogue** – Engaging across differences.  
4. **Justice** – Advocating for the common good.  
5. **Service** – Leading through humility.

“In This Together” leans into the *Principles of Good Practice for Student Affairs at Catholic Colleges and Universities*, with particular emphasis on:

- **Principle 1: Inclusive Community**  
- **Principle 4: Dignity of All People**  
- **Principle 7: Dialogue**

### Student Staff Performance & Accountability
**BEHAVIOR / PERFORMANCE CONCERNS AND ACCOUNTABILITY MEASURES**

**Behavior/Performance Concerns:**

1. **GPA Requirements:**
   - Failing to meet a term GPA of 2.5.
   - Failing to meet a cumulative GPA of 2.5.
   - Two or more consecutive terms of not meeting a term GPA of 2.5.
   - Two or more consecutive terms of not meeting a cumulative GPA of 2.5.

2. **Behavior Patterns:**
   - Recurring patterns or multiple instances within a single incident of Level 1 behaviors.
   - Recurring patterns or multiple instances within a single incident of Level 2 behaviors.

3. **Code of Conduct Violations:**
   - Violations resulting in conduct status up to and including a Formal Warning.
   - Violations resulting in conduct status up to and including Conduct Probation.

**Accountability Measures:**

1. **For Initial Concerns:**
   - Issuance of a warning in the student leader position.
   - Implementation of a Written Performance Plan.
   - Ongoing Coaching at Level 2.

2. **For Continued or Severe Concerns:**
   - Dismissal from the student leader position.
   - Rescinding of future position offers.
   - Placement on probation in the student leader position.
   - Implementation of a Written Performance Plan.
   - Ongoing Coaching at Level 3.

These measures aim to address and rectify behavior and performance issues while providing structured support and accountability.

**Sources:**
- `RA Assessment & Learning Process + RA Accountability.pdf`
- `RA Assessment & Learning Process + RA Accountability.pdf`

### Student Staff Role & Expectations
Things to Remember in Your Role

As a student staff member, you now serve as an authority figure in the residence community. Students look up to you, listen to you, and often take your advice seriously. Keep the following in mind as you interact with them:

- Recognize that you are an authority figure now.
- Understand that students look up to you and pay attention to what you say.
- Remember that students listen and often take your advice.
- Do not solve their problems for them; instead, support them as they work through their own challenges.
- Show that you care about them and that you want what is best for them.
- Ask questions and stay curious about their experiences and perspectives.
- Avoid making assumptions about their situations or needs.

### Supporting International Students
Take intentional time to connect with international students and learn about their specific challenges and questions. These conversations can open the door for students to share what is on their minds, such as where to buy good winter jackets or good places to eat around St. Paul.

One effective approach is to actively share information about opportunities and explain why they might be a good fit for the student. For example, an international student shared:

“My RA was also an international student. She was always telling me about opportunities and why they would be good for me. She's talked to me about programs like Aquinas Scholars and jobs like being a Resident Advisor. It always felt like she wanted the best for me. I loved that she took the time to do that as it made me feel cared for.”

In practice, this means:

- Proactively initiating conversations with international students.
- Asking about and listening to their concerns, needs, and interests.
- Offering concrete suggestions, such as campus programs (e.g., Aquinas Scholars) and roles (e.g., Resident Advisor positions).
- Explaining how these opportunities could benefit them personally and academically.
- Demonstrating genuine care by consistently following up and making time for them.

By doing these things, you help international students feel supported, informed, and cared for in their residential community.

**Sources:**
- `Res Life Training 2025.pdf`

---

### Common Experiences of International Students

International students may encounter a range of challenges and adjustments, including:

- **Language barriers (verbal and nonverbal)**  
  Difficulties understanding or using spoken and written language, as well as interpreting or expressing nonverbal communication.

- **Homesickness and lifestyle change**  
  Missing family, friends, and familiar surroundings, while also adapting to new daily routines, customs, and ways of living.

- **Academic expectations**  
  Adjusting to different teaching styles, classroom norms, grading systems, and workload expectations.

- **A new identity**  
  Navigating changes in self-perception and how others perceive them, including cultural identity and role changes in a new environment.

- **Small or no social network**  
  Arriving with few or no existing connections and needing to build new friendships and support systems from the ground up.

**Sources:**
- `Res Life Training 2025.pdf`

---

### Supporting Residents / International Students
Supporting International Students on Your Floor

As a staff member, you play a key role in helping international students feel welcome and included in the residence community. You can support them in the following ways:

- Make an intentional effort to get to know international students on your floor.
- Make an intentional effort to help them feel included in floor life and community.
- Consider international students when planning floor events, and design activities that are accessible and welcoming to them.
- When possible, help them understand U.S. customs and culture, and be willing to answer questions or explain unfamiliar practices.
- Be a friend: show interest, offer support, and be approachable.
- Be understanding: recognize that they may be adjusting to a new country, culture, and campus environment, and respond with patience and empathy.

**Sources:**
- `Res Life Training 2025.pdf`

---

### Supporting Specific Student Populations
International Students at St. Thomas

This section provides an overview of international students at St. Thomas and outlines how staff can support them. It addresses four key questions:

1. **Who are the international students of St. Thomas?**  
   International students at St. Thomas are individuals who come from countries outside the United States to pursue their education at the university. They may be undergraduate or graduate students, degree-seeking or exchange students, and they bring a wide range of cultural, linguistic, and educational backgrounds to campus.

2. **Why do international students come to St. Thomas?**  
   International students choose St. Thomas for a variety of reasons, which can include the academic programs offered, the reputation of the institution, personal or family connections, opportunities for cultural exchange, and the overall campus environment and community.

3. **How do international students come to St. Thomas?**  
   International students arrive at St. Thomas through established admission and enrollment processes. This typically involves applying to the university, meeting academic and language requirements, obtaining the necessary immigration documentation, and completing any required pre-arrival steps before beginning their studies on campus.

4. **How can you help international students?**  
   Staff and community members can support international students by being aware of their unique experiences and needs, offering clear and respectful communication, connecting them with appropriate campus resources, and fostering an inclusive and welcoming environment that helps them succeed academically and socially.

**Sources:**
- `Res Life Training 2025.pdf`

### Tommie Advantage Assessment & Supervision Guidelines
Residence Life Tommie Advantage 2025–2026  
Assessment & Reporting and Supervision Expectations

This section outlines how to design and document assessment and supervision for each Tommie Advantage lesson plan for 2025–2026 (FY, SY, or FY & SY; all [STRATEGY] #[OCCURANCE] Lesson Plans, including specific learning outcomes [LO #.x]).

---

### 1. Assessment & Reporting

Assessment helps determine whether the intended student learning has occurred. As in a classroom, we use multiple ways to measure learning, with the strongest evidence coming from residents directly demonstrating what they have learned. We also gather information from RAs and CD/GSAs to inform future planning.

Almost all lesson plans will include feedback components. Some lesson plans will also include direct assessment of student learning. Assessment strategies may include:
- Direct assessment of student learning  
- Indirect assessment of student learning  
- RA feedback  
- CD/GSA feedback  

This section of the lesson plan will also include:
- Due dates for all assessment-related tasks  
- Links to all tools and forms needed to complete assessments  

#### 1.1. Planning and Designing an Assessment Strategy

When planning the assessment strategy for a lesson plan, consider and document:

- What is the purpose of the assessment?  
- With whom will the assessment results be shared?  
- What is acceptable evidence of the student’s mastery of the intended learning?  
- How will students provide this evidence?  
- Is the assessment language reflective of the strategy-level outcomes?

#### 1.2. Required Details for Each Assessment Strategy

For each assessment strategy used in a lesson plan, include all of the following:

- **Type of assessment**  
  (e.g., direct assessment, indirect assessment, feedback form, etc.)

- **Who is responsible for completing it**  
  (e.g., facilitator, participant, supervisor, etc.)

- **Rationale for assessment**  
  Explain why this learning is being assessed, in this way, at this time.

- **Timeframe for assessment**  
  Specify when the assessment occurs (e.g., pre & post, during, etc.).

- **Deadline for assessment completion**  
  Include the exact date and time by which the assessment must be completed.

- **Rubric**  
  Pull the appropriate rubric from the Rubrics document, including the “No evidence,” “Beginning,” “Middle,” and “Advanced” language.

- **Tool(s) for completing the assessment**  
  Provide links and details for the tools used (e.g., Microsoft Forms, Roompact, etc.).

Rubrics are used to determine the degree to which learning has occurred, using the defined performance levels (No evidence, Beginning, Middle, Advanced).

---

### 2. Supervision Expectations

Supervision expectations describe everything supervisors (CD/GSAs) must do before, during, and after the implementation of each lesson plan. If RAs are the facilitators, this section must be completed.

Use the “Supervising the Tommie Advantage” document for general preparation and supervision tasks for each strategy. This lesson-plan-specific section should then be tailored to the particular lesson plan and must include:

- **Before implementation**
  - How supervisors (CD/GSAs) will deliver the lesson plan information to their RA team.  
  - Any preparation tasks supervisors must complete to support RAs.  
  - Deadlines for these preparation tasks.

- **During implementation**
  - How supervisors will track expectations and progress while the lesson plan is being implemented.  
  - Any specific follow-up or check-ins required with RAs during implementation.

- **After implementation**
  - Accountability measures and expectations for RAs once the lesson plan has been completed.  
  - Any required documentation, debrief, or feedback from RAs and CD/GSAs.  
  - Timelines and deadlines for post-implementation tasks.

Supervisors must:
- Set clear deadlines for all supervision-related tasks (before, during, and after implementation).  
- Communicate these deadlines to the Associate Director so they can be added to Live In Team Meeting agendas.

RAs should review the Supervision Expectations section of each lesson plan to understand what follow-up and accountability to expect from their CD/GSA after implementation.

**Sources:**
- `Understanding a Lesson Plan.pdf`

---

### Tommie Advantage Lesson Plans
**Residence Life Tommie Advantage 2025 – 2026 Strategy Lesson Plan**

**Overview:**  
This lesson plan is designed to achieve specific learning outcomes for First-Year (FY) and Sophomore-Year (SY) residents as part of the Residence Life Tommie Advantage program for the 2025-2026 academic year. The strategy aims to equip residents with skills and knowledge corresponding to the beginning, middle, or advanced stages of the learning outcomes, as outlined in the Tommie Advantage Rubrics document.

**Strategy Level Outcomes:**  
The primary focus of this lesson plan is to ensure that residents can achieve the designated learning outcomes. These outcomes are articulated as demonstratable actions, allowing for measurable assessment. For example, residents might be expected to "Outline the three requirements for an effective learning outcome" or perform other specific tasks to a defined degree.

**Additional Intended Outcomes:**  
Beyond the core Tommie Advantage outcomes, this strategy also aims to achieve supplementary goals. These might include helping residents connect personally with their Resident Assistant (RA) or familiarizing them with upcoming housing processes. These additional outcomes are also expressed as demonstratable actions to facilitate assessment.

**Rationale:**  
The rationale section provides context and justification for the strategy, helping RAs understand the importance of the lesson plan. This may involve connections to previous or future strategies, alignment with the time of year, addressing student needs, or responding to behavioral trends. The rationale is crucial for fostering RA engagement and ensuring they can effectively communicate the benefits of the learning experience to residents.

By the conclusion of this lesson plan, residents should be able to demonstrate the skills and knowledge imparted, based on the structured and measurable outcomes outlined.

**Sources:**
- `Understanding a Lesson Plan.pdf`

### Tommie Advantage Overview Session
**Tommie Advantage Overview Session**

The Tommie Advantage Overview session provides a comprehensive introduction to the program. Participants can access the session materials through various resources:

1. **Slide Deck**: The presentation is available in the Tommie Advantage Overview Slide Deck, which can be accessed and downloaded [here](https://stthomas.instructure.com/courses/84343/files/10631372?wrap=1).

2. **Recording**: A recording of the session, lasting 18 minutes and 24 seconds, is available for review.

3. **Additional Resources**:
   - **Tommie Advantage SLED Worksheet**: This worksheet is designed to supplement the session and can be accessed and downloaded [here](https://stthomas.instructure.com/courses/84343/files/10631375?wrap=1).
   - **Understanding a Lesson Plan**: This document provides insights into lesson planning and is available for access and download [here](https://stthomas.instructure.com/courses/84343/files/10631376?wrap=1).
   - **Residence Life Tommie Advantage Dates 2025-2026 - RA Version**: This document outlines important dates for residence life and is accessible [here](https://stthomas.instructure.com/courses/84343/files/10631374?wrap=1).

These resources are designed to enhance understanding and facilitate the effective implementation of the Tommie Advantage program.

### Tommie Advantage Program Schedule
**Residence Life Tommie Advantage Schedule – Fall 2025**

The Residence Life Tommie Advantage program for Fall 2025 is designed to foster a sense of community, enhance communication, and support students in their transition to campus life. Below is the detailed schedule of activities and meetings for both First-Year (FY) and Second-Year (SY) students:

- **August 8-10**: "Belonging in our Community" (Room Agreement Part 1) focuses on establishing a sense of belonging. Learning Outcomes (LOs): 1.a, 3.b.
- **August 15-17**: "Connection to New Community" aims to help students connect with their new environment. LO: 2.a.
- **August 19-20**: "Introduce Important Policies for Living In Community" covers essential policies. LO: 2.d.
- **August 20-September 14**: "Introducing Tommie Advantage" and "Move In & Policy Reminders, Getting Connected" provide an overview of the program and reminders about move-in procedures. LOs: 2.a, 2.d.
- **August 20-January 29**: "Campus Resources" offers ongoing information about available resources. LO: 4.b.
- **August 25**: FY students express excitement for move-in. LO: 2.a.
- **August 27**: FY Community Meeting 1 discusses connections, important information, and instructions for Room Agreement. LOs: 2.a, 3.b.
- **August 28-September 1**: FY Roommate Agreement 2 focuses on connecting and setting boundaries. LOs: 2.a, 3.b.
- **August 28**: SY students express excitement for move-in. LO: 2.a.
- **September 2**: SY Community Meeting 1 covers connections, important information, Room Agreement instructions, and community standards. LOs: 2.a, 2.d, 3.b.
- **September 3-7**: SY Roommate Agreement 2 emphasizes connecting and setting boundaries. LOs: 2.a, 3.b.
- **September 3-21**: FY & SY Touch Base Meeting 1 includes a brief roommate check-in and connection with Resident Assistants (RAs). LO: 2.a.
- **September 15-October 13**: "Strategies for Academic Success" and "Communicating in Roommate Conflicts" provide academic support and conflict resolution strategies. LOs: 4.b, 3.b.
- **September 22-26**: FY & SY Community Meeting 2 discusses accountability within the community. LOs: 2.d, 3.b.
- **September 24**: FY & SY Roommate Agreement 4 involves a roommate relationship check-in. LO: 2.a.
- **September 29-November 9**: FY & SY 1:1 Conversations focus on connection, Room Agreement Part 3, and communication. LO: 2.a.
- **October 14-19**: FY & SY Roommate Agreement 6 updates the Roompact Agreement. LOs: 2.a, 3.b.
- **October 14-November 2**: "Managing Conflict" and "Engaging in Divisive Conversations" address conflict management and communication. LOs: 2.c, 3.b.
- **November 3-30**: "Reflecting on Core Values" and "Strategies to Communicate Across Difference" encourage reflection and effective communication. LOs: 1.a, 3.b.
- **November 10-14**: FY & SY Community Meeting 3 focuses on connection and break checks. LO: 2.a.
- **December 1-January 1**: "Transition Reflection, Break Info" and "World of Celebrations" provide transition support and celebrate diversity. LOs: 5.a, 1.b, 3.b.
- **TBD**: FY & SY Event 1, "World of Celebrations," continues the theme of celebrating diversity. LOs: 1.b, 3.b.

This structured program aims to support students in building a strong community, developing effective communication skills, and successfully navigating their college experience.

**Sources:**
- `Tommie Advantage Overview.pdf`
- `Tommie Advantage Overview.pdf`

### Tommie Advantage Programming Schedule
Residence Life Tommie Advantage Schedule  
Academic Year 2025–2026  

This schedule outlines all required Tommie Advantage (TA) communications, bulletin boards, meetings, roommate agreements, 1:1s, touch base meetings, and events for First-Year (FY) and Sophomore/Upper-Year (SY) residents. All facts, dates, learning outcomes (LOs), and strategies must be followed as listed.

Key:
- FY / SY: Audience (First-Year, Sophomore/Upper-Year, or both)
- Strategy: Communication / Engagement method (Comm. Comm., Comm. Mtg., Roommate Agreement, 1:1 Conversation, Touch Base Mtg., Event, Resource Board, TA Bulletin Board, EOD Bulletin Board)
- LO(s): Learning Outcomes referenced (e.g., 1.a, 2.a, 3.b, etc.)
- “ASS. DUE”: Assignment due date
- “RA FB DUE”: RA feedback due date
- “NA”: Not applicable
- CD/GSA: Community Director/Graduate Student Assistant
- EOD: End of Day
- SYE: Second-Year Experience
- UD: Upper-Division
- CSA: Center for Student Achievement (or equivalent partner)
- CWB: Center for Well-Being
- OSI: Office of Sustainability Initiatives
- OCSL: Office of Community Service and Learning
- WoC: World of Celebrations

### Touch Base Meetings Overview
Touch Base Meetings – Overview and Purpose

This section provides an overview of Touch Base Meetings, explains how they connect to our “WHY,” outlines expectations for completing them, and concludes with a brief wrap-up and space for questions.

1. What is a Touch Base Meeting  
A Touch Base Meeting is a scheduled check-in designed to maintain regular communication, alignment, and support between staff members and their supervisors or team leads. These meetings ensure ongoing connection, timely feedback, and clarity around responsibilities and expectations.

2. Connecting to Our WHY  
Touch Base Meetings are directly tied to our core purpose (“WHY”) as a residence life organization:  
- They reinforce our commitment to consistent, intentional support for staff and residents.  
- They help ensure that our daily work remains aligned with our mission, values, and long-term goals.  
- They provide a structured opportunity to reflect on how current tasks and initiatives contribute to our broader purpose.

3. Completing Touch Base Meetings  
- Touch Base Meetings should be scheduled and completed regularly, according to the timelines and expectations set by your supervisor or department.  
- Each meeting should include time to review current responsibilities, discuss progress on ongoing projects, and identify any challenges or support needs.  
- Staff and supervisors are expected to come prepared with relevant updates, questions, and follow-ups from previous meetings.  
- Documentation or brief notes from each Touch Base Meeting should be completed as directed by your department to ensure continuity and accountability.

4. Wrap Up & Questions  
At the end of each Touch Base Meeting:  
- Summarize key discussion points, decisions, and action items.  
- Clarify next steps, responsible parties, and any deadlines.  
- Allow time for questions to confirm understanding and address any remaining concerns.  

This overview is intended to guide the consistent use of Touch Base Meetings as a tool for communication, alignment, and connection to our organizational “WHY.”

**Sources:**
- `Touch Base Meetings.pdf`

### Working with Specific Student Populations
### International Students

International students are residents who come from many different regions around the world. Their backgrounds and experiences in the U.S. can vary widely:

- Some may need extra support at times.
- Some may have already been living in the U.S. for quite some time.
- Some may already be well adjusted to life in the U.S.

Staff should be aware that international students are not a uniform group; their levels of adjustment and support needs can differ significantly.

**Sources:**
- `Res Life Training 2025.pdf`
